# The 40 Hour Cabin: 3 Trees

*One small chainsaw, three trees, forty hours or less: a geodesic wedge cabin, winched up between the trees, skinned and floored in any order*

Exported 2026-10-03. Every figure in this file was computed at export time from the geometry in this repository.

---

# Cover

![The cover of The 40 Hour Cabin.](../../deliverables/book/figures/book-cover.png)

---

# Title pages

## Title page

The 40 Hour Cabin: 3 Trees

*One small chainsaw, three trees, forty hours or less: a geodesic wedge
cabin, winched up between the trees, skinned and floored in any order.*

A builder's manual: the geometry, fabrication, construction and variations
of a hubless timber geodesic system, documented from one standing
prototype — raised between two trees on three cables, because that is how
this one was actually built.

By the builder. Built with the tools included with this book, which are
named in the back.

![The frame, day fourteen.](../../../deliverables/book/figures/front-frame.png)

That is the whole claim in one picture: a frame of 120 members,
cut from 3 trees, standing on day fourteen of the fortnight this
book teaches — and the fortnight's hands-on total is the title's other
claim, counted below.

## The forty hours

The title is an arithmetic, not a slogan. The clock is the one the
companion wedge book prints, and every rate in it is a measured session
figure rather than a process estimate:

**At the log.** The frame's 120 members, split at the
measured 30 wedges per 4-hour
session: **16 hours** of splitting.

**From wedge to standing.** Each member erected at
15 minutes: **30 straight hours**
of assembly.

**With the error allowance.** A 25 per cent allowance on
the erection, because a first build always pays its tuition:
**37.5 hours** — inside the 40-hour week the title
is named after, with 2.5 hours to spare.

The honest reading, stated once and used everywhere after: the forty
hours is the *building* — the frame, from split wedges to standing, in
forty hours or less. The harvest that produces the wedges is a further
16 hours at the log, and the skin, the floor and the
ground are added later, in any order, which is the point of the next
page and of the whole book.

## The part you are allowed to get wrong

Your skull is not symmetrical either, and it protects the brain just
fine. This book builds on that sentence.

A frame's job is to be a *frame*: forty triangles whose lengths set
their angles, standing because the shape cannot fall without stretching
a stick. It does not have to be pretty, milled, graded or symmetrical —
the lumpy skull protects the brain, and the lumpy frame holds the
cabin. The parts that must be right are the two that touch the
weather and the earth: the **skin**, and the **ground**. Those are the
chapters this book treats with the most care, and they are the parts
that can be added, replaced or removed in any order — the frame is
hot-swappable by design, because every panel, every layer, every floor
and every pad is a separate object that bolts on and off.

The build order this book actually used is the order its title page
promises: frame first, winched between the trees, then whatever the
winter asks for — skin before floor, floor before skin, the ground
last of all. The book is written so that any order works.

## Copyright, and the engineer's note

Copyright © 2026, the author.

This book documents construction methods, geometric principles, prototype
builds and design possibilities. It is not a substitute for site-specific
structural engineering, and nothing in it is offered as code-compliant
design. Codes, loads, materials, soil conditions and permitting
requirements vary by place and by site. A structure intended for permanent
occupancy -- and anything carrying snow, wind or people you are not
prepared to lose -- should be reviewed by a qualified engineer and the
local authority before it is built. The floating rig of the closing
chapters is a design possibility, priced but not rated: the trees, the
cables, the mast and the hoist all carry real loads, and none of those
ratings is in this book.

Three kinds of claim run through this book, and each is labelled so they
cannot be mistaken for one another:

**Known geometry.** A fact of the solved frame, true at every size: a 2V
dome of this configuration has this many members, at these ratios. These
are calculations, and the book shows the calculation behind each one.

**Tested construction.** Something that happened on the standing prototype
this book is written around: the joint that was used, the fortnight that
was clocked, the winch raise that put the cabin between the trees, the
mistake that was made and corrected. These are reports, not promises.

**Design possibilities.** Things that are priced or drawn but not yet
built: the rotating pad, the iris, the network. Each is marked as such
where it appears, and the closing chapter restates every claim in the book
as the condition it rests on.

A method whose three kinds of claim are kept apart is a method a reader
can check. That separation is the point of this book, and the rest of it
is the working out.

---

# What this book is

3 trees, one chainsaw off a hardware-store shelf, one hundred
and twenty structural members, and a geodesic dome standing in fourteen
days — 37.5 hours of hands-on work, inside the forty-hour
week the title is named after.

That is the whole claim, and everything in this book exists to either explain
it, prove it, or teach you to do it.

## It is a real build

The dome in the photographs was not rendered, proposed, or costed on a
spreadsheet. It was cut, and the process of cutting it produced a number of
surprises, most of which are in here. Several chapters exist specifically to
report a figure that does not flatter the method — Chapter 13 revises the
recovery claim downward, Chapter 45 halves an hourly rate this project
publicised, and the errata pages list everything else that has had to be
taken back. A book about a new building method that contains no corrections is a
brochure.

## It is three books at once

The reader I had in mind wanted three different things, and they read at
three different speeds, so rather than blend them into a mush each chapter
declares which one it is.

**The story** is what happened, in order, to somebody with a saw: the
frankendome that came first, the afternoon the wedge was found, and the week
lost to a problem that turned out not to exist. Seven chapters, and you can
read them straight through as a narrative.

**The manual** is do this, then this. Eighteen chapters, and if you follow
only those you get a dome. It is a complete set of instructions on its own.

**The explanation** is why any of it works — why a triangulated frame will
accept a stick no mill would sell, why splitting a round log is free and
squaring it is not, and why this method does not transfer to a rectangular
house. Fifteen chapters, all skippable, all there because a method you do not
understand is a method you cannot adapt.

The rest is reference: the tables you come back to at the saw rather than
read in an armchair.

![Three ways through this book.](../../../deliverables/book/figures/strand-map.png)

## Every number here is computed

This is the unusual part, and it is worth a paragraph.

Not one figure in this book was typed into a sentence. Every dimension, count,
angle, percentage and price is a token in the manuscript that gets filled in,
at the moment the book is made, by the same software that draws the
three-dimensional models. When it says the dome is 21.6 feet
across, that number came out of a solver about a second before it reached
this page.

The reason is not showing off. It is that a number typed into a paragraph is
correct on the day it is typed and silently wrong from the first time anybody
changes anything — and there is no way to find those. Nothing fails. The book
just quietly becomes untrue in places nobody remembers.

So the arithmetic in this book is a test suite. It runs, it checks itself
against the geometry, and where two ways of computing something disagree the
book prints both and says which is which. Chapter 23 is nothing but a proof
that the two methods in Part IV are the same calculation, and it prints the
residual — 0.0e+00 inches — rather than asking you to take
it on trust.

Every tool that produced these numbers is named in the back, and every one of
them is included with this book. You can change the tree and rebuild the
whole thing around your own.

## What it is not

It is not an engineer's stamp. Nothing here has been checked by a licensed
structural engineer, and where a figure depends on species, grade or moisture
this book says so and tells you who to ask instead of guessing.

It is not a code compliance document. Your jurisdiction has opinions about
round buildings and they are not in here.

And it is not a promise that you will do this in a fortnight. I did, on the
second attempt, having already made every mistake the errata pages
record.
The forty hours is the honest claim — the frame, split wedges to standing —
and the calendar around it is yours: a fortnight of part days, or a long
weekend of full ones, with the skin, the floor and the ground added later,
in any order.

---

# Part 1: How to Build One

> Your skull is not symmetrical either, and it protects the brain just fine. The frame is the part you are allowed to get wrong. The skin and the ground are not.

*The method, in the order it is actually done: what a dome is, why the shape carries load, how three trees become forty panels, and the forty hours that raise those panels into a cabin winched between the trees -- with the skin, the floor and the ground added later, in any order.*

---

# 1. The Tree That Was Already Down

*A windfall pine, a small saw, and a question nobody had asked out loud*

## The Tree That Was Already Down

This book starts with one specific tree, because every number after this page
descends from it, and because the story does not make sense if the tree stays
abstract.

A pine came down in a storm on land I could walk to. Not a tree anybody
planted for the purpose. Not a timber sale. A windfall: sixty or so feet of
pine that had stood in a line of others, gone over in one night, root plate
up, crown in the brush, and the trunk lying where it fell with the ground
still pressed into its bark.

The useful part of a windfall like that is the straight part between the
root flare and the first big limb. On this tree that ran 48
feet: 12 inches across at the butt,
8 at the top. The flare below it and the crown above
it were firewood. In the middle was a cylinder of pine the length of a
shipping container, and there was no way to use it.

That sentence is the whole book in miniature. The wood was not missing,
rotten, or in some faraway forest. It was on the ground, within carrying
distance of where the building now stands. What was missing was every
machine, service, and middleman that stands between a fallen trunk and a
usable stick of wood — and it is those, not the tree, that decide what a
piece of wood is worth. Chapter 2 walks that chain one step at
a time. This chapter is about the tree at the near end of it, before anybody
got paid.

## Sixty feet of pine, and no way to use it

Here is the problem stated plainly, because most of the ways it gets solved
are ways of moving the problem somewhere more expensive.

A standing tree is worth almost nothing until somebody converts it. A
windfall is worth even less: it cannot wait for a forester, it will not be
part of any sale, and it will rot where it lies. Convert it the normal way
and the trunk has to travel — dragged to a landing, loaded, trucked to a
mill, and then hauled again as boards to wherever the building is. Every one
of those journeys is paid by the mile and the hour, and a mill will not
touch a two-tree job without a setup charge that makes the exercise
pointless. The honest summary of the conventional route is that the tree
was worth less than the diesel to move it, which is why the tree stayed
where it fell.

The saw that changed that was a 38.2-cc chainsaw with a
16-inch bar that cost 199 dollars on a shelf. The
whole shelf price of the machine that eventually turned this tree into a
building was less than what hauling the trunk to somebody else's mill would
have cost before a single board was cut — not an exaggeration, a comparison,
and it is the comparison the method lives on. The industrial route charges
for handling, and handling is most of the route. A saw you already own, on
the site where the wood already lies, removes the route and charges only
fuel, a chain, and time.

But on the day I am describing, none of that was obvious. What was obvious
was a 48-foot trunk that no mill wanted, cut from a
tree no buyer would bother with, on ground nobody was being paid to stand
on. The saw sat on its shelf because a saw alone does not make the trunk
usable. A saw makes cuts. What I did not yet know was which cut made the
tree stop being a problem and start being a frame.

Measured properly, the trunk held 318 board feet of solid
wood. That figure is not a boast; it is what the tape said, and the way the
book counts it is explained in full in Chapter 13. The same
trunk, sawn into two-by-fours the way a mill would, keeps about
45 per cent of itself as saleable lumber. Split the
way this book will split it, it keeps 87.4 per cent —
1.93 times as much — without a second machine ever
touching it. All of that is later. Here it only matters as the reason the
question in the next paragraph was worth asking, because the difference
between 45 and 87.4 per cent of a
318-board-foot trunk is the difference between a load of
firewood and a building.

![The book's tree: 12 inches at the butt, 8 at the top, 48 feet of usable trunk.](../../deliverables/book/figures/tree-measured.png)

## The tree, measured

The drawing matters more than it looks. Every chapter that follows — the
recovery arithmetic, the two sizing methods, the cut list, the fortnight —
takes its numbers from this same trunk, and if the book ever disagrees with
itself about the tree, the disagreement is a bug, not an editorial position.
So the tree gets its own page, drawn to scale, with the three measurements
the whole method hangs on.

**The butt.** 12 inches. The widest point that still
counts as trunk; below it is the flare, which splits badly and gets bucked
off.

**The top.** 8 inches, where the straight stem runs
out at the first limb. Above it is crown, which is firewood.

**The usable length.** 48 feet between the two. A
trunk tapers from 12 to 8 inches
along that run, and everything about how the log gets cut is decided by
that taper: buck it into 6-foot sections and each
section is close enough to a cylinder that one jig fits them all.

Those are the only three numbers a tree-first build needs from the tree.
Not species, not grade, not a timber buyer's walkaround. Butt, top, length.
Chapter 22 turns exactly these three into a dome diameter, and
the round trip in Chapter 23 proves it is the same
calculation a designer runs from the other direction. The drawing here is
not decoration. It is the input.

## What I actually wanted

It is worth saying what the tree was *for*, because the method is only
interesting against the thing it was asked to build.

I wanted a round building, big enough to live in, built alone.

Round, not square: a dome, 21.6 feet across with
365 square feet of floor when the arithmetic was done — a
serious room rather than a shed, and the largest size these trees
would fill. Chapter 22 shows that 3 trees are what
it takes, and that the frame only actually consumes
1.88 of them; the rest of the pile is
spares, blocking, floor stock and firewood, and the book says so rather than hiding it.

Built alone, because that is the constraint that shaped every decision after
it. A crew can lift a sixteen-foot stick; one person cannot. So the members
are 6 feet, the panels are lifted one at a time,
and the whole frame is designed around what a single person can carry, set,
and fasten without a second pair of hands. The book never pretends this is
the fastest way to build. It is the way that works when the only labour
budget is your own, and the fortnight in the middle of this part is what
that constraint actually costs.

And from what was on the ground, because the economics only work that way.
Buy the timber and the frame costs what timber costs; Chapter 45
does that arithmetic both ways and prints the number that does not help the
argument. Here it is enough to say the goal was never to build cheaply in
money alone. It was to build with the tree that was already down, one saw,
and two weeks — and to find out what that made possible that the lumberyard
price could not.

That is the book. The rest of it is the tree answering.

---

# 2. Fifteen Middlemen

*Why a two-by-four costs what it costs, and how much of that is wood*

## Fifteen Middlemen

Between a standing tree and a board on a rack there are, depending on how you
slice the chain, something like fifteen separate businesses, machines, and
handoffs — and every one of them gets paid. The board does not cost what the
tree costs. The board costs what the tree costs *plus fifteen wages, fifteen
fuel bills, fifteen margins and fifteen sets of handling*, and almost none of
that is wood.

This chapter walks the chain, because the whole wedge method is a way of
skipping it, and you cannot skip something intelligently without first
knowing what it is.

The claim to hold in your head while reading it: a two-by-four's price is
mostly distance and patience. The tree spent a lifetime standing still; the
board has to be moved, processed, dried, graded, and waited on, and each of
those is a service somebody sells. The method in this book removes fourteen
of the fifteen steps — the diagram on the next page names them — and keeps
exactly one. That is not a metaphor. It is the count, and the rest of the
book is the consequence of it.

## From stump to rack

Walk it once, end to end, and count the hands.

**Fell.** Somebody drops the tree. Trained, insured, standing in the weather.

**Skid.** The trunk is dragged to a landing where a truck can reach it.

**Haul.** A log truck moves it to the mill, paid by the mile and the hour,
and the round trip is empty one way.

**Scale.** Somebody measures and grades the log, because the mill buys by
volume and quality, and the measurement is a negotiation.

**Saw.** The mill breaks it down: slabs off the round, boards out of the
cants. This is the step people picture, and it is one step of
14.

**Edge.** Every board gets its wany edges ripped off square.

**Trim.** Ends squared to length.

**Dry.** The boards are stickered and air-dried, or kilned — weeks to months
of time, and the kiln burns fuel.

**Plane.** The rough board is dressed to its finished size. A nominal two-by-
four has now become a dressed one-and-a-half by three-and-a-half, and the
missing wood is not an accident; it is this step.

**Grade.** Somebody looks at every board and assigns it a grade, because
graded lumber can be sold by phone and ungraded cannot.

**Bundle, ship, stock.** It is unitised, moved again, and shelved in a yard
that rents its ground, its racks and its fork trucks.

**Sell.** A desk takes an order and a margin.

That is the honest list — 14 operations between the tree
and the graded board, and every one of them has fuel, wages, rent and a
margin in it. None of them is a scam. Each one exists because the customer
for a board is a contractor forty miles from the forest who needs a
straight, dry, graded, predictable rectangle *tomorrow*, and the chain
delivers exactly that. The chain is good at its job. Its job is just mostly
not wood.

Now count the method this book uses: fell, buck, split, jig, raise —
8 operations from standing tree to member standing in a
frame. The middle of that list — skid, haul, scale, saw, edge, trim, dry,
plane, grade, bundle, ship, stock — is gone, not because it was unfair but
because it was unnecessary. The wedge does not need the tree to become a
rectangle, so it does not need the machinery whose entire purpose is
producing rectangles.

![Every step between a standing tree and a board on a rack.](../../deliverables/book/figures/middlemen-chain.png)

## The chain, and where the wood goes

The drawing tells the story the walk could not, because the story is not
only who gets paid. It is also what disappears.

Every processing step removes material, and the industrial chain removes it
at both ends and in the middle. The slabs and edgings off the round log. The
planer shavings off the dressed board. The trim cuts. The degrade — boards
that crack, warp or stain while drying and get down-graded or scrapped. When
the same trunk this book uses is sawn into two-by-fours, roughly
45 per cent of the solid wood survives as saleable
lumber. The other 87.4 per cent of a good log — minus the
kerf — survives when it is split into wedges instead. Chapter
13 does that arithmetic in full, including the parts that are
less flattering than the headline. Here it belongs in the chain picture for
a simpler reason: the waste is not an accident of carelessness. It is the
price of the rectangle, paid in wood, at a chain of stations that each
exists to pay it.

Look at the diagram with that in mind. The industrial route is long because
each station exists to solve a problem the previous station created. The
sawmill exists to cut rectangles, so the edger exists to clean their edges,
so the planer exists to smooth them, so the kiln exists to dry them flat, so
the grader exists to sort what all of that did to the wood. The wedge route
is short because it never creates the rectangle problem. There is nothing
to edge, nothing to plane flat, and a green wedge that dries is allowed to
move a little, because the frame was designed for a stick that is already
the shape the tree made.

## The price of a 2x4, taken apart

The shelf price this book uses is 8.88 dollars for a
sixteen-foot two-by-four — a real sticker price, declared up front in the
constants table at the front of the book, because it is a price and prices
are looked up, not derived.

Now take it apart, and the first thing to notice is how little of it is the
tree.

The tree's share of that board, at the stump, is a fraction of the sticker.
A sixteen-foot two-by-four is nominally 8.00 square inches
by sixteen feet — a little under eleven board feet of wood. At a stumpage
price of a few cents a board foot standing, the wood in that board left the
forest worth pocket change, and arrived on the rack worth
8.88 dollars. Everything in between — the fell, the haul,
the sawing, the drying, the planing, the grading, the shipping, the yard,
the desk — is what the price actually buys. The wood is the smallest line
on the receipt.

That is not a complaint about the lumber industry. It is a measurement of
what the industry is: a logistics and processing business that happens to
have trees as its raw material. And it is the exact reason the wedge method
works economically. When you stand next to a tree that is already down,
with a saw in your hand, you are not competing with the industry's skill.
You are simply not buying its product. The 14 steps are
replaced by 8, and the price of the building material
collapses to fuel, chain oil, and your own hours.

Chapter 45 does that arithmetic both ways — what the frame costs
bought, against what it costs cut — and prints the hourly rate it implies,
including the version that flatters the method least. This chapter only
needed to establish why the two numbers are so far apart. They are far
apart because one of them is a price for wood and the other is a price for
fourteen services, and the services are what the saw on your shelf makes
unnecessary.

## Nominal, dressed, and the missing third

A two-by-four is not two inches by four inches. It is sold as if it were,
and the difference matters enough that the book keeps both numbers on the
page wherever wood is counted.

**The nominal board** — the name on the ticket — is two by four:
8.00 square inches of section.

**The dressed board** — the thing you actually carry out of the yard — is
one and a half by three and a half: 5.25 square inches.
The rest went to the planer.

That is a thirty-five per cent difference in section between what the
lumber is called and what it is, and it is not a trick; it is the planing
step from the chain above, made visible. The book always says which board
it means when it compares, because comparing a wedge against a nominal
two-by-four flatters the wedge by a third, and a book that flatters its own
material by accident is not worth reading. There is also a third board the
industry does not sell and the book does not pretend to: the rough-sawn
two-by-four, planer-skipped, at something between the two numbers. The
money chapter (Chapter 45) and the head-to-head in Chapter
7 both state their board explicitly, and where the choice
matters they print the comparison against both.

Why the book cares this much: because the wedge's honest number — the
figure that decides whether the whole idea is worth anything — changes by
that thirty-five per cent depending on which board you compare it against.
The book's answer, stated once here and used everywhere after: compare
against the dressed board. It is what you would have bought, and it is the
smaller, less flattering target. The method is strong enough to be
measured against it.

---

# 3. The Frankendome

*Forty triangles, no two struts alike, and the failure that taught the method*

## The Frankendome

Before the dome this book is about, there was another dome, and it matters
that it existed, because every decision in the method that followed was a
reaction to what that first one got wrong.

It was built from whatever wood was lying around — offcuts, salvaged sticks,
mixed sections, boards that had been other things. Forty triangles, joined
edge to edge, each strut whatever shape the pile happened to supply. The
idea was deliberately forgiving: do not sort, do not select, do not machine.
Let the connector absorb the difference between one stick and the next.

The idea worked, which is the worst thing that can happen to an idea that is
secretly bad. The frame stood. It stood for six months, outside, through
weather, and it taught more about how a dome behaves than any drawing had.
And it was awful: lumpy, twisting, no two joints alike, and impossible to
reproduce. Every lesson in this book is downstream of that specific
combination — *it worked, and it was awful* — because it proved both halves
of the method at once. A triangulated frame will stand on almost any
consistent-ish sticks, which is the permission. And nothing about a frame
built that way repeats, which is the trap.

This chapter is the story of the trap.

## Strut-agnostic, and what that cost

The design rule of the first build was *strut-agnostic*: no stick is special,
no stick is measured, any piece of wood of roughly the right length can be
any member. Assemble the triangles from the top of the dome down, one at a
time, and wherever two edges meet, a folded bracket joins them. Eight screws
a bracket. Two bolts a seam. Tighten, move on.

The appeal was real. The pile was free. Nothing needed grading. A stick that
was two inches wide where the next was three could still be a strut, because
the bracket's flaps could be bent to whatever the wood wanted, and the screws
found the wood where the wood was. Building that way feels like freedom —
every decision made at the moment of assembly, by the piece in your hand —
and it took the whole build to learn what that freedom cost.

It cost everything that comes after a build. The dome stood, but:

**Nothing repeated.** No two members were alike, so no operation could be
jigged. Every cut, every hole, every bracket bend was a one-off, made to
fit the exact stick in hand. The fortieth panel took as much thinking as the
first.

**The connector did all the work.** Because the sticks agreed about nothing,
the bracket had to absorb every difference: width, thickness, angle,
wander. That is a lot to ask of a folded piece of metal, in forty places,
for the life of the building — and Chapter 4
is the afternoon I spent learning exactly how much.

**It could not be drawn.** There was no cut list, because there was no
standard member. The frame existed only as itself; you could not hand the
build to anyone, and you could not build a second one.

**It was never finished.** A frame that is improvised joint by joint is a
frame you keep adjusting. It stood, but it never stopped being the thing
you had to keep an eye on.

The photograph on the next page is that dome. It is in this book because the
book's method is best understood as the answer to it, and because it is
honest to show the before. A book about a better way to build owes the
reader the worse way it is better than.

![The frankendome: it stood, and no two members matched.](../../deliverables/book/figures/franken-standing.png)

## The frankendome standing

Look at it. Lumpy where a solved dome is smooth. Every member its own
section, its own bow, its own idea of straight. The seams pinch and gap
where the sticks disagreed. And yet the shape is recognisably a dome, and it
is standing, which is the part that is easy to forget when the tidy version
of this story gets told: the thing did work. A frame of forty triangles,
joined any-old-how, will carry its own weight and a skin. Triangulation is
that forgiving — Chapter 6 explains why — and the method in
this book inherits that forgiveness as its foundation.

What the photograph cannot show is the labour inside it. Ten days to stand
it, not because the work was hard but because none of it repeated. Every
bracket was folded to a different angle. Every screw row was laid out fresh.
The second dome took as long as the first would have, because there was no
first dome there was only a sequence of one-offs.

The wedge method exists because of what this picture makes obvious: the
geometry of a dome does not care what shape its sticks are, but the person
building it absolutely does, and the difference between a frame you build
once and a method you can build with is whether anything in it repeats.

## The ten-piece pentagon

One part of the frankendome was genuinely good, and it is worth separating
it from the rest, because it was the only piece carried forward.

The base — the ring where the dome meets the ground — was not improvised.
It was ten pieces, cut to a plan, joined into a decagon, and it went together
in an afternoon and never moved. The contrast with the chaos above it was
the whole lesson of the build in miniature: the one part that was drawn
before it was cut, the one part where every piece had a name and a number,
was the one part that behaved.

That decagon became the footing of the method. The frame this book builds
also meets the ground as a ten-sided ring — 10 rim edges,
each a member in its own right — and the ring is the one place the book
tells you to set out with a string and a check rather than a shrug. Chapter
39 is that ring, given the treatment the
frankendome proved it deserved.

The pentagon, too, came forward in spirit. The frankendome's triangles were
joined edge to edge in whatever arrangement the assembly order produced; the
method's panels are assembled into the same two triangle shapes every dome
has — the same count, the same shapes, the same corners. What changed is
not the geometry. It is that in the method, every panel of a given shape is
built on the same fixture, from sticks of the same section, with the same
holes. The frankendome proved the shape. The method made the shape
repeatable.

## Why I stopped

The frankendome was not abandoned because it fell down. It was abandoned
because of a sentence I could not unthink, after watching the tenth improvised
joint go in:

**A thing you build twice is only worth doing by hand once.**

Nothing in that dome repeated, so nothing could be jigged, so nothing could
get faster, and nothing could get more accurate, and no second dome could
ever be the same as the first. The strut-agnostic idea had removed the need
for precision at the cost of removing the possibility of it. Every bracket
was a small negotiation between two sticks that had never met and never
would again. That is not a build method. It is a sculpture process that
happens to produce a room.

The question the wedge method answers is the question the frankendome left
on the table: what would the sticks have to look like for one fixture to
build every panel? The answer turned out to be in the next chapter — not
*which sticks to buy*, but *which way to open the log*. And the moment that
answer landed, the frankendome stopped being a failed building and became
what it is in this book: the control group. Same dome. Different pile.

Chapter 5 is where the pile changed.

---

# 4. The V-Bracket Afternoon

*Trying to join shapes that have nothing in common*

## The V-Bracket Afternoon

Between the frankendome and the wedge there was an afternoon spent trying to
make the frankendome's problem go away by making a better bracket. That
afternoon matters because it is the step most builders would have stopped
at, and because the bracket it produced is a genuinely good answer to the
wrong question.

The problem was stated clearly by then: the frame's sticks did not match, so
the joint had to. The fix seemed obvious — stop improvising the joints one at
a time and design one connector good enough to join any stick to any stick.
Do the tolerance in metal, once, instead of in thinking, forty times.

So I sat down and designed the V-bracket: two flat flaps meeting at the
angle the panels make, four holes in each flap, the flaps long enough to
run half the length of a member so the screws spread along the stick instead
of bunching at its end. Fold one from flat stock in a minute on a bench
brake, and it will hold any two pieces of wood that arrive at the corner,
whatever their sections, whatever their bows.

And it worked. That is the trap, again. A bracket that absorbs every
difference works on the first panel and the fortieth. What it costs does not
show up until you look at what the bracket has become.

## A bracket that does not care

Here is why the V shape is good at its job, because the goodness is real and
the method that follows borrows from it.

A V-bracket does not assume a member's width. The flaps are wider than any
stick the build will use, so a two-inch board and a three-inch board both
land on the same plate, and the screw rows find whatever wood is there. It
does not assume a thickness: the bend that forms the V is made to the
panel's fold angle at assembly, not stamped to a tolerance at a factory. And
it does not assume straightness: the screws are what pull the stick to the
plate, and a bowed stick just gets pulled harder.

That is a beautiful property, and it is worth saying what it is in one
sentence, because the wedge method is, in a way, this sentence taken to its
conclusion: **the connector absorbs the difference, so the wood does not
have to be made the same.**

The warning sign was in the same sentence. When the connector absorbs every
difference, the connector is now doing all the work of the frame. Every
joint is a negotiation, held by screws that are the only thing in the whole
structure that know the shape they are enforcing. Eight screws a bracket,
two bolts a seam, all of it site-bent, site-drilled, site-tightened — the
connector was not a detail. It had become the building.

And a connector that does everything has to be *right* everywhere. The
flaps were long, which spread the load, which was honest engineering — and
it meant forty-odd brackets, each consuming stock, each needing folding,
drilling, fitting, checking. The frankendome had replaced the problem of
matching wood with the problem of manufacturing forgiveness, in metal, at
forty corners. Same labour, moved.

## Four joints that nearly worked

The drawing shows the four joints that happened along the way, because the
V-bracket was not arrived at in one step, and each of the earlier ones
failed in a way that named a piece of the final answer.

**The hub.** A flat plate with every member bolting to its own face — the
classic geodesic hub. It assumes all members arrive at the same point at
the same angle, and they never do when the sticks are salvaged. Every
attempt needed shims, washers, longer bolts. The hub is perfect for a kit
of identical struts, and useless the moment the struts differ.

**The angle plate.** A single fold, made to the panel's angle. It joins two
sticks in a line but cannot hold the third that closes the triangle, and
the third is the one that turns three sticks into structure.

**The wrap.** A strip of steel wrapped around both members and screwed
through. It holds anything, for a while, but it crushes nothing into
alignment — the joint stays as crooked as the wood, and every wrap is a
one-off that has to be cut to the pair it is joining.

**The V-bracket.** The survivor, and it failed too — not at holding, but at
repeating. It can join anything to anything, which is exactly why no two of
them are alike, which is exactly why the frame never gets faster to build.

![Four joints, and the one thing each could not do.](../../deliverables/book/figures/bracket-attempts.png)

The pattern across all four is the same. Each joint failed not by being weak
but by being asked to solve, in metal, a problem the wood kept re-creating.
The hub assumed sameness. The plate assumed a line. The wrap assumed
nothing and therefore had to be made for everything. The V accepted all of
it and paid in labour. None of them asked the question the next chapter
does — not *how do I join whatever arrives*, but *what would have to be
true for the sticks to arrive the same*.

## The question that ended it

The afternoon ended with the bracket in my hand doing its job, and a
question that would not go away. The bracket worked by absorbing difference,
and it had taken me all afternoon to make a bracket good at that. But every
flap length, every screw, every bend was the cost of a difference that did
not need to exist.

So: if the bracket no longer had to absorb any difference — if the sticks
arrived at the corner already the same section, already at the same angle,
already the same length — what would the joint collapse to?

The answer, worked out later, is Chapter 15: a member end
landing on a member side, one key, a few screws, nothing folded, nothing
measured twice. But the more important answer arrived that same afternoon,
and it is the sentence that turned the project around:

**Do not build a joint that tolerates the difference. Build sticks that do
not have one.**

That is the wedge method's founding sentence. The bracket was a good idea
pushed past its natural size; it remains in the method only as a small,
fixed part where a fold is genuinely the right tool. What it taught was
that the wood was the thing to fix, and the wood was the one thing I had
not tried changing — because a stick of wood seemed like something you buy,
not something you decide. Chapter 5 is the afternoon that
stopped being true.

The bracket attempts earned their page in this book for one more reason:
they are the roadmap of every wrong turn a builder can make at a dome's
corner. Somebody, somewhere, is designing a hub right now. The book's
answer is the drawing above, and the sentence under it: make the sticks
match, and the joint stops being the hard part.

---

# 5. The Cut That Changed It

*Split the trunk like a cake, and the stick is already the right shape*



## The Cut That Changed It

I want to tell this one without hindsight tidying it up, because the tidy
version — *I realised the dome would accept any consistent section, therefore
I split the log radially* — is not what happened and it teaches nothing. What
happened was that I was tired of brackets.

## Half, half, half again

The frankendome had taught me that a frame of forty triangles will stand up
even if no two of its sticks match. The V-bracket afternoon had taught me
that making a connector tolerant enough to join anything to anything means
the connector is now doing all the work, forever, in forty places.

So I had stopped asking how to join dissimilar sticks and started asking the
opposite question: what would it take for them not to be dissimilar?

The answer that everybody gives is *mill them*. Square them up, run them
through a planer, and now they are all 2x4s. That answer costs a mill, a
planer, a shed to keep them in, and about half the tree.

I was sitting on a section of trunk at the time. Not thinking about it.
Sitting on it.

And the thing about a round section of trunk is that it is already
rotationally symmetric. Every radius is the same as every other radius. If
you split it through the middle you get two identical halves, not because you
were careful but because the log was round before you touched it. Split those
and you get four. Split those and you get 8.

Three passes. Eight sticks. Every one the same, to whatever tolerance the
tree was round to, which is a tolerance nobody had to hold and nobody had to
check.

![Three cuts. Eight structural members.](../../deliverables/book/figures/trunk-eighths.png)

I did it that afternoon on a piece I had been about to burn.

## What became obvious immediately

Four things, in about a minute, and I want to record that they were obvious
because the ones in the next section took a week and I do not want to flatter
the story.

**One section, everywhere.** Every stick in the frame has the same
cross-section: 3.83 inches across the bark face,
5.00 inches from pith to bark, 9.82 square
inches of wood. So one jig fits all of them. One fixture, one set of stops,
one procedure repeated a hundred and twenty times. That is what makes this a
fortnight instead of a summer.

**Depth where you want it.** A sector is deep — it runs the whole way from
the pith to the bark — and depth is where bending stiffness lives. The stick
is not a compromise between what the tree gave and what a mill wanted. It is
the deepest member you can cut from that log, which is a strange thing to get
for free.

**No machine between the tree and the frame.** Fell, buck, split, build. The
chainsaw does all of it. There is no second machine, no second setup, no
second building to keep the second machine in.

**Four ways up.** This one I noticed while stacking them. The sector is not
symmetric about its own long axis — it has a point and a curved back — so you
can rotate it four sensible ways in the wall and get four different things:
which face the weather sees, which face the next stick lands on, whether the
points of a pair aim together or apart. That is Chapter 14, and it is the
most genuinely new thing in the method.

## And what took another week

Now the part the tidy version leaves out.

I assumed, because the sticks were identical and the panels were identical,
that the *joints* would be identical. Forty triangles, all the same, all
meeting each other the same way.

They do not.

A dome is a sphere approximated by flat panels, and the angle at which two
panels meet is not the same everywhere on it. It cannot be — the sphere
curves differently in different places, and a 2V hemisphere has two classes
of edge for exactly that reason. So the seam between two panels folds by one
amount here and a different amount there, and a key cut to fit one seam will
not fit the other.

I did not want this to be true. I spent the better part of a week trying to
make it not be true: fudging the panel outlines, trying a compressible
gasket, trying to talk myself into the idea that the difference was small
enough to force closed. It is not small enough. Forty panels of *nearly*
closing is a dome with a gap in it.

What actually resolved it was measuring instead of arguing. Across all
55 seams there are only **2** fold
angles. Not forty. Not a continuum. Two: 18.03 degrees and
22.46.

That was the moment the method became buildable rather than merely elegant.
2 angles means 2 key profiles:
cut one of each until it fits, then repeat it 25 and
30 times. The difference between "the seams vary" and "the
seams take 2 values" is the difference between a
research project and a fortnight, and I lost a week to not knowing which one
I was in.

The chart is in Chapter 16, where it belongs.

## The bit I got wrong in public

While all this was going on I was saying, out loud and on camera, that
nothing in the dome needed a mitre.

That is not true and it was never true. The end of each stick that lands on
the side of the next one — the butt end — is a compound cut: a mitre and a
bevel together. What is true, and what I should have been saying, is that no
end is ever mitred *to another end*. Every joint is an end landing on a
flat side, which means each stick is cut once, on one end, before it goes
anywhere near the assembly, and the other end is left long and sawn off in
place.

That is a much better claim than the one I made, and it is a shame I spent
several months making the wrong one. Chapter 15 has the geometry, and the errata pages in this part carry the rest of what this project has had to take back.

## What it is worth

The number that convinced me it was not just a neat trick: splitting this way
keeps 87.4 per cent of the solid wood in the trunk, against
about 45 per cent for the same log sawn into
two-by-fours the way a mill would. That is 1.93 times the
usable wood, from a cheaper process, with one machine instead of four.

Chapter 13 does that arithmetic properly, including the parts of it that are
less flattering than they first appeared.

But sitting on that log, what I had was a much simpler observation, and I
still think it is the whole book:

**A round thing splits into identical pieces for free. A square thing has to
be made square, and you pay for that in wood, in machines, and in time.
Domes are the buildings that will accept the free pieces.**

---

# 6. Triangles Do Not Care What Shape Your Stick Is

*Why a triangulated frame can accept a member no mill would sell*

## Triangles Do Not Care

This chapter is the permission slip for the whole method, so it goes first
in the explanation strand and it is short enough to be unfair to nobody.

Start with the sentence the title page made its argument out of: **your
skull is not symmetrical either, and it protects the brain just fine.**
The frame is the part of this building you are allowed to get wrong — the
lumpy, split, self-labelled frame that the frankendome proved will stand.
The parts that must be right are the skin and the ground, and the book
treats those with the care the title page promised. This chapter is the
*reason* the frame is allowed to be lumpy, stated as geometry rather than
as reassurance.

The wedge method proposes to build a building's frame out of sticks that are
not rectangular — a fifth of a round log, sawn faces, bark back, a point at
one edge. No lumberyard sells that. No building code assumes it. And yet the
frame stands, because of one property of triangles that is easy to state and
takes the rest of this chapter to spend:

**A triangle does not care what shape its sticks are. It only cares how long
they are, and that they reach each other.**

Everything the mill does — square the edges, flatten the faces, dress the
section to a standard — exists because rectangles ask their pieces to be
beams: flat boards, loaded across their faces, whose stiffness and strength
depend on the exact shape of the section. A triangulated frame asks
something else entirely of its pieces. It asks them to be *struts*: pushed
or pulled along their own length, ends only, never sideways, never flat.
For a strut, what matters is how much wood is in the section and how far the
load is from pith to bark — not whether the section is a rectangle.

So the central question of the book is not "is a wedge as good as a 2x4?"
in general — Chapter 7 runs that comparison honestly and
prints the column the wedge loses. The right question is narrower and much
more useful: *inside a triangulated frame, where members are loaded along
their length, is a wedge a good member?* This chapter builds the frame that
question assumes, so the later chapters can answer it.

## Axial, not bending

The difference between a strut and a beam is the difference between the two
ways a building can load a piece of wood, and most of conventional framing
is the second way.

**A beam is loaded across its length.** A floor joist carries the floor on
its narrow face; a rafter carries the roof on its wide one; a stud carries
weight down its length but braces against sideways push. In all of them the
load is trying to bend the piece, and bending is cruel: the material at the
middle of the section does almost nothing, which is why an I-beam has its
steel at the top and bottom and air in between. Bending strength lives in
the extreme fibres, far from the section's centre, so the shape of the
section — rectangle, and which way round — decides how strong the piece is.
This is why a 2x4 on edge is dramatically stiffer than the same 2x4 laid
flat, and why the mill bothers to dress boards to a true rectangle at all.
A rectangular building is a system for turning wood into beams, and beams
need rectangles.

**A strut is loaded along its length.** Squeeze the ends of a stick toward
each other, or pull them apart, and every fibre of the section shares the
load equally. Shape stops mattering; the amount of wood matters. A round
stick, a square stick, a wedge — for axial load, they differ only in
section area (and, for a long thin strut in compression, in the way the
section resists buckling, where depth matters more than shape).

Now look at a triangulated frame. Every load that arrives at a joint is
carried to the next joint along the members, as push or pull along their
lengths. The frame does not bend its members; it moves force through them.
That is the entire trick of geodesic structures, and it is why a dome's
struts can be slender for their span, and why the frame stands up without a
single piece of sheet material keeping it square. A rectangle needs a
diagonal, or a sheet of plywood, or it racks over and lies down. A triangle
is already its own diagonal. The drawing on the next page is that sentence.

![A rectangle needs a sheet to stand. A triangle does not.](../../deliverables/book/figures/racking-compare.png)

## The same load, two frames

Look at the two frames side by side.

On the left, a rectangle. Push on its top corner the way wind pushes on a
wall, and the rectangle does what rectangles do: the corners stop being
ninety degrees, the frame leans, and unless a sheet of plywood or a diagonal
brace is holding it, it goes over. The joints are doing everything the
shape cannot. That is why a conventional wall is a frame *plus* sheathing —
the studs are beams, and the sheet is the triangle they never built.

On the right, the same corner, triangulated. Push on it. The load cannot
change the angles, because the angles are fixed by the lengths of the three
sticks, and a stick's length cannot change without stretching or squashing
the stick itself. So the push travels down the members as pure compression
and pure tension, along the grain, exactly where wood is strongest and
exactly where shape does not matter. Nothing bends. Nothing needs a sheet.
The frame is its own bracing, at every corner, forever.

The whole method follows from that picture. Because members are loaded
axially, the frame will accept a member of any section that has enough area
and enough depth — which is precisely what a split log provides. Because
nothing bends, the frame does not ask for the flat, dressed, graded faces a
beam economy demands. And because the triangle's angles are fixed by its
lengths, the frame enforces its own geometry: get the lengths right and the
shape has no choice. The frankendome stood on that fact alone, with no two
members matching, and it stands on the same fact with every member
identical. Chapter 3 was the demonstration. This
chapter is the reason.

## What the wedge is good at

If the frame wants area and depth, the wedge should be measured by area and
depth — and on a modest log, it beats the store-bought board at both.

A dressed two-by-four carries 5.25 square inches of wood,
planed down from its nominal size — a rectangle, because it must be a beam. The wedge this book cuts is 3.83 inches
across its bark face and 5.00 inches from its point to its
bark, for 9.82 square inches of section. The comparison that
matters is in Chapter 7, computed properly on an
8-inch log so the wedge gets no advantage from a fat
tree. Here, two qualitative points are enough to set the direction:

**Depth is where stiffness lives.** In bending, stiffness rises with the
*cube* of depth; double the depth and a beam gets eight times stiffer.
A wedge runs from the pith to the bark — the full radius of the log. For
the same width of stick, that is more depth than any board a mill cuts
from the same tree, because the mill's rectangle has to fit inside the
circle, and the wedge does not.

**Area is where axial strength lives.** The wedge is a sector: one eighth
of the log's circle. Measured as a strut — which is what the frame asks it
to be — it carries more wood in its section than the dressed board the
same log would have produced, because it never gave up the corners to the
edger and the planer. Chapter 13 counts those corners; the
count is the difference between 87.4 per cent and
45 per cent of the tree.

The honest note, saved for the full table: a wedge is not a better *beam*.
Lay it flat and ask it to be a shelf and the missing rectangle matters.
The claim is narrower and it is the only one the book makes — in this
frame, loaded this way, the wedge is a better use of the tree than the
board, and the shape the tree already had is not a defect the mill forgot
to remove. It is the feature the frame was waiting for.

## One eighth of a tree

The section, stated plainly, because the next chapters argue with it:

A 6-foot section of this book's tree, split into
8 equal sectors, yields sticks of 9.82 square
inches each — 3.83 inches across the bark face,
5.00 inches deep from point to bark.

The dressed two-by-four the same tree would have been sold as carries
5.25 square inches. The nominal one — the name on the
ticket, which no longer exists in the wood — carries 8.00.

So the wedge is a bigger section than the board the mill would have cut
from the same log, in the one measure that matters for a strut, and it
cost a chainsaw's fuel instead of a mill, a kiln, a planer and a yard.
That single line — 9.82 against 5.25,
from the same tree — is the economic engine of the whole book, and
everything after this page is the machinery that turns it into a standing
frame.

---

# 7. Not a Worse Two-by-Four

*The head-to-head nobody runs honestly, run honestly*



## Not a Worse Two-by-Four

Everybody asks the same question, and they are right to ask it.

*Is a split log actually as good as a board?*

The honest answer has three parts, and only two of them are flattering. This
chapter gives all three, because a method defended only with its good numbers
is a method nobody should trust with a roof over their head.

## The rectangle is not the tree's idea

Modern construction has trained us to think of wood as something that
naturally arrives in rectangles. A tree goes into a sawmill and a stack of
2x4s comes out, and the form is so familiar that it stops looking like a
choice.

It is a choice. The rectangle is not a property of wood. It is the output of
a manufacturing process designed around a particular kind of building — one
made of vertical studs, horizontal plates, flat walls, right-angle corners
and rectangular openings. For that building the rectangle is close to
perfect, and the industry that produces it is very good indeed at what it
does.

A geodesic dome is almost the opposite of that building. Its structural
language is triangles, changing face planes, radial geometry and repeated
angular joints. Nothing in it is a right angle. Nothing in it is a flat wall.

So the question reopens, and it is worth asking properly rather than
assuming the answer in either direction: *for this building, is the
rectangle still the right thing to make out of the tree?*

## More wood, from a modest tree

Start with the simplest measure: how much wood is actually in the stick.

Take an 8-inch log — not a monster, a tree most people
could fell and move without help. Split it 8 ways and each
sector holds **6.28 square inches** of wood.

A dressed 2x4 — the board actually on the rack, which is
1.5 x 3.5 inches whatever it says on the label — holds
**5.25**.

That is **+20 per cent more wood** in the wedge, from a
tree you could carry out of a wood lot in sections.

And it is the conservative version of the claim. A larger log widens the gap
sharply, because area grows with the square of the radius while a 2x4 stays a
2x4. The book quotes the small log deliberately: a claim is worth more made at
its weakest.

The wedge is also deeper. Pith to bark is 4.00 inches,
against 1.5 x 3.5 for the board — and depth is where bending
resistance comes from.

![The two sections, at the same scale.](../../deliverables/book/figures/section-compare.png)

## And now the number that does not help

Here is where an honest chapter parts company with a sales pitch.

**Area is not strength.** How much a section resists bending depends not only
on how much wood is in it but on *where that wood sits* — material far from
the middle does most of the work, material near the middle does almost none.

And a wedge puts a great deal of its wood near the pith, in the narrow part
of the pie slice, where it contributes very little.

Run the numbers properly and the comparison comes out like this:

| | wedge | 2x4 on edge |
|---|---|---|
| Wood in the section | **+20%** | — |
| Resistance to bending (stiffness) | **-1%** | — |
| Bending strength | **-33%** | — |

Read that middle row again. The wedge has +20 per cent
more wood in it and is **not stiffer**. It comes out within a per cent of a
2x4 stood on edge — effectively identical, having spent twenty per cent more
material to get there.

And the bottom row is worse: in bending *strength* the wedge is
-33 per cent against the same board.

That is the number this method's advocates do not usually print, and it is
printed here, in a table, near the front.

![The comparison in full.](../../deliverables/book/figures/versus-table.png)

## One consolation, before the argument

There is a caveat to the caveat, and it is a fair one.

The comparison above puts the 2x4 **on edge** — the orientation in which a
board is strongest, and the way a carefully framed floor joist is installed.
A great deal of real framing does not get that. Sheathing battens, blocking,
purlins and plenty of cheerfully improvised structures end up loading boards
the flat way, and against a 2x4 laid flat the wedge is
**5.4 times stiffer**.

So the honest summary is: worse than a well-oriented board, dramatically
better than a badly-oriented one.

That is still not the argument.

## Which is the right question

The argument is that *the comparison itself is the wrong shape*.

Asking "is this wedge as good a beam as a 2x4?" assumes that being a beam is
the job. In a rectangular building it usually is: a stud, a joist and a
rafter all spend their lives resisting bending, largely alone.

A triangulated frame does not work that way. That is the entire point of
triangulating it. A properly designed triangle converts an external load into
forces running *along* its members — compression down one, tension along
another — instead of asking any single member to bend. The network carries the
load; the sticks mostly just push and pull.

And for a member being pushed or pulled along its length, the property that
matters is **cross-sectional area**. Not section modulus. Area.

Which is the column the wedge wins, by +20 per cent.

So the useful question is not:

> Is this wedge as good a rectangular beam as a 2x4?

but:

> Is this wedge an effective axial member inside this triangulated system?

And there the answer is straightforwardly yes, with more wood in the section
than the board it replaces, from a tree that never went near a mill.

## Mostly

One more piece of honesty, because "mostly axial" is doing real work in that
paragraph.

Panels are not purely axial. Wind pushes on the skin between the members and
snow sits on it, and those loads reach the frame as bending in the individual
sticks. It is not the dominant case and it is not nothing, and it is exactly
where the wedge's -33 per cent deficit is a real deficit
rather than an academic one.

Two things about that:

* The wedge is deep. 4.00 inches pith to bark is more
  depth than the board has, and depth is what resists the bending it does
  see — which is why the stiffness came out level rather than behind despite
  the shape being worse.
* Panel span is short. The distance between supports in a 2V panel is a few
  feet, not the fifteen a floor joist crosses, and bending demand falls away
  fast as spans shorten.

None of which is a substitute for somebody competent checking your particular
building for your particular snow load. Chapter 10 says the same
thing at more length and names who to ask.

## What this chapter does not claim

It is worth fencing the argument, so nobody quotes it doing work it cannot do.

This chapter does **not** claim that a split-log wedge is better than a 2x4
as a wall stud, a floor joist, a roof rafter, a shelf, a header or a piece of
furniture. For all of those the board wins, often easily, and the reasons are
in the table above.

It claims something narrower and much more defensible:

> For a purpose-designed geodesic dome whose geometry, connectors and jigs
> are built around radially split timber, the wedge is a better use of the
> tree than first converting that same tree into ordinary dimensional
> lumber.

That is the whole claim. It depends on the building being designed around the
material from the beginning — which is what the rest of this book is about.

We are not asking an unconventional piece of wood to imitate a 2x4.

We are building something in which it does not have to.

---

# 8. Forty Panels, One Hundred and Twenty Members

*The 2V hemisphere, counted*

## Forty Panels

This chapter names the frame itself, because every number after it — the
cut list, the jig, the fortnight, the seams — is a count of something that
exists on this frame, and the frame deserves to be introduced before it is
counted.

It is a two-frequency geodesic dome: a hemisphere of 40 flat
triangular panels, held up by 120 wooden members. There are
65 distinct edges in its geometry, of which 55
are seams where two panels meet and 10 run around the
base where the dome meets the ground and join nothing. Those are the four
numbers the whole book hangs on, and none of them was chosen. All of them
fell out of the geometry — an icosahedron, subdivided once, projected onto
a sphere, cut in half at its widest line — and the rest of this chapter
shows where they come from, because a count you can retrace is a count you
can trust, and a count you cannot is a story.

The frame in the picture is the actual solved dome this book builds — the
same meshes the simulator solves, pushed apart along each panel's own
normal so you can see that it really is forty separate triangles and not
one continuous surface. That separation is not just a drawing trick. It is
how the thing gets built, one finished triangle at a time, on the ground,
and it is the subject of Chapter 18.

## Where 2V comes from

The geometry is older than the dome and worth knowing once, because
everything the frame does follows from it.

Start with an icosahedron: twenty identical equilateral triangles, twelve
vertices, the roundest of the five Platonic solids. It is already close to
a sphere, but its faces are big flat slabs. Subdivide: split every edge in
half and connect the midpoints, and each of the twenty faces becomes four
smaller triangles — eighty faces in all. Now the move that makes it a
dome: push every vertex outward until it lies exactly on a sphere of the
same radius. The triangles stop being identical — those nearer the
original vertices stretch differently from those near the midpoints — but
they all lie on one spherical surface, which is what "geodesic" means
here: the straight lines of the original faces become the chords of a
sphere.

Cut that sphere at its equator and keep the top half. What remains is the
frame: 40 triangles, because a full subdivided icosahedron
has eighty faces and the hemisphere keeps half.

That one operation — subdivide once — is what "2V" names: the frequency
two subdivision of the icosahedron. It is the smallest subdivision that
produces a useful building, and its gift is the number in Chapter
9: all 65 edges of the hemisphere
come in only two lengths, because the subdivision only ever produces two
kinds of edge — the original icosahedron edges, now halved, and the new
edges between midpoints. Two lengths, not forty, is what makes one person
with one jig able to build this at all. Chapter 47 shows
what higher frequencies buy and cost; the whole of the rest of this book
stays at 2V, because 2V is where the trade stops being worth paying.

![Forty panels, 120 members, 55 seams between them.](../../deliverables/book/figures/frame-exploded.png)

## The frame, exploded

The plate is the frame with its 40 panels pushed apart along
their own normals, each triangle floating a little way out from where it
will finally sit. Read it as an inventory before you read it as a picture.

**Forty triangles.** Two shapes, in a fixed ratio: the triangles that sit
around the five-fold points and the triangles that fill between them. Not
forty shapes — two — which is the second great simplification of the
frame, after the two lengths.

**Three members each.** Every triangle is built complete, with its own
three sticks, its own joints, its own edge. Three times forty is
120: the member count of the whole frame, and the number the
tree chapter will chase in wood.

**The seams where they will meet.** Where two panel edges lie against each
other there will be a seam — a key between the two members, a gasket, a
line on the outside of the shell. The frame has 55 of them.
The 10 edges around the base are different: nothing meets
them from outside, so they are rim, not seam, and the book keeps the two
counts apart because a builder closing 55 seams must not be
told to close 65.

![Counted from the solved dome, not chosen.](../../deliverables/book/figures/frame-counts.png)

## What the frame contains

The table counts the frame two ways and they agree, which is the whole
point of printing it.

**By panels:** 40 panels times 3
members each is 120 members.

**By edges:** 55 seams each holding two members — one from
each neighbouring panel — plus 10 rim edges holding one,
is 120 again.

The two routes give the same number because the frame is panelised: every
panel owns all three of its edges, so an interior seam is always a
sandwich of two members and a key, never one shared strut. That costs
wood — 1.85 members for every unique edge — and
Chapter 18 is the full accounting of what the extra wood
buys. For this chapter the table's job is narrower: the frame is not a
number somebody remembered, it is a number the geometry produces, and the
geometry produces it twice.

## Why hemispheres and not spheres

One honest question deserves an answer before the build chapters start:
why is the book about half a sphere, when the geometry naturally makes a
whole one?

**A hemisphere has a floor.** Cut a sphere at its equator and the cut
line is a flat circle, lying in a plane, exactly where a building wants
its ground. The dome meets the earth along its 10 base
edges, all in one plane, and every vertex of the base ring sits on the
ground at once. A full sphere has no such line; it touches the ground at
a point and the entire weight of the building hangs from that point's
anchorage. The hemisphere is the shape a sphere becomes when you give it
somewhere to stand.

**The rim is the honest part.** Those 10 base edges are
the one place the geometry does not close on itself — the place where the
frame's seams run out. The book treats the rim as its own piece of the
build: Chapter 39 gives it its own footing,
Chapter 41 puts its doors in it, and Chapter
38 names it as the one place error can accumulate.
A hemisphere is not a sphere with the bottom missing. It is the shape
that has a bottom, and that is why it can be a building at all.

**And the crown is the trade.** Half a sphere sacrifices the volume above
the equator — the part of the ball nobody could stand in anyway — and
keeps everything from the widest line down, which is where the room is.
The floor area, the headroom, and the standing-height map are all counted
from that choice in Chapter 43. For now the frame simply
is what it is: 40 triangles, 120 members,
55 seams, 10 rim edges — the count the
geometry gives, printed whole, before anything is built from it.

---

# 9. Two Lengths, Not Forty

*The whole reason a dome can be built by one person*

## Two Lengths

Chapter 8 counted the frame:
40 panels, 120 members, 55 seams.
This chapter is about the number inside that count that makes the whole
thing buildable by one person with one jig, and it is the smallest
simplification in the entire method:

**Every one of the dome's 65 edges comes in two lengths. Not
forty. Two.**

A room of forty different panels sounds like forty different kinds of
stick, forty different cuts, forty different chances to be wrong. The 2V
geometry quietly removes that nightmare before it starts. Because the frame
is an icosahedron subdivided exactly once, its edges inherit only two
families: the halves of the original icosahedron edges, and the new edges
that connect midpoints. Project them onto the sphere and they stretch, but
they stretch into two values, not forty. Two lengths of edge, in a fixed
ratio of 1.1308 to one — and everything else in the build method
follows from that sentence.

A two-length frame is a frame you can jig. A forty-length frame is a frame
you can only improvise, which is exactly the failure Chapter
3 documented with ten days of labour. This chapter
states the two lengths, shows the four actual cut lengths that fall out of
them, and explains the surprising part: that four is not a mistake and not
a regression. Four is what the pinwheel joint does to two, and the reason
is worth its own section.

## Short and long

The two edge families have names, and the names matter because they travel
all the way to the cut list and the jig.

**Short.** The edge between two midpoints — the new edge the subdivision
created. It spans a smaller arc of the sphere, 31.72 degrees,
and its length is 0.5465 times the sphere's radius.

**Long.** Half of an original icosahedron edge — the older edge, spanning
36.00 degrees, 0.6180 times the radius.

Those two factors are not something a designer chose to keep the saw
settings down. They fall out of the icosahedron's coordinates, and four
independent routes — straight coordinates, central angles, the law of
cosines, and a measured drawing — all land on the same pair, which is the
book's idea of proof for a number this important. At this book's dome, the
two lengths are 72.00 inches and
62.73 inches before the joint takes its bite: a
difference of less than a foot across the whole frame.

The triangle shapes follow the same logic. The hemisphere contains
10 equilateral triangles — three long edges, sitting around
the five-fold points — and 30 isosceles ones, two long edges
over a short base. Two triangle shapes, two edge lengths, one dome. The
forty panels of the previous chapter are forty *copies* of two designs,
not forty designs.

![Every stick in the dome, in four lengths.](../../deliverables/book/figures/member-classes.png)

## The member schedule

The table is the bridge from geometry to lumberyard, and it is generated,
not remembered: every row comes out of the same solved frame the
simulator builds, so the table and the dome cannot disagree.

The headline is the first column of it: the whole frame reduces to
4 cut lengths. The longest member is
72.00 inches; the shortest 62.73.
Between them sit the other two, and the spread across all four is the
width of one hand. That is the entire vocabulary of the build: four
numbers on a board beside the jig, and every stick that passes through the
bench is one of them.

Why four and not two, when the frame has only two edge families? Because
the pinwheel joint insets every member from the vertex it serves, and the
inset depends on which side of the corner a member lands on. A long edge
becomes two slightly different long members — one for each side of the
triangle it borders — and the same for short. The table prints all four,
with their counts, because a cut list that hides the difference produces
forty panels that almost fit, which is the most expensive kind of fit
there is. Chapter 15 is the joint's geometry in full; here
the point is only that two became four *for a reason*, and the reason is
printed.

That four-length schedule is what the model prints. What the members
*measure* — the master cut list, 3 resultant lengths
that make a panel the right size once it is together — is shorter:
72.0, 63.5 and
61.5 inches. The equilateral panels take three of the
long member; the isosceles panels take the long at the base, the middle on
the left and the short on the right. The build's list is the measured one
and the model's is the approximation, and Chapter 24
prints both side by side rather than quietly picking one — a builder needs
the master list at the saw and the seating schedule at the bench.

## Why the pinwheel splits two into four

The step from two lengths to four is the one place a sceptical reader
should slow down, because it looks like the dome is quietly getting more
complicated, and the honest answer is that it is — by design, and the
design buys more than it costs.

A hub dome cuts every strut to end at the vertex, so two edge families
really do mean two stick lengths, and the saw never needs a third setting.
The price is the hub itself: a machined connector at every corner, drilled
to a tolerance, bolted to the end of every strut, and — as Chapter
4 recorded — useless the moment the sticks
stop being factory-identical.

The pinwheel throws the hub away by making every member end land on the
*side* of the next member instead of at the corner. That single decision
is what lets rough, split, slightly irregular timber frame a dome at all:
a butt end on a flat sawn face does not need the precision a hub demands.
But it has a geometric consequence, because a member that stops short of
the corner stops at a different distance on each side of the triangle. A
long edge needs a long-left and a long-right; a short edge needs a
short-left and a short-right. Two becomes four.

So the honest ledger reads: the pinwheel costs one extra saw setting per
edge family, and in exchange it deletes the hub, the mitre on every end,
and the requirement that rough timber behave like milled stock. Four cut
lengths instead of two is not the dome getting complicated. It is the
entire bill for the joint that makes the method possible — and it is a
bill paid once, on a jig, where a fence and a stop make the four lengths
as repeatable as two. The next chapter spends that simplicity on the
question of what a stick of this shape can actually carry.

---

# 10. Compression, Tension, and One Eighth of a Tree

*What a wedge strut can actually carry*

## One Eighth of a Tree

The last three chapters built the case for the wedge as a shape: a
triangulated frame loads its members along their length, and along their
length all that matters is how much wood is in the section. This chapter
turns the case into numbers — and then, because a book is not an engineer,
hands the rest of the question to one.

The unit of the whole method is one eighth of a tree: a 45-degree
sector of the trunk, 3.83 inches across its bark face,
5.00 inches from pith to bark, 9.82 square
inches of wood. That stick will be pushed and pulled along its grain for
the life of the building, and its ends will press sideways into the sides
of its neighbours. Those are the two load directions this chapter cares
about — along the grain, and across it at the joint — and they behave so
differently that the entire honesty of the method depends on keeping them
apart.

The chapter ends with the one number the book will actually give you, and
the reason it will not give you the number you probably wanted. The two are
related: the book can compute geometry, and geometry is what a wedge's
bearing area is. What a wedge's wood can *take* is a property of your
tree's species, grade and moisture, and any book that prints that number
for you is guessing about your tree while pretending to know it. This one
does not.

## Along the grain, and across it

Wood is not one material. It is two, depending on direction, and the
difference is roughly an order of magnitude.

**Along the grain** — end to end, the direction the tree grew — wood is
the material engineers mean when they say pine is strong. A stick squeezed
along its length carries load through the fibres themselves, every one of
them sharing. A stick pulled along its length is even stronger: the fibres
take tension along their length the way rope does. This is the direction a
triangulated frame uses, which is why Chapter 6 could say
that shape does not matter and mean it: for push and pull along the
member, the section's area is the count that matters, and the wedge has
9.82 square inches of it.

**Across the grain** — sideways, pressing into the flat of the wood — the
same stick is a different material. Load the side of a pine stick and the
fibres are asked to squash between their neighbours; they yield far
sooner, they dent rather than break, and the load the joint may carry is a
fraction of what the same member carries along its length. Every wooden
building lives with this fact; it is why a post stands on a plate instead
of a point, and why bolts through timber are spread out rather than
clustered.

The wedge frame meets the problem head-on, and the design decision is in
the next section. Here the point is the discipline: this book's claims
about what a wedge can carry are claims about the *along-grain* direction
— the direction the frame actually loads — and its numbers at the joint
are numbers about *area*, which is geometry and computable, not about
permissible pressure, which is not. The safety page further down exists
because the two are easy to swap, and swapping them is how dome books get
people hurt.

## The end-to-side bearing

The pinwheel joint's load path is worth stating precisely, because its
elegance is real and its limits are real, and both live in the same
sentence.

Every member's butt end — sawn square, once, on the jig — lands on the
flat sawn face of its neighbour. The contact patch is the end of the
stick, and the end of a sector is the sector itself: 9.82
square inches, nothing notched away, nothing reduced to a tongue or a
dowel. A conventional joint would cut away wood to make the connection and
then bolt what is left; the pinwheel does the opposite. The whole section
bears, because the whole section is what the log produced, and the joint
gets its strength from refusing to throw any of it away.

The bearing patch, stated as geometry: the butt end is a sector of a
