10.0-inch circle — the depth of the member, doubled —
cut at 45.0 degrees, which is the same sector shape
the tree was split into.

![The bearing, at a seam: one member's butt on the next member's side.](../../deliverables/book/figures/force-bearing.png)

So the book's honest number for the joint is
9.82 square inches of end grain against side grain, per
member, at every interior corner. The frame has 120 members,
each with one loaded butt, and the arithmetic ends there, because the next
number — how many pounds that patch may carry — belongs to your tree, not
to this book.

What the book *can* say, and does: the bearing is generous by the
standards of light framing, the load spreads across the full width of the
sector rather than a bolt row, and the key between the members carries
the seam's alignment while the wood carries the building. The chapter on
the pinwheel (Chapter 15) and the one on the connector
(Chapter 17) show how that division of labour is built. This
chapter's job was the load path, and it is this: along the grain through
the members, across the grain at the butt, area the whole way.

## What this book will not tell you

This page is a warning, and it is not decorative.

This book will not tell you what your tree's wood is allowed to carry.
Allowable stresses depend on species, grade, moisture content, and the
direction of load; they come from published tables tied to specific
species grown in specific regions, and they are applied by someone who can
look at the actual timber. A pine from a plantation is not a pine from a
windbreak, a knot at a bearing face is not a knot mid-span, and green wood
carries less than dry wood while it dries. None of that can be looked up
by a book that has never seen your log.

It follows that nothing in this book is a structural sign-off. The frame
described here is a prototype method, demonstrated by a build, not an
engineered standard. Where a load figure matters — a floating dome hung
from trees, a floor hung from a mast, a roof carrying snow — the book
names the number as an engineer's job and leaves it there. Chapter
11 repeats this in its accounting of the costs nobody puts
in a dome book, and when this project forgot its own rule -- printing a
load-ish claim it could not stand behind -- it said so in public, on the
page rather than in a footnote.

The rule, once, plainly: **compute the geometry, check the code, and ask
an engineer about the loads.** Everything this book computes is geometry.
Everything an engineer decides is the rest.

## The number I will give you

One sidebar, one number, so the distinction above has a concrete object.

The bearing area of one butt end against one side: 9.82
square inches. That is geometry — the sector's area, computed from the
tree's own taper — and the book will stand behind it, because it is the
same arithmetic that produced the cut list, and it can be checked with a
ruler on the stick in your hand.

What that patch is *allowed* to carry, in pounds per square inch, is a
property of the species, the grade and the moisture — a table entry, not
a computation — and this book will not print one. It will tell you which
direction to look (across the grain, at the butt, at the wet case), and
it will tell you that the frame's own redundancy is the reason the method
tolerates being built from timber nobody graded: 40 panels
mean a member can be watched, and a member can be swapped, which is how
the frame was kept honest through its first year.

The book gives you the geometry and the method. The engineer gives you the
stamp. Neither one can do the other's job, and this book is built so that
the boundary between them is a page you cannot miss.

---

# 11. What a Dome Costs You

*The chapter that argues against the book*

## What a Dome Costs You

Every book about a building method owes its reader one chapter that tries
to talk them out of it. This is that chapter. It is not here for balance;
it is here because the costs it lists are real, they arrive late, and
they are the ones the rest of the dome literature leaves out — which is
exactly how people end up surprised by them.

The argument of this book is that a wedge dome is a remarkable way to get
a strong, cheap, buildable frame out of 3 trees. The argument of this
chapter is that a frame is not a house, and between the two sit three
costs the frame does nothing about: the room is round, the roof is many
angles, and the world of permits and appraisals expects a rectangle. Each
one is priced in time and money, each one is worse than the same problem
in a box, and none of them is fatal. They are the admission price of the
shape, and the honest builder pays it knowingly or pays it twice.

The chapter ends with the cases where the rectangle wins outright, because
those exist and the book's method is not a religion. If you read this
chapter and still want the dome, the rest of the book is yours. If you
read it and do not, the book has just saved you a year.

## Furniture does not fit a curve

The first cost shows up on moving day, and it never fully leaves.

A wall that curves is a wall that refuses the rectangle. Beds, dressers,
bookshelves, kitchen cabinets, a sofa — the furniture economy is built on
straight walls and ninety-degree corners, and against a dome's wall every
straight piece leaves a wedge of dead air behind it. That dead wedge is
real floor you paid for and cannot use, in every direction, at standing
height. The honest floor number for a dome is smaller than the plan-area
number, and Chapter 43 computes the standing-height map so
the loss is visible before you build rather than after you move in.

The fixes are real and none of them is free. A pony wall under the dome
buys back vertical wall at the edge — Chapter 57 prices it,
and it is the cheapest square footage in the building — but it is still a
cost the box never pays. Built-in storage shaped to the curve costs
custom work. Loose furniture loses its footprint to the geometry, piece
by piece. And the centre of a round room, where the headroom is highest,
is also where the room most wants its furniture to be — which is how a
round room ends up furnished as a ring around an empty middle, with the
good space in the middle spent on walking.

None of this is hidden. It is just not in the frame's price, and a frame's
price is what dome books quote. Count the round-room tax as real: a
fraction of the floor, a custom-furniture bill, or both, from the first
night you sleep in it.

## Roofing a compound surface

The second cost is the one that decides whether the dome survives its
third winter, and it is the reason dome builders talk about skin more than
they talk about anything else.

A dome's roof is one surface made of many flat panels meeting at
55 interior seams, every one of them a line where water can
get in. A shingled rectangle sheds water downhill in sheets; a dome sheds
it down forty facets and fifty-five valleys, and the valleys are joints.
The frame chapters show the seam doing its job with a key and a gasket,
and the weather chapter (Chapter 42) says the
number out loud — 55 of them — because the honest way to
think about a dome's roof is not "one roof" but "55 small
roofs, all meeting".

What that costs: the seam system is not optional and not trivial. The key
must fit its fold angle, the gasket must take up the tolerance, and the
whole assembly must still shed water after the wood has moved through a
year of drying. The skin options are priced in Chapter
40, and the pattern across them is the same — the
cheap skin costs more later, and the good skin costs now. A rectangle
with standing-seam metal and two valleys is a solved problem with a
hundred years of practice behind it; a compound surface is a solved
problem only if you do the work this book spends its middle chapters
describing. The roof is not where the dome is weak. It is where the dome
is *particular*, and particular costs attention, which costs time, which
is the currency a two-week frame is supposed to save.

## Permits, appraisals and resale

The third cost never touches the building and still moves the price.

Permits assume a rectangle. The building department's checklists, the
prescriptive code tables, the inspector's mental library — all of them
are written around conventional framing, and a hubless dome of split
wedges is not in any of them. That does not make it unpermittable; it
makes it *unusual*, and unusual means the plan review takes longer, the
official may want an engineer's letter (Chapter 10 tells you
what the engineer's letter does and does not cover), and the fee schedule
may have no line for you. Somewhere, the system will charge for being
surprised.

Appraisals assume a comparable. An appraiser prices a house by finding
three similar recent sales; a dome has no comparables, so the value comes
out of the method instead of the market, and the number is whatever the
method defends. That does not make the dome worth less — it makes its
worth *harder to finance*, and financing difficulty is a real cost for
anyone who is not paying cash.

Resale is where the two meet. The buyer's lender wants the same appraisal
the seller's did, the buyer's inspector has never seen a wedge, and the
pool of buyers who want a round house is smaller than the pool who want a
rectangular one. The network chapters in Part Two (Chapter
67 especially) exist because this project's answer to
resale is to make the dome *movable* — value it as a building that can
leave its pad — but that is a proposal, not a market, and the chapter
says so. The honest line for this chapter: a dome you build yourself and
keep is not exposed to any of this. A dome you build hoping to sell like
a house is exposed to all of it.

## When to build a rectangle

The closing test, stated as plainly as the method allows.

Build a rectangle when the building is a shed, a garage, a workshop, or a
storage room — anything where the square shape, the standard sheet goods,
the off-the-shelf door and the appraiser's familiarity are worth more
than the dome's savings. The catalogue this project prices shows the
border: the cheapest and dearest per-square-foot numbers in it are
7 and 68 dollars, and the box shed it
costs beside the dome shed comes out at 12,426 dollars
against 9,444 — close enough that the shed decision is
about doors and shelving, not about the frame. Build a rectangle when the
site, the code, or the lender is already fighting you, and the fight
would eat the savings. Build a rectangle when you need the work done by a
crew whose language is studs and trusses, not jigs and seams.

Build the dome when the floor is round and wants to be, when the frame
must be cheap enough to build twice, when the tree is already down, or
when the building is a home and the home is yours. The finished-home
comparison in the same catalogue — 84,983 dollars against
87,378 — shows the dome's frame advantage persisting into
the finished house, and Chapter 45 takes that number apart
line by line, including the lines that do not help.

The rectangle is a fine building. It is the right building in most cases.
This book exists for the cases where it is not, and the method it
describes is the strongest version of the alternative — measured against
the rectangle's own numbers, printed here, in the chapter that was
supposed to talk you out of it.

---

# 12. Split Like a Cake

*Three passes, eight sectors, and why eight*

## Split Like a Cake

This is the chapter with the actual cut in it — the one motion the whole
method hangs on, reduced to a procedure you could hand to somebody at the
log.

The idea was introduced in Chapter 5 as a discovery. Here it
is a technique. A round section of trunk, bucked to
6 feet, gets split along its length into
8 equal sectors — like a cake — by seven cuts, all of them
straight rips through the pith. Every stick that comes off is the same
shape: a 45-degree wedge, 3.83 inches
across its bark face, 5.00 inches deep from point to bark,
9.82 square inches of section. There is no squaring, no
planing, no second machine. The saw that felled the tree makes the
members.

The rest of the chapter is the order of the cuts, the reason the order
matters, and the reason it is eight and not six or twelve. The chapter is
short because the operation is. That is the point, and it is worth
remembering when the pile is knee-deep: the method's entire conversion
step is seven cuts per section, and each cut is a straight line.

## The three passes

The procedure, in order. The order is not tradition; it is what keeps the
section stable while you cut it, and a cut made out of order costs you a
pinched bar and a ruined pass.

**Pass one: halve it through the pith.** Stand the section on end or lay
it in a cradle, aim the bar down the centre of the end grain, and rip
from one end to the other. You now have two halves, each with one flat
sawn face and one round bark face. Do this pass first, every time,
because it is the only cut in the whole sequence where the section is
still round and wants to roll; once it is halved, every piece has a flat
to sit on, and everything after is stable.

**Passes two and three: quarter each half.** Lay each half on its flat
face and rip it into two quarters, again through the pith line. Four
quarters, each 45 times two degrees wide, each
with one flat and one round face still on it. The flat sits on the
cradle; the saw follows the centre line; nothing moves.

**Passes four through seven: eighths.** Each quarter gets one more rip,
from the remaining flat face through to the bark, and becomes two
eighths — the final sector. Seven cuts in total: one halving cut, two
quartering cuts, four eighth cuts. 8 sticks, all the same
sector, from a section of trunk that no longer exists.

Two things make the sequence forgiving rather than fussy. First, every
cut after the first is made on a piece that already has a flat face to
sit on, so the workholding is the piece itself. Second, you are never
measuring an angle: the cut list is "through the middle", repeated, and
the tree supplied the symmetry. A sector is 45
degrees because a circle divided in half three times is; nobody held a
protractor to it.

![The order of the passes. Halve, quarter, eighth.](../../deliverables/book/figures/split-sequence.png)

## Three cuts, eight sticks

The drawing shows the sequence end-on: one circle, then two halves, then
four quarters, then eight sectors, with every cut a diameter. Read it as
a production plan rather than a diagram, because the sequence is what
makes the work fast.

The first observation is that every pass doubles the number of pieces —
one becomes two becomes four becomes 8. The second is
that the work per piece falls as the pieces multiply: the halving cut is
the whole width of the log, the quartering cuts are half that each, the
eighth cuts a quarter of the log's width each. The third is that the
sequence never asks a decision. Nothing in it requires choosing where the
next stick comes from or how thick it should be; the geometry decided all
of that when the log was round. A procedure with no decisions is a
procedure you can repeat forty times without drifting, and a procedure
that doubles its output per pass is a procedure whose rhythm pays you as
you go.

Chapter 29 puts the sequence on a clock —
5.0 sticks an hour, sustained, once the cradle and
the rhythm are settled — and Chapter 33 shows
what happens to the pile after. Here the sequence stands alone, because
it is the one thing in the method that cannot be substituted: no mill,
no planer, no router table will produce this member, because the member
*is* the cut.

## One log, two, four, eight

The production hierarchy deserves its own paragraph, because it is the
part of the method that looks obvious and is quietly load-bearing.

One log becomes two halves becomes four quarters becomes
8 members. Every major cut doubles the piece count, which
means the whole tree converts in three layers of work: one cut at the
top of the tree, two in the middle, four at the bottom. That shape —
wide at the bottom, narrow at the top — is exactly what one person with
one saw can exploit. The slow cuts are few and wide; the fast cuts are
many and shallow. And because the pieces at each layer are identical to
their siblings, the workholding at each layer is one cradle, one stop,
one posture, repeated.

This is also the shape a guide rail, a cradle or a splitting fixture
would be built around, if you ever stop doing it freehand — the geometry
already knows where every cut goes, so a fixture that knows it too is
just the sequence, made of steel. The book keeps the hand version,
because the hand version is the whole point: the method exists so that a
person with a chainsaw and 3 trees needs nothing else.

## Why eight and not six or twelve

The question gets asked, usually at the first split pile: why eight?
The answer has three parts, and each one is a hard constraint.

**The sector angle has to leave the seam key workable.** A sector is
45 degrees, and the seam between two panels folds
by 18.03 to 22.46 degrees depending on where
it sits — Chapter 16 prints the full schedule. The gap the key
has to fill is the difference between the sector angle and the fold, and
at eight splits that difference is a real, makable, compressible thing.
Split the log into six and the sector is sixty degrees, the key becomes
a structural wedge in its own right, and the seam starts deciding the
geometry instead of the other way round. Split it into twelve and the
sector is thirty degrees — a sliver, hard to handle and hard to keep
straight over six feet.

**A sixth is too fat to handle alone.** Six sectors from a
12-inch butt are heavy, wide pieces with a
sixty-degree point; they are awkward to cradle, awkward to carry, and
awkward to fasten. Eight lands the member at 3.83 inches
across and 9.82 square inches of section — the size a
single person moves, lifts and sets all day, which is the constraint
Chapter 28 keeps coming back to.

**Eight is the halving count.** Two, four, eight — the sequence above
only needs cuts "through the middle", never a measured angle. Six needs
a sixty-degree first cut, held by eye or by fixture; twelve needs a
thirty-degree refinement. Eight is the finest split you can reach with
nothing but halving, and halving is the only cut a saw can aim by
symmetry alone.

The table on the next page runs the alternatives side by side so the
choice is visible rather than asserted.

![Sector width and depth against split count.](../../deliverables/book/figures/split-counts.png)

## What other split counts give you

The table is the arithmetic of the choice: six, eight, ten, twelve,
each with its sector width, depth and section. Eight is the reference
row, and the reason is not that eight is magic — it is that eight is the
row where the key fits, the stick is carryable, and the cut list is
still "halve it". Every other row trades one of those three away, and
the table makes you look at which one.

One more note for the pile, because it will come up: the split count is
a *build decision*, set before the first section is opened, and it
travels. The member width it produces sets the pinwheel inset, which
sets the cut list, which sets the jig. So the decision is made once,
from this table, and every number downstream follows. Change it later
and you are not adjusting a cut; you are starting the frame again. So
the table gets its own page, and the choice gets its own warning: read
the row, then commit.

---

# 13. Eighty-Seven Percent

*The recovery arithmetic, with the kerf paid for*

> **This chapter corrects something.** The 88% recovery figure in the project brief, which was computed for a 15-inch butt rather than this book's 12-inch tree.

## Eighty-Seven Percent

The headline number of the whole method, stated once with its caveats
attached, because the caveats are where the number lives:

Split the way this book splits, and a trunk keeps 87.4
per cent of its solid wood as structural members. Sawn the way a mill
saws, the same trunk keeps about 45. That is
1.93 times the usable wood, from a cheaper process,
with one machine instead of four.

The caveats, in the same breath: the 87.4 counts
*split wood only* — nothing planed, nothing dried, nothing graded; the
sawn comparison is the honest one for two-by-fours packed in a circle,
which is flattering to neither side; and the famous "88 per cent" from
this project's earlier films belongs to a bigger tree than this book's.
This book's tree is 12 inches at the butt and
keeps 87.4 per cent; the films' tree is
15 inches and keeps 88.4. Both numbers
are correct, for different trees, and the last page of this chapter
prints both rather than quietly picking the flattering one.

The rest of the chapter is the arithmetic, so the headline stops being a
claim and becomes a thing you could re-derive with the saw's kerf in
your hand.

## What a mill loses, and to what

A sawmill's job is to turn a round log into rectangular boards, and
everything it loses is the price of the rectangle.

**The slabs.** A circle does not contain its rectangle. The two rounded
sides of the log — the slab wood — cannot become boards of any width,
so they come off first, and they are the biggest single loss. On a
small log they are a huge fraction of the circle; the fatter the log,
the smaller the fraction, which is why mills pay per board foot and
prefer the big trees.

**The edgings.** Even the boards cut from the heart of the log carry
wany edges — bark, roundness — and must be edged square. More wood off.

**The trim.** Boards are sold in even lengths, square at both ends.
Every board loses its trim.

**The kerf.** Every cut turns wood into sawdust. A mill's band or
circle blade is thinner than a chainsaw's, but a mill makes far more
cuts per log, and the kerf is paid on every one.

**Drying degrade.** The boards are stickered, stacked and dried, and a
fraction of them check, cup, twist or stain past their grade. The wood
was there; the board is not.

**The grade fall.** Of what remains, some boards grade lower than the
rest, and low-grade lumber sells for less — a loss in value even when
nothing is lost in volume.

Add the five together and the famous range appears: a log sawn into
two-by-fours keeps roughly half of itself as saleable lumber. This
book's tree, packed honestly with true two-by-fours, keeps
45 per cent. None of that is incompetence. The
mill is extremely good at a job whose product is a rectangle, and the
waste is the price of the rectangle — paid in wood, at every station of
the chain Chapter 2 walked.

## What the wedge loses

Now convert the same trunk the wedge way, and count the losses again.

**The bark.** Lost either way; it was never wood. Not counted against
either method.

**The kerf — and that is nearly all.** The wedge makes seven cuts per
section, through the full diameter. Each cut is a 0.25-inch
kerf — a chainsaw's kerf, fatter than a mill's blade — and across
8 sections the kerf bill comes to 40
board feet of the trunk's 318. That is the honest, paid
price: 40 board feet of sawdust.

**Nothing else.** No slab, because the sector uses the whole radius.
No edging, because nothing needs a square edge. No trim beyond the
bucking. No drying degrade to speak of — a wedge is allowed to move as
it dries, and Chapter 48 explains why
a split moves less than a board. No grade fall, because the book grades
its own pile by eye and uses the rejects for blocking.

So the ledger closes: 318 board feet in the trunk, minus
40 of kerf, leaves 278 board feet of
structural member — 87.4 per cent — at
4.35 board feet a stick, which is what the worked
build in Chapter 24 turns into a cut list. The wedge
does not beat the mill at milling. It beats the mill at *this* — making
a structural member for a frame that never asked for rectangles — and
the beat is the difference between 87.4 and
45 per cent of the same tree.

![The same log, converted twice. 87.4 percent against 45.](../../deliverables/book/figures/recovery-compare.png)

## Both conversions, same log

The drawing shows the same round section twice. On the left, the mill's
version: rectangles packed into the circle, slabs and edgings falling
away to the outside, the honest count of what a two-by-four economy
keeps. On the right, the wedge's version: the circle divided into
8 equal sectors, and every sector kept whole.

The picture makes the argument the numbers cannot. The mill's losses
are the *outside* of the circle — the part farthest from any rectangle.
The wedge's only loss is the thin lines of the kerf itself, running
through the middle. The mill throws away the corners; the wedge keeps
the corners and throws away the cut lines. The corners of a round log
are not bad wood. They are perfectly good wood of the wrong shape, and
"wrong shape" is a judgement the rectangle made, not the tree. Chapter
6 is the reason the frame is allowed to disagree, and
this drawing is the disagreement, priced in board feet.

## Section by section

The table carries the comparison down the whole trunk, one bucked
section per row, both conversions, because a taper means the answer is
not one number but a run of them — and the run is what you actually
cut.

Each 6-foot section of the trunk is a slightly
smaller circle than the one before it. The butt section packs more
rectangles and yields fatter sectors; the top section packs fewer and
yields slimmer ones. The table shows both columns shrinking as the
taper runs, and the recovery staying where the geometry put it: the
wedge's advantage is not a lucky average, it is a property that holds
section by section, from the 12-inch butt to the
8-inch top. Read it as a check on the headline
rather than as a second claim: if any row disagreed with the story
above, the story would be the thing to fix.

## Where 88 became 87

The errata page, because this project said "88 per cent" on camera and
this book prints 87.4, and a reader who notices the
gap deserves the explanation rather than the silent edit.

The films' recovery figure — 88.4 per cent — was
computed for the films' reference tree: 15 inches at the
butt, 60 feet usable, the big plantation pine of the
pine-value film. A fatter log puts more wood inside its circle and less
into its slabs, so the same split-and-kerf arithmetic keeps more of it:
88.4 per cent.

This book's tree is smaller — 12 inches at the
butt, 48 feet usable, the windfall of Chapter
1 — and the same arithmetic, run on
the smaller circle, keeps 87.4 per cent. The kerf is
the same 0.25-inch chain; the circle it eats is simply a
smaller fraction of a smaller pie.

Neither number is wrong, and neither was ever a lie. They are two
recoveries for two different trees, and the earlier films named their tree while
this book named a different one. The book's discipline — stated on this page, and kept on every errata
page like it — is that a correction names
the error, prints both numbers, and leaves the arithmetic visible. That
is what this page is: 88.4 for the pine the films
priced, 87.4 for the tree this book builds, and the
reason they differ is the diameter, printed in the table above, one row
per section.

---

# 14. Four Ways Up

*One stick, four orientations, four different buildings*

## Four Ways Up

This chapter is the most original idea in the method, and it is the one
nobody sees coming, because it concerns a property most building methods
do not have: the stick has a front.

A two-by-four is symmetric enough that nobody asks which way up it goes.
A wedge is not. It has a point and a curved bark back, two flat sawn
faces meeting at a 45-degree angle, and it is
never the same stick twice. Which way its point aims — into the room,
out to the weather, along the seam — changes what the frame does with
it, without changing a single dimension. One stick, cut once, can be
four different members, and the four buildings that result are four
different answers to the same geometry.

The chapter walks the four orientations and ends with the table that
chooses between them, read straight from the simulator's own seam
readings. The default — the one the rest of the book builds in — is
named and defended. The other three are not mistakes; they are the
method's spare capacity, and each one buys something specific at a
specific price.

## Rotating about its own axis

Hold the wedge out in front of you, point up, bark toward your chest.
Rotate it about its own length — the axis from butt to head. At every
quarter turn it presents a different face to the room and a different
edge to the weather, and nothing about its cut list changes. That
freedom is the whole idea: the frame's geometry cares where the
stick's *surfaces* sit, not where the stick's *shape* came from, so the
builder gets to choose the surface, free.

The four orientations come from the two decisions the rotation makes.
First, where does the point aim — toward the centre of the dome or
toward the centre of its own panel? Second, do the two sticks flanking
a seam aim their points together or apart? Point *dome in* means every
wedge points at the middle of the building, bark to the weather. Point
*panel in* means each stick aims into its own triangle's middle, the
pair beside a seam pointing apart. Point *dome out* turns every wedge
backward, bark to the room, point at the sky. Point *panel out* turns
the pair toward each other across the seam. Four ways up, same stick.

What changes between them is real, and it is measurable: the two sawn
faces open by a different angle, the key has to be a different shape,
and the key's own base moves from 2.80 inches across to
11.67 in the widest arrangement. The next four
sections take them one at a time; the plate shows all four.

![The same stick, four ways up.](../../deliverables/book/figures/orientations-four.png)

## All four, side by side

The plate is the whole chapter in one look, and it is not a sketch: each
pane is a seam **cut across, through the solver's own meshes**, so what
you are looking at is the joint, not an illustration of it. Two members,
one from each panel, and the key in gold between them. In every pane the
weather is up.

Read the panes for the two things that change. First, *which face of the
stick the key bears on*: in the first pane the key sits between the two
narrow sawn faces, a slim spline; in the second and fourth it bears on
the broad sides and becomes a wide shim; in the third the wood is turned
around, so the key is fitted from the inside. Second, *how much key*:
the width printed under each pane is the base of the key as the solver
slices it, and it is not the same number twice.

The four arrangements are not four aesthetic choices. They are four
key-cutting choices and four weather choices, and the table at the end of
the chapter prices them. First, each in its own words.

## Point dome in

The default. Every wedge points at the centre of the building, bark
out, sawn faces lying along the seams. This is the orientation the rest
of the book builds in, for three reasons that stack.

**The key gets flat faces.** A seam's key sits between the two sticks'
sawn faces — the only faces the saw ever made flat — so it bears on
machined wood rather than on bark. At this orientation those two faces
stand 26.97 degrees apart, the key is a narrow taper
2.80 inches across its base, and it bears
6.0 inches along the seam. That is the joint every
chapter before this one has been drawing.

**Bark faces the weather.** The curved back of every wedge is the
building's outside face: one continuous, shed-ready, bark-clad surface.
Chapter 42 builds the rain strategy on exactly
this — water runs down bark, off the seam's shoulder, to the rim.

**The stick tells you which way it goes.** This is the quiet one, and
builders feel it at the top of a ladder. Bark out, pith in: the stick
is self-labelling. Nobody marks "this side out" on a hundred and twenty
members, because the tree already did — the bark is the outside, the
point is the inside, and a wrongly rotated stick announces itself the
moment you try to lay the key against it.

And one geometric detail that turns out to matter at the bench: the key
is **outboard of the two points**. Cut a seam across and the members'
points are the innermost thing in it, with the key sitting in the wedge
of space between them and the meeting line of the two bark faces — so
the key is reached, fitted and checked from *outside* the shell. That is
the practical reason this orientation is the one the book builds in,
and the next one down the page loses it.

The cost of point dome in is the ridged interior: the room's wall is a
field of points. Acceptable in a frame you will sheath; Chapter
43 decides how to live with it.

## Point panel in

Each stick aims its point into the middle of its own triangle, so the
pair beside a seam point *apart* — away from each other, one into each
neighbouring panel's centre.

What it buys: the key moves off the narrow sawn edges and onto the
broad sides of the pair, and it stops being a spline and becomes a
broad shim. Cut the same seam across and the two faces now open
153.03 degrees, with the key
11.67 inches across its base — 4.2
times the width of the default's key. For a seam asked to carry more
than alignment, that is real contact area rather than a line of it, and
the simulator keeps the orientation as a first-class option for exactly
that.

What it costs: the bark faces lean away from the weather and into the
seam's shadow, so the outside surface is no longer a clean field of
bark; the wide key is more timber per seam than the default; and the
self-labelling gets subtler, because "point toward the middle of your
panel" is an instruction you must remember, not one the stick shouts.

## Point dome out

Every wedge reversed: bark to the room, point to the weather. The
building's outside becomes a field of ridges — forty panels of them —
and its inside becomes the smooth curved field of bark.

What it buys is mostly weather. The pointed outside sheds water the way
a ridged roof does, each ridge its own drip line, and the seams sit in
the valleys between ridges instead of on the crests — a configuration
some roof builders prefer on principle, because the joint lives where
the water is already heading.

What it costs is the thing the previous section praised most. The key
still sits between the two sawn faces — the geometry does not change
what the wood is — but the *meeting line* moves to the other side: the
bark faces now converge on the inside of the shell, the key's base is
6.27 inches across (against 2.80 in the
default) with the faces 63.03 degrees apart, and the
key now lies *inboard* of the two points. Fitted from the room side, not
from the weather side. Everything about the joint still works; the
person fitting it is now indoors, on a ladder, in a small round room,
which is a worse place to be laying a key than the outside of a roof.
And the self-labelling inverts: pith out, bark in, which reads wrong to
every instinct a woodworker has.

Point dome out is the orientation to remember exists and not choose —
unless the building's skin is a ridged shingle system designed for it,
which is a real design and not this book's.

## Point panel out

The fourth rotation: the pair beside a seam aim their points *at each
other, across the seam* — each stick's point reaching over the joint
toward its neighbour's back, bark faces turning in toward the panel's
middles.

What it buys: the room side of every panel is a field of the flat sawn
faces — the smoothest interior the four orientations offer — and the
key is broad, like the panel-in orientation:
11.67 inches across its base with the faces
153.03 degrees apart. What it costs: the pair's
points are toward each other across the seam, so the weather face of the
building is partly sawn face — the face the finish chapters warn you
never to leave exposed — and the two sticks' broad faces, not their
edges, are what the key bears against, which is more timber per seam
than any other orientation. Chapter
48
returns to this when it discusses char and oil, because the one
orientation that puts sawn face outdoors is the one this book does not
build.

## Choosing one

The table is the decision, and it is read straight from the simulator's
own seam readings rather than from adjectives. Each row: one
orientation, its contact geometry, its weather face, its inside face,
and the verdict the solved model gives it.

The row that matters is the default's, and the reason it wins is not
that it is perfect — it is that it is the only orientation where every
job lands on the face built for it. The key gets the sawn faces. The
weather gets the bark. The room gets the points it can sheath over.
And the stick labels itself, so the hundred and twentieth member is
placed as correctly as the first.

The other three rows are the method's spare capacity: point panel in
when the seam must be stiffer, point dome out when the skin is designed
for ridges, point panel out almost never. The table keeps all four
because a choice made from the table is a decision, and a choice made
from habit is a gamble — and the orientation, once the first panel is
built, is the one decision in the method you cannot take back.

---

# 15. The Pinwheel

*Three sticks, one triangle, and not one mitre*

> **This chapter corrects something.** The 'no mitres anywhere' claim, already corrected on camera in lesson_wedge_why.

## The Pinwheel

This is the joint that makes the whole method buildable by one person,
and it is worth stating in one sentence before the geometry spends it:

**No member end ever meets another member end. Every member's butt end
lands on the flat side of the next member, one cut per stick, and the
corner is empty.**

That sentence deletes, in order: the hub, the mitre at every end, the
compound-angle saw settings, and the requirement that rough split
timber behave like milled stock. What it costs — and it does cost — is
a slightly smaller panel than the triangle it sits in, and a cut list
four lengths long instead of two. Chapter 9
already counted that bill. This chapter shows the joint that issues
it.

The name is descriptive. Look at a corner of the built panel: the three
members overlap like the blades of a pinwheel, each butt end lying on
its neighbour's side, chasing each other around the triangle, none of
them meeting at the vertex. The vertex — where a hubbed dome puts its
connector — is empty air. The frame does not need anything there,
because the triangle is closed by the three overlaps, and the overlaps
are held by screws through flat wood into flat wood.

## Why mitres were the enemy

The conventional way to join three struts at a corner is to make them
meet: each end cut to the right compound angle so the three ends kiss
at a point, then a hub or a plate holds the kiss together. It is the
geometry of every geodesic kit ever sold, and for machined aluminium
struts in a factory it is fine.

For this method it is fatal, for three reasons that compound.

**A mitre is two angles at once.** The end of a dome strut has to
match both the panel's fold and the member's position around the
corner — a mitre and a bevel together. Cut one slightly wrong and the
struts no longer meet; cut forty panels' worth slightly differently
wrong and the dome has a gap for every mood.

**Every stick needs two of them.** Both ends of every member would be
mitred — 120 members, two ends each, each end its own
setting — against a stick that is rough, tapered, green and moving.
Precision joinery on wood that never agreed to be precise is not a
building method; it is a bet against the material.

**The corner has to be perfect or nothing is.** When three ends meet
at a point, all three must arrive exactly, or the hub cannot close. The
tolerance is concentrated in one place, which is the worst place for a
frame built from a log: every error in the whole build queues up at
the vertex and waits.

The pinwheel sidesteps all three by moving the joint. The ends do not
meet, so they do not have to match. Each end lands on a flat side, and
a butt end on a flat side needs only to be square to its own length —
one cut, made once, on a jig. The overlap is where the tolerance lives,
and an overlap is a joint that can be off by half an inch and still
hold, because the screws have a whole side of wood to find.

## Each end lands on a side, not a point

The pinwheel, in the way that finally makes it obvious, because the
obvious version is what you will teach somebody at the bench.

Take three sticks. Lay the first two in the triangle's shape, and
instead of cutting them to meet at the corner, let each one run *past*
the corner along the side of the other. Now the first stick's end rests
on the second stick's side, not its end. Lay the third stick the same
way: its end rests on the first's side, and the second's end rests on
the third's. Three ends, three sides, one closed triangle, and the
actual corner of the triangle — the point where the lines would meet —
has nothing in it at all.

That is the whole joint. Every member is cut square on its butt end and
left long on its head end, and the overlap is resolved at assembly.
The result reads as a pinwheel because each stick's butt chases the
next stick's side around the corner, and the chase closes the triangle
without any stick ever touching the vertex.

Why this survives rough timber: the bearing patch is a full side of a
stick — 9.82 square inches of flat-to-flat contact,
as Chapter 10 measured — and the screws run through the
overlap at right angles to the grain, where wood holds best. Nothing
in the joint depends on an angle being cut accurately, because the
only cut angle in the whole assembly is the square butt, and the
square butt is the one cut a jig can make identical forty times.

![Three members, three end-to-side joints, no mitre anywhere.](../../deliverables/book/figures/pinwheel-exploded.png)

## One panel, taken apart

The drawing explodes one panel and labels the three overlaps, so the
sentence above has a picture to check against.

**The butt end.** Square to the stick's length, the one machined cut
on the member. It is made on the jig, once, with the stick located on
its sawn faces — Chapter 34 is that cut in full.

**The bearing side.** The neighbour's sawn face, where the butt lands.
Flat, because the saw made it flat, and wide, because the whole sector
is wide.

**The head end.** Deliberately long. It runs past its own bearing by
the overfit allowance — 6 inches in this build —
and gets sawn off in place against the jig's fence, which is how the
panel ends up true without anybody measuring a stick to a tape. Chapter
35 is that trick, and it is the quiet reason the
method forgives.

**The empty corner.** The vertex itself, untouched. In a hubbed dome
this is where the money is — the machined connector, the bolt, the
precision. Here it is air, and the air is doing its job by not being
asked to do anything.

Read the exploded drawing once and the build order falls out of it:
butt cuts first, on the jig; panels assembled flat, overlaps screwed;
heads flushed; panel lifted. Nothing met anything. Nothing had to.

## What the inset costs

The honest price of the pinwheel, because the joint's elegance is real
and its bill is real, and the book prints both.

The triangle's *edge* — the geometric line from vertex to vertex — is
longer than the member that fills it, because the member stops short of
the corner to land on its neighbour's side. The difference is the
inset: the distance each stick pulls back from the vertex so its butt
lands on the next stick's side instead of on the next stick's point.
Every member length in the cut list is the edge length minus its share
of the inset, which is why the cut list has four lengths where the
geometry has two — the inset differs between the long and short edges,
and between the two sides of each.

The inset also shrinks the panel. The frame's triangles, drawn vertex
to vertex, are bigger than the wood that fills them; the panel is the
triangle minus three strips around its edge. That strip is not waste —
it is the bearing, the overlap, the screw row — but it is real, and it
is why the member schedule in Chapter 24 is the
number it is rather than the bare chord factors.

And the inset scales with the *stick*, not the dome. A wider member
pulls back further; a bigger dome changes nothing. That single fact is
why the method survives scaling — Chapter 51 makes it
the spine of Part Two — and it is paid here, in the cut list, once.

At the bench the inset is not paid in arithmetic at all. It is already in
the members: the master cut list of Chapter 24 —
72.0, 63.5 and
61.5 inches — is what the sticks measure once they have
been cut to make a panel the right size, so the inset is spent at the saw
and never re-derived at the bench. The model's own schedule prints the
inset as a fourth length; the build's list is the measured three, and the
difference between the two is a model of the joint rather than a measurement
of it.

## The mitre claim, corrected

The errata, named in full, because this project said it in public and
the correction belongs in public too.

An earlier film in this project claimed that no cut in the dome is a
mitre. That was wrong, and it was corrected on camera before this book
existed — the film's own correction chapter is the model this page
follows.

The true statement is narrower and better. No member end is ever
mitred *to another member end*, because the pinwheel never lets two
ends meet. But the butt end itself is not a plain square crosscut: it
carries the bevel that matches the neighbour's side and the angle that
matches the panel's fold — a compound cut, made once, on one end, off
the jig, with the same setting for every stick of a class. The whole
dome reduces to a handful of saw settings — the bevel is the same
everywhere, and the mitre takes a few fixed values — and that is the
claim worth making: not "no mitres", but "no mitre is ever measured,
and no two ends ever meet".

The errata pages in this part carry the rest of the list. This page is
one entry in it: the claim, the error, the corrected version, and the arithmetic
that decides which is which — the same treatment the book gives every
number it has ever got wrong.

---

# 16. The Seam and Its Key

*Where two panels meet, and the angle nobody wants to hear about*

## The Seam and Its Key

This is the least glamorous chapter in the book, and it is the one that
decides whether the dome closes.

The frame's 40 panels do not simply sit next to each
other. They meet at 55 interior lines, and at every one
of those lines two flat triangles fold against each other at an angle
that the sphere, not the builder, chose. Between the two members in
each seam sits a key — a strip of wood or compressible material cut to
the fold — and the key is where the whole tolerance budget of the
method finally lands. Get the keys right and the dome closes like a
lid. Get them wrong and forty panels of *nearly* closing is a dome
with a gap in it, which is what Chapter 5 spent a week
learning the hard way.

The chapter has one unwelcome fact to deliver first — the fold angle
is not constant — and then two honest ways to answer it, a table of
every seam in the dome, and the third option that sidesteps the whole
question. It is short, because once the fact is accepted the answers
are small. The week was spent on the accepting.

## The fold angle is not constant

Say it early, because everything after depends on it: **the angle at
which two panels meet is not the same everywhere on the dome.**

It cannot be. A dome is a sphere approximated by flat triangles, and a
sphere curves differently in different places. The panels around the
five-fold points of the underlying icosahedron meet at one angle; the
panels along the belt between them meet at another. A 2V hemisphere has
exactly two fold angles — 18.03 degrees and
22.46 degrees in this build — and that is the good news
hidden inside the bad one. Not forty angles. Not a continuum.
2. The bad news is that 2
is not one, and a key cut to one of them will not seat in the other.

The schedule on the next pages counts them: 25 seams at
the tighter angle, 30 at the wider, 55 in
all. The difference between the two — under five degrees — is small
enough that every instinct says to ignore it, and large enough that
forcing it closed puts the frame in permanent tension, the keys in
permanent shear, and the builder in the position the frankendome
already visited. The honest answers accept the difference and spend a
little labour on it. The dishonest one — "it is nearly the same" —
costs the dome.

And here is the theorem that makes the method's peace with it, because
it deserves the name: the two fold angles and the two leftover gap
angles each sum to exactly the sector the tree was split into —
45 degrees. The gap the key fills beside a
18.03-degree fold is the rest of 45;
beside a 22.46-degree fold it is the rest again. The
sector you split out of the tree is, exactly, the seam budget the dome
needs. Nothing in the method was tuned to make that true; it falls out
of the two geometries and it is why the key problem has an answer at
all.

## Raw trapezoid, or shaved flat

Two honest ways to make the key fit the two folds, and the book prices
both.

**The raw trapezoid.** Leave the split faces of the members exactly as
the saw left them — each sits 22.5 degrees off its
member's centreline — and cut the key to fill the V they make. Cut the
seam across and the two sawn faces stand 26.97 degrees apart,
with the members' points as the innermost wood and the key lying in the
space between them and the meeting line of the two bark faces: a taper
whose base is 2.80 inches across, at the inside, and whose tip
reaches the apex at the outside. It bears 6.0 inches along
the seam. The cost is a key that is not one part but two — one profile
per fold angle — and a stock pile sorted by profile. The gain is that the
member is never touched by a plane, and every piece of the joint stays
exactly what the saw made. The figure below is that section, cut out of
the solver's own meshes.

**The shaved flat.** Plane a narrow land along each member's two sawn
faces until they sit parallel to the seam, then one flat key fits
everywhere — 1.12 inches across as the solver models it,
one key for all 55 seams. The cost is planing —
0.48 inches at the deepest, which is
10 per cent of the member's depth, over a land
only as wide as the key's bearing — and the labour of it, which Chapter
17 works through completely, because the number is smaller
than the instinct expects and "smaller than expected" is exactly the claim
this book verifies before it repeats.

Both answers are correct; they are two ways to spend the same small
labour, and the figure shows both sections side by side so the choice is a
comparison rather than a preference. The book's build uses the shaved-flat
answer, because one key everywhere is one less way for the pile to be
sorted wrong, and because a planed land gives the key a true bearing. The
tapered key remains the answer for a builder with no plane and no patience
for one.

![One seam, closed two ways: the tapered key and the shaved flat, both cut from the solver's meshes.](../../deliverables/book/figures/seam-both-ways.png)

## One seam, both ways

The drawing shows the same seam twice. On the left, the raw split
faces and the tapered key between them — the key's two edges cut to
different slopes, each matching the face it bears on. On the right,
the same two members with a narrow land planed flat on each, and one
parallel-sided key dropped in the gap, the same key that fits every
other seam in the dome.

The drawing is the decision, and the decision is smaller than it looks.
The tapered key moves the work into the key stock — two profiles,
sorted and matched. The shaved land moves the work into the members —
one profile, one plane setup, repeated at every seam. Either way the
work exists; the book simply chooses to pay it where the sorting
cannot go wrong. Chapter 33 shows the fixture that
makes the shaved land repeatable, and the seam schedule below shows
the two angles it is compensating for.

## Every seam in the dome

The table is the whole seam budget on one page: all 55
seams, their fold angles sorted, the two extremes named, and the key
profile each one takes. It is generated from the solved dome, not
surveyed from the build, so it is the plan the frame was drawn to —
and the plan the build should be checked against.

Read it as a work order rather than a curiosity. 25 of
one profile, 30 of the other, and the check that closes
the chapter: when the last panel is in, the number of keys placed
should equal 55, the number of profiles used should equal
2, and the dome should close without being
forced anywhere. If any of those checks fails, the frame is not the
frame this book drew, and Chapter 38 is where
the book tells you where the error went.

## Hose, key or nothing

The third option, and the one that ends the chapter, because sometimes
the honest answer to an angle is a material that does not care about
it.

**The compressible seam.** A gasket — rubber, foam, a length of hose
— fills the gap by deforming instead of by matching. It absorbs the
fold difference, the drying movement, and the build's own tolerance in
one object, and it seals while it does it. What it cannot do is carry
structural load the way a timber key can, which is why the frame's
seams are built with a timber key *plus* the gasket where the shell
must be weathertight — the key locates the geometry, the gasket seals
the gap, and neither is asked to do the other's job.

**And the honest nothing.** The frame, left bare, closes without any
key at all: the butt ends bear directly on the sawn faces, and the
seam is a gap the shell chapters will have to deal with. It is not a
building; it is a stage of one. But it is a legitimate stage, and
building the frame bare first — checking the fold schedule against the
real sticks before any key stock is cut — is the cheapest way to find
the error the table above was designed to prevent.

The key, then, is the method in miniature: a small part, cut to the
two numbers the geometry actually produces, repeated until the pile is
gone, and checked against the count when the dome closes. The least
glamorous chapter in the book. The one that decides whether it
closes.

---

# 17. Let the Connector Hold the Angle

*Keep the wood, machine the small part*



## Let the Connector Hold the Angle

There is a principle underneath this whole method, and it is worth stating on
its own because it is the thing that makes rough natural timber buildable to a
tolerance at all.

> The tree supplies the mass. The jig supplies the repeatability. The
> connector supplies the precision.

Three jobs. Three different things doing them. Nothing asked to do two.

## Precision is expensive per cubic inch

The instinct with an irregular material is to machine it until it is regular.
Plane the faces, square the ends, bring everything to a dimension, and then
build with confidence.

That instinct is why dimensional lumber exists, and inside a mill it is the
right instinct — a mill can afford to hold a tolerance because it is holding
it on thousands of pieces with a machine built for nothing else.

A person with a chainsaw cannot afford it, and the reason is not skill. It is
that **precision costs roughly in proportion to how much material you have to
be precise about**. A long, heavy, awkward member is expensive to hold, to
reference, to feed and to correct. A small block is cheap to hold and cheap to
remake if you get it wrong.

So there is a choice about *where to put the accuracy*, and the two answers
lead to very different afternoons:

![Where the precision lives.](../../deliverables/book/figures/precision-location.png)

One route asks for accuracy 120 times, on the largest and most
awkward pieces in the build, with one chance at each. The other asks for it
2 times, on the smallest pieces, and then repeats the
result 55 times.

Both end with a dome that closes.

This is not a way of avoiding accuracy. Nothing here is less accurate than the
alternative — the seams end up at the same angles either way. It is a question
of *where the accuracy is cheapest to buy*, and the answer is: in the small
part.

## The wedge starts close to useful

There is a piece of luck in this, and it is the reason the wedge suits a dome
rather than merely tolerating one.

A rectangular board arrives with two faces at ninety degrees to each other.
Ninety degrees appears nowhere in a geodesic dome. Every angular relationship
in the frame has to be created from nothing, by cutting.

A split sector does not arrive square. It arrives with two sawn faces that
each sit **22.5 degrees** off the member's own
centreline, because that is half of one 45-degree
sector.

And the seams of this dome fold by 18.03 and
22.46 degrees.

Those are not the same numbers. The wedge does not magically arrive at the
right angle — that would be too good, and this book does not do too good. But
it arrives *within a few degrees* of what the joint wants, rather than
sixty-odd degrees away.

The natural shape of the tree becomes part of the joint system instead of an
obstacle that first has to be removed. That is the difference between a
material that suits a structure and one that merely survives it.

## Two ways to close the difference

The gap between the face you have and the face the joint wants can be closed
from either side.

**Shape the key.** Leave both sawn faces exactly as split and cut a tapered
key that fills whatever is left. No wood is machined at all. The cost is that
the key is not the same shape at every seam, so there is more than one key
profile to make.

**Shape the wood.** Plane both faces parallel and drop in a plain rectangular
key that fits everywhere. One key profile for the whole dome. The cost is a
machining pass on every stick.

Chapter 16 covers what each does to the seam. This chapter is about
what the second one costs, because it sounds much worse than it is.

## What planing the faces actually costs

Here is the arithmetic, and it is the most reassuring table in the book.

The raw split face sits at 22.5 degrees. A flat key wants
a face at half the seam's fold angle. The difference between those two is
everything that has to come off.

![What the shaved-flat option costs.](../../deliverables/book/figures/shaving-cost.png)

Over a two-inch mating land — you do not need the whole face to bear, only a
band of it — the deepest cut anywhere in the dome is
**0.48 inches**.

On a member 5.00 inches deep, that is
10 per cent of the section.

So even the "machine the wood" option does not convert a wedge into something
resembling dimensional lumber. It removes a narrow sliver from a narrow band
and leaves the overwhelming majority of the member exactly as the saw left it.
You are still not making boards. You are making one precise interface on an
otherwise raw stick.

That is worth knowing before you decide, because the shaved-flat route is
often written off as a betrayal of the whole idea. It is not. It is a
10 per cent concession that buys you a single key
profile.

## Or do not machine anything at all

There is a third answer, and in some builds it is the best one: make the
connector out of something that does not need to fit exactly, because it
deforms.

A rubber or closed-cell spline, a length of hose, a compressed gasket — any of
these fills a *range* rather than a value. Feed it a seam anywhere between the
two fold angles and it takes up the difference by squashing.

What a compliant key can do, all at once:

* absorb the angular difference,
* absorb manufacturing variation in the members,
* seal the seam against weather,
* isolate one panel from another acoustically and against vibration,
* hold a preload so the joint stays tight as the wood moves.

What it cannot do is carry the load a timber key carries. A compliant seam is
a weather joint and a tolerance joint; it is not a structural one. So the
choice depends on a question you should answer before you buy anything: **is
this seam holding the building up, or holding the weather out?**

In a frame where each panel is a complete, self-braced triangle — which is
what Chapter 18 is about — a great deal of the shell's stiffness
lives inside the panels rather than across the seams, and that is what makes
the compliant option viable at all.

## Why this is a manufacturing idea, not a dodge

It is worth defending this properly, because "let the connector take up the
difference" can sound like an excuse for sloppy work.

It is the opposite. It is how repeatable production works everywhere.

A machine shop does not hold a tolerance on every surface of every part; it
holds it on the surfaces that locate and mate, and leaves the rest rough
because rough is cheaper and does no harm. An engine block is machined to a
few thousandths where the bearings sit and left as cast everywhere else.
Cabinetmakers scribe one edge to a wall and leave the other three alone.

The principle is always the same: **decide which surfaces do work, and spend
your accuracy there.**

In this dome, the surfaces that do work are the mating faces of the seam and
the ends where one member bears on another's side. Everything else — the bark,
the taper, the natural bend, the exact diameter — can vary, and the jig is
designed so that it can.

The tree is not being asked to be accurate. It is being asked to be strong,
which it already is.

---

# 18. Why Every Panel Keeps Its Own Edge

*One hundred and twenty members for sixty-five edges, and what the difference
buys*



## Every Panel Keeps Its Own Edge

If you have been adding up as you read, something has not been adding up.

## The arithmetic that does not look right

The frame has 40 triangular panels. Each panel has
3 members. So:

> 40 × 3 = **120
> members**

But the shell only has **65 edges**. Count the lines on a
drawing of the dome and that is what you get: 55 interior edges
where two panels meet, plus 10 around the open bottom where
a panel meets nothing.

So there are 55 more members than there are edges for them
to be.

That is not an error. It is the single biggest deliberate decision in this
frame, and it costs **1.85 times** the timber a
conventional geodesic skeleton would use.

It is worth being clear about the size of that, because it is not a rounding
difference. If you built the same dome with one shared strut per edge you
would need 65 members instead of 120. The panelised
frame uses almost twice as much wood in the frame.

Here is why it is worth it anyway.

## What is actually at a seam

In a classical geodesic frame, an edge is one strut, and the two triangles
either side of it both fasten to that same strut. Efficient, and the standard
way it is done.

In this frame, each panel is built complete — three of its own members, closed
into its own triangle — before it goes anywhere near its neighbours. So where
two panels meet there are two members, one belonging to each, with the key
between them.

![One seam, two members.](../../deliverables/book/figures/seam-sandwich.png)

Panel A's member. The key. Panel B's member.

The duplicated edge is not lumber somebody forgot to remove. It is the price
of a panel being a *thing* rather than a position in an assembly.

## What the extra wood buys

Six things, and the first three are why the fortnight in Part 5 is a fortnight
rather than a season.

**A panel can be built flat.** All three of its members exist and belong to
it, so the whole triangle can be assembled on a bench, at waist height, on the
ground, in the dry. Nothing about building it requires any other panel to
exist yet. In a shared-strut frame you cannot finish a triangle without the
struts its neighbours also use, which is why those domes tend to get assembled
in the air, strut by strut, at the top of a ladder.

**A panel can be checked before it goes up.** It has a measurable shape while
it is still somewhere you can measure it. A panel that came out wrong is
firewood and half an hour; a panel discovered wrong when it is the
thirty-eighth thing lifted into place is a much longer day.

**A panel can be finished before it goes up.** Skin it, seal it, insulate it,
run a cable through it, paint it — all on the ground. This is a genuine
transformation of the work: it turns awkward three-dimensional construction at
height into repetitive two-dimensional production at a bench, which is
manufacturing rather than improvisation.

**The seam gets room for a gasket.** Two members with a gap between them is a
place a key, a spline or a compressible seal can live. One shared strut has no
such space — the panels either side bear directly on it, and weatherproofing
that joint is a separate problem solved with tape and hope. Chapter
17 is about what can be done with that space, and Chapter
16 about what has to be.

**A panel can be taken out again.** Undo one seam's fasteners and a panel
lifts out, leaving its neighbours standing and complete. Nothing else in the
shell depended on that panel's members. Replace a rotted one in year fifteen,
cut a window into one in year three, take three out to get a bathtub in —
these become ordinary jobs rather than partial demolitions.

**The frame gains redundancy.** Every interior edge has two members in it. One
splitting, checking or being damaged does not leave that edge unsupported. It
is not a designed redundancy and nobody should rely on it, but it is real.

## The check that has to close

Two independent ways of counting the members have to give the same answer, and
this is worth doing yourself once, because it is the check that tells you the
frame in your head is the frame on the ground.

**Count panel by panel.** Every panel has 3 members.

> 40 × 3 = 120

**Count edge by edge.** Every interior seam holds two members, one from each
panel. Every rim edge holds one, because there is nothing on the other side of
it.

> (55 × 2) + 10 = 120

Both give 120. The software in this project runs exactly this
check every time the book is built, and refuses to print anything if the two
disagree — because if they ever did, one of the two pictures would be of a
building that does not exist, and there would be no way to know which.

## When to share a strut instead

This chapter should not pretend the choice is obvious in every case.

Share the strut if:

* **Timber is your scarcest resource.** 1.85 times the
  members is 1.85 times the trees, and if you have one
  tree rather than two that decides it.
* **You have a crew and scaffolding.** The panelised method's main advantage is
  that one person can do it on the ground. With four people and a lift, in-air
  assembly is not the ordeal it is alone.
* **The dome is small.** Below a certain size the members are short enough to
  handle comfortably at height, and the panel machinery is overhead you do not
  need.
* **You want the lightest possible frame.** For a temporary structure, a
  greenhouse, or anything that has to be carried in, the weight of the
  duplicated members is a real cost.

Keep the panel if:

* **You are building alone**, which is what this method was designed for.
* **You want to finish panels on the ground**, which is most of the reason the
  build fits in a fortnight.
* **You want a weatherable seam**, because the gap is where the seal goes.
* **You expect the building to change**, because panels come out.

This book chose the panel, and every number in it follows from that choice.
Chapter 22's 3 trees are 3 trees
precisely *because* the
frame duplicates its edges. A shared-strut dome of the same diameter would
come out of about one.

That is the trade, stated plainly: **roughly twice the wood, for a building
one person can make on the ground and take apart again.**

---

# 19. Bends, Knots and Small Units

*Why six-foot pieces forgive what sixteen-foot boards do not*

## Small Units

Every chapter so far has argued from what the method saves — the mill,
the haul, the middlemen. This chapter argues from what it *tolerates*,
which is the quieter advantage and, for a builder working with whatever
grows, the more useful one.

The method's smallest unit is 6 feet: a
bucked section, then a wedge, then a member. That smallness changes
what counts as a defect. In a stick-frame economy, a bend or a knot
ruins a sixteen-foot board and gets charged to the whole load. Here, a
bend costs you a section, a knot costs you a stick, and the pile
around them is untouched. The method does not need straight trees; it
needs short straight *runs*, and a forest full of trees that are
"nearly straight for six feet" is a forest full of building material
nobody else will pay for.

That is the whole chapter: the defect economy of small units. It is
why the method can grade its own stock by eye, why it can build from
storm debris and clearing waste, and why the wood it uses never has to
travel further than the ground it fell on. Chapter 2
counted the chain the wood avoids. This chapter counts the *wood* the
chain avoids — the crooked, knotty, low-grade trees that never get on
the truck in the first place.

## A bend costs you a section, not a tree

Take the crookedest trunk you have — the one a mill would wave off the
landing without pricing. The method looks at it differently.

A bend does not make a tree useless. It makes the tree into two
straight logs with a kink between them. Buck the trunk on either side
of the bend — cut the kink out as firewood — and what remains is two
sections of straight-ish timber, each long enough to split into
8 members. The bend cost you a foot of trunk and a
minute of saw time. In a mill's accounting the same bend costs the
whole log its grade, because a mill needs its boards straight over
their full length, and a kink in the middle of a sixteen-foot board
is a board with no market.

The reason is the unit size. A mill's product is long boards; a bend
anywhere in the length ruins the board. This method's product is a
6-foot member; a bend only ruins the member
it falls inside. Cut around it and keep cutting. A trunk with three
bends yields four good sections and three short firewood rounds, and
all four sections are full-value frame stock. The same tree, sawn,
would have produced a pile of shorts worth the haul to the dump.

And because the frame's members are short, they can be *short of*
straight in a way long boards cannot. A member with a slow bow still
sits in the jig, still lands its butt on its neighbour's side, still
closes the panel — the pinwheel's overlap takes up what the eye can
see, and the panel comes out flat because the jig, not the stick,
decides where the corners are. Chapter 30 is that sentence
built in wood.

## Knots, and where they are allowed

The second defect economy rule: a knot is not one problem, it is two,
and only one of them matters here.

**A knot mid-span is a stress riser.** In a beam — a joist, a rafter —
a knot in the middle of the span is where the bending stress
concentrates, and a big knot there can decide the board's grade by
itself. Even in this frame, a knot in the middle of a member is the
worst place for it, because a compression member buckles at its
weakest section, and the weakest section is the one with the knot.
So the mid-span rule still applies: grade the pile, and keep the
heaviest knots out of the middle third of compression members.

**A knot at a bearing face is a joint problem.** Where one member's
butt lands on the next member's side, the load spreads across the
whole contact patch — 9.82 square inches of it, as
Chapter 10 measured. A small knot inside that patch is a
dent in the bearing, not a hinge; the load goes around it through the
wood beside it. A knot *at* the contact line, on the other hand, can
split the face the screws are pulling against. So the bearing rule:
sort the knotty ends away from the butt, and let the head end carry
them — the head end gets sawn off anyway, and Chapter
35 makes that offcut free.

The sorting takes an afternoon and a crayon, and it is the difference
between "the pile has knots" and "the pile has a plan". The next
section gives the procedure.

## One bent trunk, salvaged

The drawing shows one crooked trunk twice. On the left, the mill's
view: a log that will be rejected or down-graded, because no board
can be sawn straight out of it. On the right, this method's view: the
same trunk bucked on either side of its bends into straight sections,
each section split into eighths, the kinks themselves stacked as
firewood.

The two pictures are the same tree. The difference is not in the
wood — it is in what each system demands of a length of wood. The mill
demands sixteen straight feet, because its product is a board of
sixteen straight feet. The method demands 6
usable feet at a time, because its product is a member of
6. The tree that fails the first demand and
passes the second is not a worse tree. It is the same tree, measured
against a different product — and the trees that fail the mill's
demand are exactly the trees that are free.

![A bend a mill would refuse, and the sections either side of it.](../../deliverables/book/figures/bent-trunk.png)

## Grade your own stock

The procedure, because "sort the pile" is advice until it is a list.

**One: the bend pass.** Walk the pile and mark any member with a
visible bow over its length. A slow bow — under a thumb's width over
six feet — goes in the general pile; the jig will hold it straight.
A hard bow goes to blocking, bracing and stakes, where a curve costs
nothing.

**Two: the knot pass.** Mark the middle third of every member as it
comes off the split, and reject to the blocking pile any member with
a knot cluster in that third. A single small knot mid-span stays; a
knot cluster does not.

**Three: the bearing pass.** Look at the butt end of every member —
the end that will land on a neighbour's side. A knot within a hand's
width of the butt face sends the member to the other orientation:
flip it, so the knotty end becomes the head end and the clean end
becomes the butt. Only if both ends are knotty does the member leave
the frame pile.

**Four: the pith pass.** A member that split along the pith — a crack
running down its point — goes to blocking. It will keep splitting
under load, and a member with a crack in it has stopped being wood you
can trust.

The passes take an afternoon for the whole pile, and they are the
entire quality system. No kiln, no grader, no certificate — four
passes, a crayon, and the same eyes that will build with the wood. The
frame's redundancy does the rest: 40 panels mean a
suspect member is a member you can watch, and a watched member that
moves is a member you can swap, which is the honest engineering behind
"grade your own stock".

## Trees nobody else wants

The economic consequence of tolerating variation: the pool of building
material widens, and the price of the widened pool is nothing.

Storm debris. Clearing waste. The windfall of Chapter
1. Low-value sawlogs no mill will
pay haulage on. A tree too bent or too short for a mill is
8 good sticks a section here, and it is often already
lying on the site. Which removes the haul, the mill fee and the
markup, and replaces them with fuel and bar oil — the ledger Chapter
45 closes to the dollar.

The point is not that crooked trees are as good as straight ones. The
point is that this method's unit is small enough that *a usable
straight run* is the requirement, not *a straight tree*, and the
world is full of usable straight runs inside trees nobody wants. A
builder who can use them builds from the one timber supply whose
price never rises: the wood that is already on the ground where the
building goes.

## And the journey it does not take

Close the chapter where Chapter 2 opened, because the
two arguments are the same one seen from different ends.

The industrial route moves wood through 14
operations and a chain of hands: forest, logger, mill, kiln,
distributor, retailer, site. This method's route is
8: tree, cutting station, drying stack, jig. Every
removed stage is fuel, time and a margin — and every removed stage is
also a *filter*, because each middleman selects for the wood worth
hauling and grading and selling. The crooked tree never survives the
first filter. The bend costs it its ride.

The method's answer is to build where the wood is, from what the
filters rejected, in units small enough that the rejection never
happens. The chain argument was about money. This one is about
material — the same 8 steps, counted as distance
instead of cost, and the same conclusion: the best wood is the wood
that never had to travel, and the method is the only route that can
use it.

---

# 20. Which Way Round Are You Working?

*Choose your method before you touch anything*



## Which Way Round

There are two ways to size a wedge dome, and the first thing you have to do
is work out which one you are actually in. Not which one you would like to be
in. Which one you are in.

If you get this wrong you will spend a week doing arithmetic that describes a
building you cannot build, and you will not find out until the day you go to
cut, when the longest stick the frame wants turns out to be eight inches
longer than the longest stick you own.

Everything in the next five chapters follows from this one decision, so it
gets its own short chapter and no other content.

## Design first, or tree first

**Design first** is the way everybody expects to work. You know what you
want: a room of a certain size, a ceiling you can stand under, a footprint
that fits between the trees you are not cutting down. You start from the dome
and you work backwards to the woodpile. The output is a shopping list — this
many sticks, this long, and therefore this much trunk.

**Tree first** is the way this book was actually written. You have trees.
They are the trees you have: a certain height, a certain taper, a certain
amount of straight length between the butt flare and the first serious bend.
You start from what a section of that trunk can be cut into and you work
forwards to the dome. The output is not a shopping list. It is a diameter,
and you find it out rather than choosing it.

Both are honest. Neither is better. They answer different questions and they
run in opposite directions, and the entire content of Chapters 21 and 22 is
those two directions worked through in full.

## The honest test

Here is the test, and it is uncomfortable.

**Are the trees already down, or already chosen?**

If they are — if you are standing next to a windfall, or you have marked two
pines at the back of the property, or somebody has offered you a load — then
you are in Method B, and picking a diameter is not a decision you get to
make. It is a wish. You may still write a number on a piece of paper. The
trees will not read it.

If the trees are genuinely not chosen — you will buy or fell whatever the
design calls for, and there is enough standing timber that a foot either way
on the bucking length costs you nothing — then you are in Method A, and you
should design the dome you want.

Almost everybody thinks they are in Method A. Most people are in Method B.
The tell is whether you would actually go and get a different tree if the
arithmetic asked for one. If the answer is "well, I'd probably make it work
with these," you are in Method B and you may as well admit it on page one
rather than on cutting day.

![Start from what you actually have.](../../deliverables/book/figures/method-decision.png)

## What both methods share

Underneath the two directions there is one relationship, and it is worth
understanding before you use either method, because it is the thing that
makes both of them exact rather than approximate.
