In a 2V dome of radius *R*, every panel is a triangle whose corners sit on the
sphere. If the members simply ran corner to corner you could scale them
straight off the radius: double the dome, double the stick. But they do not
run corner to corner. They pinwheel — each member's end lands on the *side*
of the next one rather than on a shared point — and the amount each member is
set back from the true corner depends on **how wide the member is**, not on
how big the dome is.

A wider stick sits back further. And the width of your stick comes from the
diameter of your log, which has nothing whatsoever to do with the dome.

So member length is not proportional to radius. It is radius *minus* a
correction that stays the same size while the dome grows. Which means:

* Going from radius to member length is a calculation.
* Going from member length back to radius is that same calculation, solved
  backwards.

Neither direction is a rule of thumb, a table, or a fudge factor. They are
one function and its inverse, and Chapter 23 exists solely to run them
against each other and print the difference, which is
0.0e+00 inches.

## Before you go on

Whichever method you are in, you need three measurements before either
chapter is any use to you:

1. **The diameter of your log at the middle of a section.** Not at the butt —
   the butt is the fattest part and it flatters you. The middle, because that
   is what the average stick will be.
2. **The straight length you can get out of the trunk.** Between the flare
   and the first bend that would spoil a section.
3. **The longest piece you are willing to carry on your own.** This is a real
   constraint and it does more to set the dome's size than anything you will
   read in a book. A six-foot green pine wedge is heavy. An eight-foot one is
   a two-person lift, and if there is only one of you, that is the end of the
   discussion.

Write those three numbers down. Then turn to Chapter 21 if you are designing
first, or Chapter 22 if the trees are already on the ground.

And then, whichever one you used, read Chapter 23 and check it with the other
one. That takes ten minutes and it is the cheapest insurance in the book.

---

# 21. Method A: From the Dome You Want

*Radius in, cut list out*



## Method A

You know what you want. Now find out what it costs in trunk.

This is the method to use when the timber is genuinely not decided — when you
will fell, buy or scrounge whatever the design asks for. It runs from the
building to the woodpile, which is the direction every other construction
book works in, and it is the more comfortable of the two.

It is also the one that will tell you, politely and in advance, when the dome
you have in mind needs more trees than you have.

## The procedure

### 1. Start from floor, not radius

Nobody wants a radius. They want a room.

Decide the floor area you want to stand on and convert:

> radius (feet) = √(floor ÷ π)

300 square feet gives a radius of
9.77 feet — call it 117 inches. A 2V
hemisphere's standing height at the centre *is* its radius, so
that same number is also your ceiling.

This is the first place a dome surprises people. A
300-square-foot dome is 9.77 feet tall in
the middle, which is more house than the floor area
suggests, and the volume is doing something a rectangle of the same footprint
cannot.

It is also the first place a dome disappoints people, because not all of that
floor has headroom over it. Chapter 43 has the map.

### 2. Measure the stock you expect to have

Even in Method A you cannot skip this. The pinwheel setback depends on how
wide your members are, and you cannot compute a member length without it.

If you have not felled anything yet, use the diameter you expect to be
cutting. A 12-inch butt tapering to
8 gives sectors about 3.83 inches wide
at mid-trunk, and that is the figure this book's tables assume.

If your logs will be fatter, your members will be wider, your setback will be
larger, and every length in your cut list will come out shorter than the
tables here. The tables are computed at this book's stock; recompute for
yours.

### 3. Solve the member lengths

For a chosen radius, the layout gives you 4 member
classes. This is the calculation Chapter 20 described, run in the forward
direction: radius in, lengths out.

At 300 square feet the longest member comes out at
64.5 inches and the shortest at
56.1.

![300 square feet, worked all the way through.](../../deliverables/book/figures/method-a-worked.png)

### 4. Round the bucking length up

Your longest member is 64.5 inches. Buck to
6 feet — 72 inches.

You cannot buck to 64.5 inches. Well, you can, but you
should not: a section
cut to exactly the longest member leaves no allowance for the head-end
overfit (Chapter 35), no allowance for the butt cut (Chapter 34), and nothing
at all for the end of a log being split or shaken.

**Round up to the next whole foot and take the waste.** It is the cheapest
line item in the build.

This does mean the design-first path throws away more wood than the tree-first
path, because the tree-first path chooses a dome that uses the whole section.
That is a real cost of getting to pick your diameter, and it is worth saying
out loud rather than discovering in the offcut pile.

### 5. Count sections, then trunk, then trees

The frame needs 120 members. Each section gives
8, so:

> sections = 120 ÷ 8 = 15

> trunk needed = 15 × 6 ft = 90 feet

> trees = 90 ÷ 48 = 1.88

So: **fell 2.**

Notice the number. Designing a 300-square-foot dome from
scratch and sizing the trees to it lands on the same
2 trees that this book's actual build used, from the
opposite direction. That is not a coincidence — it is the size
of dome a pair of ordinary pines makes, and it is why this method produces
houses of roughly this size over and over.

### 6. Check it against the tree you can actually get

Then go and look at the trees.

If the tallest straight trunk you can find is thirty feet rather than
48, your 1.88 trees becomes three. If
your logs are much
thinner at the butt than 12 inches, your members
are narrower, your sticks are longer for the same dome — and thinner, which
Chapter 10 has opinions about.

This is where Method A hands over to Method B, and where Chapter 23's round
trip earns its place.

## Where this method surprises people

**Member length does not scale with the dome.** Double the radius and the
members do not double. They grow by slightly less, because the pinwheel
setback stays the same size while the triangle grows around it.

This is good news at large sizes and bad news at small ones. A very small
dome has members so short relative to their own width that the setback eats a
serious fraction of them, and below a certain size the geometry stops making
sense at all — a stick that is nearly as wide as it is long is not a strut,
it is a block. Chapter 47 says where that line is.

**Height is not a free variable.** In a plain hemisphere, height equals
radius. If you want a wide floor and a ceiling taller than
half that width, you cannot have both from a hemisphere; you need a riser wall
under it (Chapter 41) or a different fraction of a sphere, which is a different book.

**The trees number is rarely a whole one.** You will get
1.88, or 2.4, or 3.1. Round up and accept the leftover, or
shorten the bucking length and see
whether the arithmetic lands better. There is no third option that gets you a
partial tree.

## The lookup table

If you would rather not do any of this, the table below is the whole method
precomputed at this book's stock section. Find your floor area on the left and
read across.

![Floor area to trees.](../../deliverables/book/figures/design-lookup.png)

Two warnings about using it:

1. It assumes members 3.83 inches wide. Different logs,
   different table.
2. The `trees` column assumes 48 feet of usable trunk
   per tree. If your trees are shorter — and most are — scale it.

## Then check it the other way

You now have a cut list. Before you cut anything, take the longest member off
that list, hand it to Method B, and see what dome comes back.

It should be the dome you started with. If it is not, you have made an
arithmetic slip or changed an assumption halfway through — a different gasket
thickness, a different split count, a different stock width.

That check is Chapter 23, it takes ten minutes, and it is the reason this
book has two methods rather than one method and a warning.

---

# 22. Method B: From the Tree You Have

*Section length in, dome out*



## Method B

This is the method the book is named after, and it is the one that produced
the dome in the photographs. It starts from a tree lying on the ground and
ends with a number you did not choose.

That last part is the whole character of the method. In Method A you decide
how big the building is and the woodpile has to comply. Here the woodpile
decides, and your job is to find out what it decided.

## The procedure

### 1. Measure the trunk

Three numbers: diameter at the butt of the usable length, diameter at the
top of it, and the straight length between them.

"Usable" means the part with no bend serious enough to spoil a section and no
branch stub big enough to spoil a stick. It is almost always shorter than the
tree. On this book's tree it was 48 feet, out of a
considerably taller pine — the butt flare went in the fire and the top went
for kindling.

The tree this book is written around measures 12
inches at the butt of that length and 8 at the top.

![The book's tree, measured.](../../deliverables/book/figures/tree-measured.png)

### 2. Pick a bucking length you can carry

This is the decision, and it is a physical one rather than a mathematical
one. Go and pick up a green section of the length you are considering. If you
cannot get it onto a sawhorse on your own, it is too long, and no amount of
wanting a bigger dome will change that on day six.

This book buckes to 6 feet.

Everything downstream comes from this number. Choose it honestly.

### 3. Count your sections

Divide the usable length by the bucking length and throw away the remainder.
48 feet at 6 feet a section is
8 sections, with 0.0 feet left over.

If the remainder is large it is worth going back to step 2 and trying a
different bucking length. A foot of offcut is firewood; four feet is a stick
you did not get.

### 4. Multiply by eight

Each section splits into 8 sectors of
45 degrees each, and — this is the part that surprises
people who have milled lumber — **each sector is a finished structural
member**. Not a blank to be trimmed. Not stock to be dimensioned. A member.

8 sections × 8 sectors =
**64 struts from one tree.**

Two trees: **192 struts.**

The frame needs **120**.

That is the title of the book, and it is not a slogan. It is
72 spare — about seven per cent — which is roughly the
right margin for a stick that splits along the pith, a butt cut that goes
wrong, and the one panel everybody builds backwards.

![Eight sections of six feet.](../../deliverables/book/figures/bucking-plan.png)

### 5. Find your member's width

The dome does not care how long your sticks are until it knows how *wide*
they are, because the pinwheel setback depends on width.

The width of a sector is the chord across its bark face, measured at the
diameter you are cutting. Use the diameter at the **middle** of the trunk,
not the butt: half your sections come from thinner wood than the butt
suggests, and sizing the layout off the fat end gives you a dome that does
not quite close at the top.

At this tree's mid diameter the sector is 3.83 inches wide and
5.00 inches deep, pith to bark — a cross-section of
9.82 square inches.

For scale: that is 1.23 nominal two-by-fours' worth of
wood, or 1.87 of the dressed boards you would actually
find on a rack. Chapter 45 is about which of those numbers to believe.

### 6. Solve for the dome

Now the arithmetic. The longest member the frame may contain is the longest
stick you have, which is your bucking length: 6 feet.

Find the radius whose longest pinwheel member is exactly that.

This is not a division. As Chapter 20 explained, member length is radius less
a setback that does not scale, so the relationship has to be solved rather
than scaled. The software in this project does it by bisection, and you can
do it by trying radii until the longest member comes out right — it converges
in about six guesses.

The answer for this tree:

| | |
|---|---|
| Radius | 129.33 in |
| Diameter | **21.6 ft** |
| Height at the centre | 10.8 ft |
| Floor | **365 sq ft** |

![Two trees, worked all the way through.](../../deliverables/book/figures/method-b-worked.png)

### 7. Read your cut list

The frame's 120 members come in 4
lengths, running from 62.73 inches to
72.00 inches. Thirty of each.

Note that the longest is exactly your stock length, to the last decimal
place. That is not a coincidence and it is not luck — it is what step 6
solved for. It also means you have no margin on the long members at all,
which is why they are cut with the head end deliberately left over-long and
sawn off in place. Chapter 35 is about that.

![Every stick in the dome.](../../deliverables/book/figures/member-classes.png)

## You do not get to choose the diameter

This is the part people resist.

You measured a tree, you picked a length you could lift, and out came
21.6 feet. Nobody consulted you. If you wanted twenty-four
feet you cannot have twenty-four feet — not from these trees, bucked this
way, without doing something else that costs you elsewhere.

You have three ways out and they are all in Chapter 25:

* **Buck longer.** A longer section gives a bigger dome, out of fewer and
  heavier pieces, and at some point even 3 trees stop being
  enough. The table in
  Chapter 22's appendix shows exactly where that line is.
* **Get a third tree.** Fine, if you have one. It is the honest answer and it
  is not a failure.
* **Accept the dome the trees gave you.** Which is what happened here.

What you must not do is design for the dome you wanted and cut for the tree
you have. The frame will not close, and it will not tell you until the last
ring.

![Bucking length against dome size.](../../deliverables/book/figures/tree-lookup.png)

## A note on the third tree

3 trees is 192 struts for a
120-member frame, so the frame actually consumes
1.88 trees. The third tree — and most of the
second — is not fully used, and
the leftover is not waste — it is the spares, the blocking, the floor's
stock and the firewood, plus the next dome's first cut list.

But it does mean the honest headline is not "one dome from three trees." It is
"one dome from a bit under two trees, and you need the third one because you
cannot get eight-tenths of a tree."

Which is a worse title.

---

# 23. The Round Trip

*Proving the two methods are one calculation*



## The Round Trip

Two chapters ago there were two methods. This chapter is here to show that
there is really only one, held at opposite ends, and to give you a ten-minute
check that catches almost every arithmetic mistake you can make with either.

The reason this matters is not elegance. It is trust.

A book can tell you that a 6-foot stick gives a
21.6-foot dome and you have no way to know whether that came
out of a solver or out of somebody's memory of a similar dome. Rules of thumb
in building books are usually the latter, and they are usually close enough
to be dangerous. So rather than ask you to take it on faith, here is the
check, and here is the actual number it produces.

## Run it forwards, then backwards

Take any radius. Compute the longest member the frame would contain. Now
throw the radius away, and using *only* that member length, compute the
radius that a dome would need in order for that to be its longest member.

If the two methods are one calculation, you land exactly where you started.

* Start: a radius, any radius.
* Forwards (Method A): radius → longest member.
* Backwards (Method B): longest member → radius.
* Compare.

## The residual

Here is the check run on six domes, from small to large:

![Out and back.](../../deliverables/book/figures/round-trip.png)

The worst disagreement across all six is **0.0e+00
inches**.

For scale: the kerf of the chain on the saw this book was built with is
0.25 of an inch. The disagreement between the two methods is
smaller than the width of the cut by a factor with a lot of zeroes in it. It
is smaller than the seasonal movement of the wood. It is smaller than
anything you could measure with a tape, a story pole, or a laser.

It is, in other words, zero for every purpose a person with a chainsaw has.

That number is not asserted here. It is computed when this book is exported,
by running the check, and if it ever stops being negligible this sentence
will print a large number and the chapter will be wrong in an obvious way
rather than a quiet one.

## What would break it

The round trip closes because both directions are solving the same
relationship with the same assumptions. Change an assumption between the two
and it will not close — and this is exactly what makes it a useful check,
because those are the mistakes people actually make.

**Change the gasket thickness.** The seam key sits between panels and every
member length accounts for it. Design at 0.75 inches, then
compute your bucking with a half-inch key in mind, and the two answers
diverge. The gap will be small and it will be in the wrong direction — your
sticks will be very slightly too long, forty times over, and the dome will
fight you at the top.

**Change the split count.** Eight sectors gives a member
3.83 inches wide. Six gives a wider one, twelve a narrower
one, and the pinwheel setback moves with it. If you designed for eighths and
then split a few logs into sixths because they were fat, those members are
not interchangeable with the rest, and the round trip on the fat ones will
not close against the layout.

**Change the stock diameter.** Same problem, from the other side. Sizing the
layout off the butt diameter and cutting most of your sections from thinner
wood up the trunk means the members you actually get are narrower than the
ones you designed. Use the mid-trunk diameter, as Chapter 22 step 5 insists.

**Mix up feet and inches.** Says itself. It happens. The round trip catches
it immediately, because the recovered radius comes back off by a factor of
twelve rather than by a thousandth of an inch.

## How to actually do the check

You do not need software.

1. Finish whichever method you used. Write down your radius and your longest
   member.
2. Take the *other* method's procedure from Chapter 21 or 22.
3. Feed it the number you got out, and see if you get back the number you
   put in.

If you are within a quarter of an inch on the radius, you have not made a
mistake worth worrying about. If you are out by more than an inch, stop —
something in the middle changed and you should find out what before you cut a
hundred and twenty of anything.

## Why this chapter exists at all

Because the mistake this is designed to catch is not a mistake in the
arithmetic. It is a mistake in the *assumptions*, and those are invisible.

Arithmetic errors announce themselves: a number is obviously wrong, or the
units are absurd, or the answer is negative. Assumption drift does not.
Everything looks reasonable at every step. You get a cut list of plausible
lengths and you cut a hundred and twenty plausible sticks, and the first
thing that tells you is the frame, on day fourteen, when the last ring is a
half inch short all the way round and there is nothing to be done about it.

Ten minutes with the other method, before anything is cut, is the cheapest
insurance in this book.

---

# 24. The Worked Build

*Every number for this book's dome, in one place*

## The Worked Build

This chapter is the whole build on five pages: the tree, the dome it
makes, the cut list, the seam schedule, and the frame they describe. A
builder who wants to work from nothing else can work from this chapter
alone — every number in it comes from the same solved model the rest
of the book quotes, and the chapter exists so that the numbers have one
place to live where they cannot contradict each other.

The two sizing methods of Chapters 21 and 22
converge here. Method A says: pick the floor, and the arithmetic hands
back the tree. Method B says: measure the tree, and the arithmetic
hands back the dome. The worked build is Method B run to the end on the
tree of Chapter 1 — the windfall,
measured, bucked, split, and solved into the frame whose numbers follow.

Nothing on these pages was chosen for the pages. It was computed, and
the computation is the same one the films and the simulator run, so
the book, the film and the model cannot disagree about the dome.

## The tree

The input, measured before any cutting: 12
inches at the butt, 8 at the top,
48 feet of usable trunk, bucked into
8 sections of 6 feet, each
section split into 8 wedges. One tree yields
64 struts; 3 trees yield
192, against a frame that needs
120. The recovery arithmetic — 318 board
feet in, 278 out, 87.4 per cent kept —
is Chapter 13 in full; here it is only the row that
feeds the rest of the chapter.

![This book's tree.](../../deliverables/book/figures/worked-tree.png)

## The dome

The output: the solved hemisphere that 3 trees of this
taper will fill. 21.6 feet across, 10.8
feet to the crown, 365 square feet of floor. The
longest member is 72.00 inches; the shortest
62.73; the whole frame stands on
673 feet of timber. The strict count says the frame
actually consumes 1.88 trees, and the book
says the rest of the pile is spares, blocking, floor stock and
firewood rather than
pretending the arithmetic is tidier than it is.

![The dome that results.](../../deliverables/book/figures/worked-dome.png)

## The cut list

All 120 members, by class, length and count, straight
from the pinwheel layout. 4 distinct lengths
cover the whole frame — the two edge classes of Chapter
9, split into four by the pinwheel's inset
of Chapter 15. The table is the jig's job description:
every stick that crosses the bench is one of these rows, and nothing
else.

Beside it sits the shorter list the build actually came out with — **the
master cut list**, 3 resultant lengths:
**72.0 inches**, **63.5** and
**61.5**. Resultant means *finished*: cut a member to
one of these and the panel it goes into is the size the dome wants, with
nothing left over to trim. Every equilateral panel takes three of the long
member. Every isosceles panel takes the long member as its base, the middle
length on the left and the short one on the right.

That orientation is a bench rule rather than a preference, and the shape
shows why. Lay an isosceles panel with its base nearest you and the
63.5-inch left member is the longer of the two sides: it
reaches over to the top point, which sits off the middle of the base
because the two sides are not equal. The 61.5-inch right
side runs up to meet it, and it is that shortest member the base butts up
to — the base's end lands on the side of the short one, which is the
pinwheel's chase closing the triangle. Build a panel with the two sides
swapped and it does not close: two inches over five feet is not something
the eye catches, so the sides are picked from their two marked stacks, in
order, every time.

The two lists differ, and the book prints both rather than picking one.
The table above is the pinwheel model's seated schedule, which represents
the inset as a fourth length; the master list is what the saw cut and what
the panels measured when they closed. The model is a model of the joint;
the master list is a report of the build, and where the two disagree, the
tape on the finished panel is the authority.

![The master cut list, and which side it goes on.](../../deliverables/book/figures/master-cutlist.png)

![The full cut list.](../../deliverables/book/figures/worked-cutlist.png)

## The seam schedule

Every seam, its fold angle and its key: 25 at
18.03 degrees, 30 at 22.46,
55 in all, the two profiles of Chapter 16.
The schedule is generated from the solved dome, so the build's check
is simple: keys placed equals 55, profiles used equals
2, and the dome closes without being forced.

![The seam schedule.](../../deliverables/book/figures/worked-seams.png)

## The dome this describes

The frame itself — the simulator's own solved meshes, in the book's
print palette, exactly the dome the four tables specify. If the
chapter above is the arithmetic, this page is what the arithmetic is
*of*: 40 panels, 120 members,
55 seams, 21.6 feet across, standing.

![21.6 feet across, 365 square feet of floor.](../../deliverables/book/figures/worked-render.png)

---

# 25. When the Numbers Say No

*Reading a result that means do not build this*

## When the Numbers Say No

The two sizing methods of the previous chapters end in numbers, and
sometimes the numbers say no. This chapter is the permission to listen
to them, and the procedure for what to do after.

A book about a build method is at its most useful when it tells you
when *not* to build — before the tree is down, before the pile is
split, before the jig is built. The failures this method can produce
are not dramatic; they are quiet, and they arrive as arithmetic. Two
trees that give one hundred and twelve struts when the frame needs one
hundred and twenty. A member too heavy for the person who has to lift
it. A stick too slender to trust. Each one is a sentence the numbers
say, and each one has a standard answer — sometimes a smaller dome,
sometimes a fourth tree, sometimes a different bucking length, and
sometimes, honestly, not this build at all.

The chapter is short and it is howto, because the decision it teaches
is the one that saves the most: reading a bad result as information
instead of as a setback, while the fix is still cheap.

## Not enough struts

The classic case, and the one the title of this book invites.
3
trees, bucked and split, give 64 struts each —
192 in all, against a frame that needs
120. The arithmetic works out with 72
to spare. But run the same method on trees that are shorter, thinner
or bucked longer than this book's, and the count shrinks: 3 trees
can come out short of the frame's count, and then the method
says no.

Read the shortfall as a measurement of how far off the trees are, not
as a verdict on the plan. The frame's count — 120 — is
fixed by the geometry; it does not negotiate. The trees' count is the
variable, and it is a product of three choices: the usable length you
bucked, the section length you chose, and the number of trees you
felled. Any one of the three can be changed, and the section below —
"Three ways out" — runs the arithmetic for each. The one answer the
chapter forbids is the silent one: one hundred and twelve struts do not
become one hundred and twenty by cutting more carefully. The count is
not a quality issue; it is a quantity issue, and only a quantity fixes
it.

The spare margin deserves one honest note, because it is easy to read
wrong. This book's 72 spare struts are not
generosity; they are the rejection allowance for the grading passes of
Chapter 19 — the bent, the knotty, the pith-split that
leave the pile. A build planned with zero spares is a build that
assumes every stick passes, and no pile does. So the real threshold is
not "one hundred and twenty struts"; it is "one hundred and twenty,
plus the rejection rate your own pile actually showed you". If two
trees give you exactly one hundred and twenty, the numbers are not
saying yes. They are saying *tight*, and tight is a no with a schedule.

## Too big to lift alone

The second way the numbers say no has nothing to do with counts. A
6-foot green wedge is heavy — the book's own
members run 9.82 square inches of wet pine — and the
whole method is built around one person carrying, setting and
fastening every piece alone. A dome sized by the tree can produce
members past the point one person handles, and then the numbers say no
in your back instead of in the ledger.

The honest test is not the table, it is the afternoon: pick up the
heaviest member the design produces, green and wet, and carry it the
distance the build will ask. If it can be carried, set and held with
one hand while the other drives a screw, the design passes. If it
cannot, the fix is not a stronger builder — it is a shorter bucking
length, which makes every member lighter, or a second pair of hands,
which Chapter 49 treats as its own
design change. The arithmetic follows the carry: shorter sections
mean more sections, more splits, and the same frame from lighter
pieces — and the tree count re-solves with the new section length,
which is the "shorter bucking" row of the section below.

The limit that matters here is the one Part Two formalises. Chapter
51 names the declared longest member one person will
handle — 6 feet — and shows what diameter it
reaches, because the flat-rate argument this book makes is bounded by
exactly this physical ceiling. The sizing methods do not know about
your back. You do, and the numbers wait for you to tell them.

## Stock too small in section

The third no, and the one that hides inside the first two: the trees
are long enough and the count works out, but the sticks are slender —
a small butt diameter, split eight ways, yields a sector too thin to
trust.

The check is against the comparison Chapter 7 runs:
a wedge from a very small log can fall below the dressed board it is
supposed to replace, and then the method's central claim quietly
inverts. The crossover is real and it is diameter-dependent — below
some butt size, the wedge is the wrong answer and the honest move is
to buy the boards. The chapter on sections prints the comparison; here
the procedure is the thing: compute your own tree's sector area before
committing, compare it against the dressed two-by-four's
5.25 square inches, and if your tree loses the
comparison, the numbers are saying no to *this tree*, not to the
method.

The options are the same three, with a twist. Shorter bucking does
not fatten a stick; it only shortens one. An extra tree adds count, not
section. The real fixes for a slender section are a thicker tree, a
coarser split — six sectors instead of eight, the row Chapter
12 priced — or a different member entirely, which is what
Chapter 72 is for: the hollow, laminated and
manufactured wedges exist precisely because some trees say no.

## Three ways out

The three standard exits, with the arithmetic each one runs, so a
"no" from the numbers always lands on a next step.

**Shorter bucking.** Cut the sections shorter and every member gets
lighter and shorter; the dome the tree sizes shrinks accordingly, and
the strut count per tree rises. Run Method B again with the new
section length — Chapter 22 is a procedure, and a
procedure re-runs. The cost is a smaller floor and more sections to
split; the gain is a build one person can carry.

**A fourth tree.** Add a tree and the count problem disappears — four
trees of this book's size give far more than 120 — while
the member weight and section stay exactly as designed. The cost is
one more fell and one more buck, and Chapter 45 shows what
an extra tree
actually adds in fuel and hours: not nothing, and rarely the thing
that kills the build.

**A smaller dome.** Solve Method A downward — Chapter 21
runs in either direction — until the tree you have is the tree the
dome needs. The floor shrinks with the radius, and the shrink is
where the honesty of this chapter lives: a dome you can actually
build is better than the dome whose numbers said no.

And the fourth option, stated because a howto chapter owes it: **do
not build this.** Not every tree, not every site, not every back
should become a dome. The method's own arithmetic will say so when it
is true, and the builder who hears it has lost an afternoon of sums
instead of a fortnight of felling. The numbers saying no are the
cheapest form of the no there is.

---

# 26. Day Zero: The Saw

*A hundred and ninety-nine dollars and a sixteen-inch bar*

## Day Zero

Before the fortnight there was a day zero, and day zero was the saw.

The whole method claims to need one machine, and the claim deserves to
be looked at honestly before the felling starts. The saw this build
used was the smallest one on the shelf: a 38.2-cc
consumer saw with a 16-inch bar, 199 dollars,
bought new. Not a professional felling saw, not a mill, not a rental —
the same saw a homeowner buys for firewood. If the method works with
this saw, it works with almost any saw; if it secretly needed more,
better to find out on day zero than on day nine.

This chapter assesses the tool the way the book assesses everything
else: what it can do, what it cannot, what the counterfactual would
have bought, and the setup and safety that decide which of the two
lists you live on. The fortnight's days each open with a sentence of
their own; this one opens the whole clock, because every day after it
is this saw's day, and the saw is the only tool in the book.

## The smallest saw on the shelf

What 38.2 cubic centimetres and a 16-inch
bar can actually do, because the honest list is shorter than the
advertised one and more useful.

**It fells a modest tree.** A 12-inch pine is
well inside what this saw drops, as long as the notch and the hinge
are cut the way Chapter 27 describes and
not the way impatience does. The saw is not the limit at felling; the
technique is.

**It rips a six-foot section along the grain.** This is the real test,
and the saw passes it with a margin — a rip through wet pine is slow
work, the chain must be sharp and the bar must stay cool, but the
0.25-inch kerf through a 6-foot
section is a normal afternoon's work, not a stunt. Chapter
29 is the whole ripping ledger, and
its honest finding is that the saw is adequate and the arm is the
bottleneck.

**It cannot be hurried.** A small saw has a small engine, a small oil
pump and a small appetite for abuse. Buried to the dogs in green
pine, it will pull its chain off the bar, overheat, and dull twice as
fast as a professional saw doing the same cut. The method works
*with* this saw by working at its pace — 5.0
struts an hour, sustained, is that pace, and the pace is the method.

What it cannot do at all: mill a board, rip a full-length log in one
pass, or survive being used like a bigger saw. None of those is
needed. The saw's job in this method is exactly three cuts — fell,
buck, split — and every one of them is within this machine's honest
envelope. The method was not adapted to a small saw; it was designed
so a small saw could do it, which is a different and stronger claim.

## What I would have bought instead

The honest counterfactual, because a build book that never admits its
tool was marginal is not being read by somebody who owns the same saw.

A bigger saw — a professional 60-cc class with a longer bar — would
have made the ripping faster, the felling safer-feeling, and the
afternoons shorter. It would have cost several times the
199 dollars, and it would not have changed the method
by one step. The split count, the bucking length, the jig, the
pinwheel — none of them are set by the saw's size. They are set by
the tree and the back, and a bigger saw changes neither.

What a bigger saw would have changed is the *margins*: fewer
sharpening stops, a cooler chain, a faster recovery when a cut went
wide. Those are real, and the book says so rather than romanticising
the small saw. But the method's claim was never "this saw is the best
tool". It was "this saw is *enough*", and enough is a fact you can
verify at the store, in the wood, on day zero — which is the day this
chapter is.

If you own a bigger saw, use it. If you own this one, the method
works with it. If you own neither, the cheapest saw that will hold a
16-inch bar is the one this book's numbers were produced
with.

## Setting it up

The setup, in order, because a chainsaw set up wrong is a saw that
punishes the setup, not the wood.

**The chain.** New or sharpened, tensioned so it snaps back against
the bar but still pulls around by hand. A loose chain comes off; a
tight chain burns the bar. Check it cold, then again after the first
cut, because a warm chain is longer than a cold one.

**The ripping chain.** Ripping with a crosscut chain is possible and
slow. A ripping chain — filed to a shallow angle — cuts with the
grain instead of fighting it, and it is the single cheapest upgrade
the fortnight has. If the budget allows one extra chain, make it a
rip chain, and keep it for the splitting days.

**Bar oil, always full.** Ripping runs the bar buried for minutes at
a time, which is exactly the condition that cooks a dry bar. The oil
cap is the only gauge that matters on day five through eight; check
it every tank of fuel.

**The file.** A round file matched to the chain, used every few
tanks, by hand, to the chain's own angle. Chapter
29 counts what a dull chain costs in
kerf and in arms; here the rule is only: sharp is the state the saw
arrives in, and you are the one who keeps it there.

**Fuel, mixed right.** Two-stroke mix at the ratio on the saw, mixed
fresh, not last season's. The fuel ledger of Chapter
55 is built on a saw that starts
first pull every morning, and starting first pull is a fuel-quality
fact before it is a mechanical one.

## Chaps, helmet, and never alone

The safety page, placed here on purpose: the saw starts on day zero,
so the rules start on day zero.

**Chaps.** Chainsaw-protective trousers, worn for every cut, no
exceptions for a quick trim. They are the difference between a
bruise and an ambulance, and they cost less than the saw.

**Helmet, visor and ear protection.** A falling branch, a kicked
chain, a saw at full throttle for hours — all three are ordinary
events in this fortnight, and all three are aimed at your head.

**Never alone.** The single rule that overrides every other rule in
the book: do not run a chainsaw with nobody in earshot. The felling
chapter says it again, the ripping chapter assumes it, and the story
chapters record the one day it was nearly broken. A phone is not a
second person. A second person is a second person.

The book's warning from the front matter stands unrepeated here but
not unrenewed: felling kills people who have done it a hundred times,
a chainsaw is the most dangerous tool most people ever hold, and
nothing in this book is worth what a silent afternoon with a saw can
cost. Day zero is the day the tool arrives. The respect it deserves
arrives the same day, or not at all.

---

# 27. Days One and Two: Felling

*Putting 3 trees on the ground where you want them*

## Felling

The fortnight's first two days are its most dangerous, and the book
does not soften the sentence: felling is the operation that kills
people who have done it a hundred times, and these two days are
felling.

The job is simple to state — put 3 trees on the ground, where the
bucking and ripping can reach them — and every word after that
sentence is the reason it is not simple to do. The tree decides where
it falls; the feller only influences the decision, through the notch,
the hinge and the back cut. This chapter is the influence, in order:
the safety that comes before the first cut, the two cuts that steer
the tree, the drawing of the hinge that does the steering, the
limbing that follows the fall, and the story half — what actually
happened on this build's second tree, because the story half is where
the chapter earns its place.

Two days for 3 trees is not padding. Each tree is felled, limbed
and made safe before the next is touched, and the second day exists
because the first tree teaches something the next one needs.

## Read this before the notch

The safety page, first, because everything after it assumes it.

**The escape route.** Before the saw starts, walk the route away from
the tree — back and to the side, opposite the planned fall — and
clear it. The route is not "away from the tree"; it is a specific,
walked, cleared line, and it is the thing you use the moment the tree
moves. A tree falls in seconds and the escape takes seconds; the
route being pre-walked is the entire margin.

**The hinge.** The strip of uncut wood between the notch and the back
cut is the hinge, and it is the steering. It keeps the tree attached
while it starts to fall, aims it along the notch, and holds the butt
down so the trunk cannot kick back. Cut through it — the back cut
carried all the way to the notch — and the tree is free to go
anywhere, which is how the word "uncontrolled" enters the report.

**Barber chair.** A leaning tree, cut badly, can split up its own
trunk as it falls — the butt tearing open like a hinged lid, fast,
at head height. It happens when the hinge fails before the tree is
committed. The prevention is the hinge's size and the order of the
cuts, and the respect is: never stand behind a felling tree, not for
one second, not to watch.

**Widow-makers.** Dead branches hung in the crown, freed by the fall
or by vibration, that come down before, with, or after the tree.
Look up before the first cut. Look up again before the back cut. The
crown decides some of what happens next, and it has been deciding
since before you arrived.

**Two people, minimum.** The front matter's rule, restated here where
it binds hardest: never fell alone. The second person is the lookout,
the caller, and the one who goes for help — and their one job during
the fall is to be out of the fall.

## The notch and the back cut

The procedure, in the order that keeps the hinge whole.

**Face the fall.** Stand where the tree should go. The notch opens in
that direction, and the tree will follow the notch unless the hinge
says otherwise — so the first decision is the fall line, made from
the tree's lean, the wind and the landing zone, in that order of
authority.

**Cut the notch.** Two cuts meet to take a wedge out of the trunk on
the fall side: the first cut slants down into the tree, the second
comes in flat from below to meet it, and the wedge falls out. The
notch's depth is about a fifth of the trunk's diameter — deep enough
that the tree has somewhere to rotate into, shallow enough that the
hinge behind it is still the width of the trunk. On this book's
12-inch trees the notch is a hand's span, and
the two cuts must meet cleanly; a notch with a step in it steers a
tree sideways.

**Cut the back.** From the far side, level with the notch's flat
floor, the back cut runs toward the notch and stops short of it. The
wood left between — the hinge — is a strip the thickness of a couple
of fingers, even across the trunk. The tree is now standing on its
hinge, committed but held, and it will fall into the notch as the
hinge lets go.

**Leave.** The moment the tree moves, the saw comes out of the cut
and the feller takes the escape route, eyes on the crown, without
turning a back on the fall. The hinge finishes the job; the feller's
job from here is distance.

The hinge dimension is the whole art. Too thick, and the tree sits
on the saw and falls late or not at all; too thin, and the hinge
breaks early and the tree falls wherever its weight wants. The even,
fist-thin strip is the difference between a fall and an event.

![The hinge steers the tree. Everything else is timing.](../../deliverables/book/figures/felling-hinge.png)

## The hinge, drawn

The drawing shows the hinge doing its three jobs at once, because the
jobs are the explanation for everything above.

**It aims.** The hinge is the uncut wood across the back of the
notch, and it is flexible only in one direction — along the fall
line. As the tree tips into the notch, the hinge bends like a door
hinge, and the tree can only go where the hinge bends. Cut the hinge
uneven and the tree steers toward the thick side; cut it straight and
the notch's direction is the tree's direction.

**It holds.** While the tree tips, the hinge keeps the butt attached
to the stump. That attachment is what stops the trunk from kicking
back off the stump as the crown hits the ground — the butt stays
pinned, the tree hinges down like a gate, and the fall is one
motion instead of a throw.

**It lets go.** At the end of the fall the hinge's fibres tear, and
the tree separates from the stump cleanly. The timing of that tear
is set by the hinge's thickness — the feller's one real control over
the whole event, and the reason the dimension is measured, not
guessed.

## Limbing without pinching

The tree is down. The next job is the crown, and it is the part
where the saw gets pinched, because a downed trunk is a compressed
spring in disguise.

The trunk lying on the ground is bent slightly by its own weight:
the top side is in *tension* — the fibres stretched — and the bottom
side in *compression* — the fibres squashed. Cut from the top down
into that bend and the kerf opens as the tension side relaxes — safe.
Cut from the bottom up and the kerf closes on the bar as the weight
settles — pinched, stuck, and often the start of a fight with a
half-cut limb under load. The rule: **read the bend, cut the
tension side first, or cut from the outside of the bend inward.**

Limb the trunk in sections, cutting the branches off flush with the
trunk but never into it — the bucking days want clean wood, not
wounded wood. Work from the butt toward the top, keep the trunk
between you and the slope, and expect every branch to move when its
tension releases. A limb under a fallen trunk is loaded wood; the
moment the saw frees it, it springs. Stand where the spring is not.

By the end of day one the first tree is a clean, bare trunk on level
ground, the crown stacked where the firewood will be cut from it
later, and the saw has done the most dangerous work it will do all
fortnight.

## What actually happened

The story half, recorded the way this book records everything that went
wrong: named, not tidied.

The first tree went exactly where it was aimed. The notch was clean,
the hinge held, and the trunk landed inside a step of the planned
line, which the plan treated as a triumph and the book records as
luck doing its job: the tree was straight, the wind was nothing, and
the lean agreed with the aim.

The second tree did not go where it was supposed to.

It was the lean. The tree had grown with a slight bow toward the
open ground, and the feller — one person, reading the trunk by eye —
cut the notch for where the tree *should* fall and the back cut for
where the hinge would let it. The hinge steered; the lean overruled;
the tree fell a quarter-turn off the aim, taking a young neighbour
down with it and landing with its crown in the brush instead of on
the clearing.

Nobody was hurt. The escape route was where it was supposed to be,
and the miss happened in slow, visible degrees rather than in one
event — which is exactly what the hinge, even a mis-aimed one, is
for. The lesson cost a morning: the second tree had to be worked out
of the brush, the neighbour's remains cut clear, and the fall line
for the *next* tree chosen by the lean alone, which is the rule the
chapter opened with and the feller had half-ignored.

The book keeps the story because the story is the point of the
chapter. The notch, the hinge, the escape route — they are not
technique for a straight tree on a calm day. They are the margin
for the tree that is not straight and the day that is not calm, and
on day two the margin was spent exactly as designed: the tree fell
wrong, and the feller walked away. Two trees down. The fortnight
had begun.

---

# 28. Days Three and Four: Bucking

*Cutting the trunk into the only length that matters*

## Bucking

The trees are down. Days three and four turn each bare trunk into
8 sections of 6 feet — the
only length in the whole method, and the number that sizes everything
downstream.

Bucking looks like the simple day of the fortnight and is not,
because every section after it is the *whole* building in embryo: the
section length sets the member length, the member length sets the
dome, and a bucking error made on day three arrives on day fourteen
as a frame that will not close. The chapter's job is the one
measurement, repeated, without drift — plus the two unglamorous
subjects that decide whether the day is a one-person job: where the
sections start, and how a green six-foot round moves.

48 feet of trunk, cut into 8
sections, offcut 0.0 feet. That is the whole day,
counted. The rest is doing it the same every time.

## Marking and cutting to length

The procedure, built around the one fact that makes bucking hard: the
trunk tapers, so a tape laid along the bark measures around a slope
and over bumps, and drifts.

**Mark with a stick, not a tape.** Cut one 6-foot
stick — a story pole — from anything straight, and mark every section
with it. The stick does not stretch, does not creep, and reads the
same at the butt and at the top. It is the jig before the jig:
the length made physical, once, instead of re-read forty times.

**Mark a line around, not a tick.** One tick on the bark disappears
into the cut. Mark the full circumference with the saw's paint or a
lumber crayon, rolled around the trunk with a square laid flat, so
the cut is guided from any side. A crooked crosscut is a section
with sloped ends, and a sloped end is a wedge with one short face —
the error that hides until assembly.

**Cut from the top side of the bend.** The downed trunk is bent by
its own weight, exactly as the limbing chapter warned: cut from the
compression side and the kerf closes on the bar. Roll the trunk or
cut from above, and let each section's weight drop it free the moment
the cut goes through. A section that hangs by a thread of bark is a
section that will fall when it wants to; finish the cut.

**Cut in the air, not into the ground.** The bar through the trunk
and into the dirt dulls the chain in one cut. Roll a short log under
the cut line, or cut most of the way through and roll the trunk to
finish from above. The chain's sharpness budget belongs to the
ripping days, not to the ground.

**Check the count.** 8 sections per tree, counted
twice — once as cut, once stacked. The count is the chapter's whole
deliverable, and a missing section is a missing 8
members, which the frame will notice before you do.

## Where to start, and why not at the butt

The instinct is to start at the butt and measure up. The method says
start further in, and the reason is the taper's fault.

The butt end of a felled tree is not cylinder — it is flare. The
root swell runs up the trunk a foot or two, and a section cut from
the flare is an oval, its rings running wild, its first split a
guess about where the pith actually is. So the bucking starts where
the flare ends: stand the tape against the trunk, find where the
diameter stops falling fast, and make that the first cut. The flare
itself becomes firewood — the offcut of the plan below — and every
section after it is true round, which is what the split count
assumes.

The same rule finds the start around bends. The felling chapter left
the trunk with its kinks in it, and bucking is where the kinks come
out: cut the sections on the straight runs, leave the bent lengths
between them as firewood, and count only straight sections toward
the 8. The trunk does not owe you its bends; the
plan knows they exist and pays them in firewood.

The top end, too, gets trimmed: the last section before the crown
is where the diameter is smallest and the knots thickest, and if the
crown side of it looks like a branch knot rather than a log, it
joins the firewood pile. The usable length the tree chapter measured
was never the whole trunk; it was the straight, flare-free, crown-free
run, and bucking is the day the distinction becomes a pile of rounds.

## Eight sections from one trunk

The drawing is the day's plan at a glance: 48
feet of trunk, laid out into 8 sections of
6, the offcut — 0.0 feet of
flare and crown — marked off and consigned to the firewood pile.

Read it as the book's first real inventory rather than a diagram.
Every section in the drawing is 8 future members; the
whole trunk is 64 struts; two trunks are
192, against a frame that needs
120. The bucking day is where those numbers stop being
the tree chapter's arithmetic and start being a stack of wood you can
walk around. If the stack does not match the drawing — a section
short, a count off, an offcut bigger than the plan — day three is the
day to find out, when the fix is another section or another tree,
not another month.

![48 feet into 8 sections of 6. Offcut: 0.0 feet.](../../deliverables/book/figures/bucking-plan.png)

## Moving six-foot green sections alone

The unglamorous logistics, because the method's one-person claim
lives or dies on this afternoon, not on the geometry.

A 6-foot section of green pine is a wet,
round, heavy thing — the butt sections near the flare can be a full
day's load on their own. The method does not pretend otherwise; it
moves them the way they want to be moved: **roll them**. A round
section rolls; the only lift is the short end-over-end to clear a
branch or a bank, and the ground does the carrying. Lay the bucking
line out so the sections can roll to the splitting station — downhill
is free, level is fine, uphill is the day's only real fight — and
the "moving" half of bucking disappears into gravity.

For the sections that must lift: never a dead lift of the whole
section. Roll it onto a low block, lever the end with a limb, or
cut it into two shorter sections and accept the split-count change —
the section length is a plan, not a law, and a back is not a
substitute for a winch. The peavey, if one is on hand, turns the
roll into a one-handed job; without it, the wedge and the lever do.

The real measure of day four is not the pile. It is whether day
five can start without lifting a single section twice — the
splitting station laid out, the sections staged where the ripping
will happen, and the fortnight's only heavy lifting behind you. The
bucking days end with 8 sections a tree, stacked
dry-side-up, counted, and the saw's chain still sharp for the work
that actually makes the members.

---

# 29. Days Five to Eight: Ripping

*One hundred and twenty struts, thirty to an afternoon*

## Ripping

Days five through eight are the bulk of the labour and the heart of
the method: every section of the two trunks, split along its length
into 8 wedges, until the frame's
120 members exist as a pile. Two trees become a
building on these four days; everything before was preparation and
everything after is assembly.

The arithmetic sets the pace and the pace is honest:
5.0 struts an hour, sustained, means
24 hours of ripping — 4
afternoons — for the whole frame. That is the number the fortnight's
reputation rests on, and it was measured on this build, not promised.
The chapter is the measurement made into a procedure: the three
passes at the log, the cradle that holds a round section still, the
pith you aim at, the rhythm that sustains the rate, and the chain
sharpness that the whole four days secretly depend on.

## The three passes, at the log

The procedure of Chapter 12, now with the saw actually in
hand, at production pace.

**Stage the section.** Every section gets stood on end or laid in
the cradle before the saw starts — never ripped on the ground where
it can roll, never half-rolled between passes. The staging is the
first third of the work, and a section staged wrong costs more time
than a cut made slow.

**Pass one: halve it.** Aim down the pith line on the end grain and
rip the full length. The saw walks itself through a straight section
if the bar starts true; the feller's job is to start true and let
the saw cut — a ripping saw is steered with small corrections, not
pushed. The two halves fall apart with flat faces down.

**Passes two and three: quarter, then eighth.** Each half sits on
its flat face in the cradle and gets halved again; each quarter the
same. Seven cuts per section, all of them "through the middle", and
the pile of wedges grows by 8 with every section
finished.

**Stack as you go.** The wedges go straight onto stickers, sawn
faces down, bark up, where the drying chapter wants them — not
thrown on a pile where the day's own moisture steams them. A wedge
stacked right on day five is a straight member on day thirty; the
stacking is part of the cut, not the cleanup after it.

**Count the pile against the plan.** 64 struts
a tree, 192 for the two, 120 in
the frame, 72 spare for the grading rejects. The
count is checked at the end of every afternoon, because a shortfall
found on day eight is a fourth tree; a shortfall found on day
thirteen is a problem with no good name.

## Holding a round section still

The first pass is the hard one for a reason the second and third do
not have: a round section wants to roll, and a rolling log under a
ripping saw is where pinched bars and ruined cuts are made.

The cradle is the answer, and it does not need to be furniture. Two
stakes driven either side of the section, a pair of wedges under it,
or a simple V of scrap timber along its length — anything that stops
the roll and leaves the top of the section at a working height. The
cradle's job is negative space: it holds the log where the saw is
not. Once the first pass is made, the halves sit on their flat faces
and the cradle's work is mostly done — flat stock on a flat bench is
stable by itself, which is the second reason the halving pass goes
first, every time.

The wedges earn their place on the *first* pass and again at the
end. Driven under a section, they level it, lift it off the dirt,
and take the weight so the saw's kerf stays open. A section that
settles as you cut closes the kerf behind the bar — the saw bogs,
the cut wanders, and the pass is ruined in its last foot. The wedge
set under the cut line is the cheapest insurance in the method.

## Reading the pith

Every rip aims at the centre of the section, and the centre of a
real section is not a point — it is a small, dark, slightly
off-centre dot in the end grain, and the first skill of the ripping
days is finding it.

The pith is the tree's first year: a soft spot, usually darker than
the rings around it, not always exactly centred. The split geometry
does not care that it is slightly off — the sectors of a slightly
off-centre split are nearly identical, and "nearly" is what the
method budgets for. What matters is that every pass goes *through*
the pith, so every sector keeps a point that is honest wood, not a
rag of pith off to one side. A sector whose point misses the pith
by an inch is a member with a soft, split-prone edge exactly where a
member most needs honest wood.

On a section that is not round — the oval butt, the flat-sided
trunk — the pith still rules. Aim at the centre of the rings, not
the centre of the shape; the rings are the tree's own record of
where its middle is, and they are right more often than the outline
is. The first pass is started from a stance where the pith is
visible the whole way — sighting down the bar, checking every few
feet — because a pass that starts an inch off arrives a foot off,
and a wedge is six feet long.

## The cutting rhythm

The chart is the claim and the proof together: 5.0
struts an hour, sustained, for six hours, afternoon after afternoon.
It is not a sprint rate; it is the rate the body can hold while the
saw cools between cuts, the pile is stacked as it grows, and the
count is kept.

Read the rhythm as a sequence of stations rather than a series of
cuts. Stage, halve, quarter, eighth, stack, count — one circuit per
section, the same motions in the same order, the saw never waiting
on a decision. The chart's flatness is the point: a sustained rate
is a rate with no thinking in it, and the method was arranged so the
thinking happened once, in these chapters, and the ripping days just
repeat. The afternoons that beat the rate are the ones where
something was skipped — a staging, a stacking — and the debt shows
up the next morning as a pile to sort and a chain to sharpen before
the first cut.

![5.0 struts an hour, sustained.](../../deliverables/book/figures/ripping-rhythm.png)

## Chain, file, and the cost of a dull cut

The four days' secret dependency: a ripping chain is either sharp
or it is spending your arms and your fuel, and the difference is
visible in the pile.

A dull chain does not stop cutting. It starts *pushing* — the saw
walks off the line, the kerf widens into a groove, and the bar works
hot while the feller works harder. The kerf is the honest ledger:
every cut is a 0.25-inch-wide line of sawdust, and a dull
chain turns that line wide and ragged, eating the recovery figure
Chapter 13 computed, one cut at a time. The file is
the fix and the cadence is the rule: a few strokes per tooth, every
few tanks, *before* the pushing starts, never after. Touch-up
filing at the bench costs minutes; a chain sharpened only when it
fails costs the afternoon it fails in.

The practical cadence on this build: file at the midday fuel stop,
whether or not the chain asks. The ripping days are the saw's
hardest work in the whole method — bar buried, grain sideways, wet
wood — and the chain that starts the afternoon sharp finishes it
dull. The file is the one tool in the fortnight that is used *more*
than the saw.

## What this is worth an hour

The sidebar's job is to put the four days' labour in the only
currency that survives the fortnight, and then to put the caveat
beside it.

Splitting your own struts is work, and the work has a rate. The
full accounting lives in Chapter 53 — the strut
valued three ways, the disagreement between the ways kept in the
table, and the rate computed against the dressed board that is the
honest comparison. Here the summary, once: an hour at the log
produces members worth more than the hour's alternative at a wage,
*if and only if* the alternative was going to be buying that timber
— and the substitution value is not money anybody has been paid. It
buys groceries only if the timber was going to be bought anyway.

The ripping days feel like the part of the fortnight where the
money is made, and in substitution terms they are — the frame's
timber is the single largest line the build avoids paying. But the
rate is a rate on *your* hours, priced against *your* alternative,
and the chapter that computes it is careful to say which parts of
the comparison flatter the method and which do not. The ripping
chapter's own verdict is narrower: the four days are the price of
the timber, the price is real, and it is paid in afternoons instead
of invoices. Whether that trade is worth it is Chapter
53's question, asked properly, with the numbers up.

---

# 30. Day Nine: The Jig

*One flat bench that makes forty panels the same*

## The Jig

Day nine is the day that pays for the other thirteen, and it is a
single day spent building a single thing: one flat bench on which
every one of the frame's 40 panels will be assembled,
identical to the last.

The jig is where the method stops being a pile of good ideas and
becomes a production line. The wedges are cut, and they are nearly
alike — nearly, because the tree was nearly round, the saw nearly
straight, the eye nearly true. The jig does not need them to be
exact; it needs them to be *held* the same way while the exact
parts — the butt cut, the head length, the screw positions — are
established by the fixture instead of by the eye. A jig is a decision
made once and repeated forty times in wood, and the rest of the
book's build chapters are that sentence, carried out.

The full reference for the fixture — every stage, labelled, straight
from the simulator — is Chapter 33. This chapter is
the story of the day it got built, and the argument for why a day is
the right price.

## Why a jig and not a tape

The temptation on day nine is to skip the bench and measure the
panels one at a time: lay three sticks in a triangle, tape them to
length, screw them. It works — for the first panel. The question is
what the fortieth panel looks like.

A tape measures each panel against an ideal that lives in the
builder's head, and no two measurements of a rough stick agree with
each other. The error compounds quietly: a sixteenth here, a
thirty-second there, and the panels drift apart in ways that only
announce themselves at raising, when two neighbours built a week
apart refuse to share a seam. The frankendome — Chapter
3 — was forty panels built exactly this way,
and its lesson was the whole reason this book exists: **nothing
repeated, so nothing could be checked against the last one.**

A jig inverts the logic. The fixture holds the *position* of every
stick — the triangle's shape, the cut lines, the screw locations —
as physical stops, and the builder's job becomes loading the stick
against the stop. The measurement happens once, when the bench is
built; after that, the panels are not measured at all, they are
*located*, and locating is an operation that cannot drift, because
the stop does not move. Panel forty is built the same way panel one
was, and the only difference between them is the sticks — which is
exactly the difference the method is designed to tolerate.

Repeatability beats accuracy, when there are forty of something. A
jig that is half a degree wrong builds forty panels that agree with
each other, and the frame closes; a tape that is read forty times
builds forty panels that disagree, and the frame does not. Day nine
buys the agreement.

## Building it

The bench, in order, kept deliberately simple — the jig's only
requirement is flatness, and everything else is stops.

**The bench.** A flat surface bigger than the largest panel: two
sawhorses and a sheet of plywood are enough, levelled and braced so
it cannot rock. Flat is the one property that matters, because the
panel's truth is measured against the bench; a bench with a twist
in it builds a twist into every panel that lies on it.

**The triangle.** Lay out one panel full size on the bench — the
actual member lengths from the cut list of Chapter
24, the actual gasket allowance — and screw down
stops along all three outside edges: the triangle's outline, made
of scrap, exactly where the members will lie. The stops are the
jig's first truth, and they are set once, from the drawing, with a
square and a story stick — the tape's last appearance in the whole
fortnight.

**The fences.** Inside the triangle, two more lines of stops mark
where the head ends will be sawn off and where the butt ends land:
the inset of Chapter 15, built as wood instead of
remembered as a number. The fence is the cut line, physical, and
the saw runs along it the way a saw always wishes it could.

**The cut planes.** Where the saw must cut, the jig provides the
plane: a straight edge the bar rides against, set at the butt's
bevel and the head's square. Chapter 34 gives the
angles; the jig holds them; the builder never sights one.

**The clamps.** Whatever holds a wedge still against the stops
while the screws go in — quick clamps, wedges, a foot on a pedal.
The member must arrive at its stops and stay; how it is persuaded
is the builder's business.

The bench takes a day, and the day is not wasted on accuracy — it
is spent on *position*, which is the cheaper thing to be right
about. A stop screwed in the wrong place is moved in a minute. A
panel built in the wrong place is discovered at raising, in front
of a crowd of its neighbours.

![The fixture every panel is built on.](../../deliverables/book/figures/jig-complete.png)

## The jig, complete

The plate is the finished fixture with a panel loaded on it, at the
moment the long heads are being sawn off against the fence — the
stage of the work where the jig's whole argument is visible at
once.

Look at what is holding the truth. The sticks are rough — bark
backs, saw marks, the ordinary drift of split timber. The triangle
they lie in is exact, because the stops are. The butt ends are
where the fence says, the heads run long past the cut plane, and
the saw is about to make the only cuts in the whole panel that have
to be right — and they will be right, because the plane the saw
rides is the plane the bench made, not the plane a hand held.

That is the day-nine bargain, paid in one afternoon and collected
for the rest of the fortnight: from tomorrow, nobody measures
anything. Chapter 31 is the
production run the bench was built for, and the run is the proof —
40 panels, one after another, each one located, cut
and screwed in the same twelve motions, and each one leaving the
bench the same panel.

---

# 31. Days Ten to Twelve: Forty Panels

*The same twelve motions, forty times*

## Forty Panels

Days ten through twelve are the production run the jig was built for:
40 panels, one after another, on one bench, from the
pile of wedges the ripping days left.

The run is where the method's promise becomes arithmetic. One panel
is twelve motions — load, locate, butt, head, drill, screw, flush,
out — and forty panels of twelve motions is a production line with
one worker, one station, and no waiting. The days go wrong only when
the motions stop being the same: a stick sorted into the wrong
class, a marking that washed off in the rain, a panel started before
the jig was checked. This chapter is the procedure, the sorting, and
the two honest notes that make the run real — the fourth panel is
the first good one, and the variant count is smaller than the pile
makes you fear.

## One panel, start to finish

The twelve motions, in order, as they will run forty times. Chapter
33 names the same stages from the simulator's side;
this is the version your hands do.

**Check the bench.** Stops tight, cut planes clean, clamps where the
hand expects them. Thirty seconds, every morning, before the first
panel — the jig's truth is the day's truth.

**Sort the three sticks.** One long member and one short for the
sides, one more of whichever the triangle asks, drawn from the
marked piles of the sorting pass below. The sticks arrive at the
bench already decided; the bench never decides.

And for an isosceles panel, *which* side each stick goes on is fixed:
**the longest — 72.0 inches — is the base, the middle
length (63.5) is the left side, and the shortest
(61.5) is the right.** Lay the base nearest you and the
left member is the longer of the two: it reaches over to the top point,
and the short right side runs up to meet it — and it is that short side the
base butts up to. An equilateral panel is three
long members and has no such rule to remember, which is most of why the
run is quick: 10 of the panels are the easy kind, and the
30 isosceles ones carry the one instruction that must not be
swapped. At the bench the two sides look almost identical — the
difference is two inches over five feet — so the rule is applied by
picking from the marked piles in order, never by eye.

**Load the first side.** Butt against the butt stop, sawn faces down
on the bench, head running long past the head fence. The stop does
the locating; the hand only presses.

**Butt-cut if it is due.** Some classes carry their butt angle from
the saw; some are cut at the bench. Either way the cut is made
against the plane the bench holds, never freehand. Chapter
34 is that cut in full.

**Load the second and third sides.** Each lands against its own
stops, butts to butts, and the triangle assembles itself under the
clamps — three sticks, three overlaps, the pinwheel of Chapter
15 appearing without anybody arranging it.

**Drill and screw the overlaps.** Through the outer stick into the
inner, at the spread the connector chapter established, pulling the
overlap closed. The screws are the only fastening in the whole
panel, and the jig's holes — or the fence's positions — make them
land the same on panel forty as on panel one.

**Flush the heads.** The three heads, still long, are sawn off
against the head fence: one plane, three cuts, and the panel is
exactly the triangle the bench describes. Chapter 35
is why the heads were long in the first place; here it is simply the
last motion of the panel.

**Lift it out.** The panel comes off the bench as one finished
thing — 3 members, their own edges, ready
to be checked, marked and stacked. That is the whole panel. Twelve
motions, measured in minutes once the rhythm is in.

## Sorting the pile

The run only stays a run if the pile does not get to decide
anything at the bench, so the sorting pass happens once, before the
production days, with a marking scheme that survives the weather.

**Sort by class, not by stick.** The four member classes of Chapter
9 each get their own stack, marked at the
cut — a crayon code on the sawn face, one stripe per class, one
colour per tree. The bench pulls from stacks; it never chooses
between sticks.

**Mark the faces, not the ends.** The ends get cut off. The sawn
face is where the mark lives through every operation that follows,
and it is the face that lies *down* on the bench, so the mark also
reads as an orientation cue: mark up, bark up, wrong way round is
visible before the clamp closes.

**Mark with what survives rain.** Lumber crayon, not pencil.
Pencil washes out of wet pine in one night; the crayon is the
marking scheme's entire weatherproofing, and the fortnight has
already had one rain.

**Run the grading passes as you sort.** The four passes of Chapter
19 — bend, knot, bearing, pith — are the same walk
as the sorting pass, and doing them together means every stick
reaches its stack already judged. The 72 spares
the tree chapter promised exist as the reject pile, and the reject
pile is the margin the run spends when a stick misbehaves at the
bench.

## The fourth panel is the first good one

The honest note, stated early so nobody panics on day ten.

The first panel of the run is a rehearsal. The motions are new, the
clamps are in the wrong places, and the result is a panel you
would not hang a roof on. The second panel is faster and nearly
right. The third finds the rhythm and the fourth is the first panel
that comes off the bench exactly the way the fortieth will — and
the run's real output is not forty panels, it is the thirty-seven
that follow the learning curve.

The book says this plainly because the curve is the plan, not a
failure of it. The method's whole claim is that the motions repeat,
and repetition is what turns the first three panels from mistakes
into tuition. The rejected first panels are not waste; they are the
cheapest practice the build will ever get, and they end their lives
as the jig's own test pieces — the panels you cut into, shim, and
check the saw against, so the bench's truth is probed by the very
thing it built.

## Panel variants

The table closes the chapter with the count that keeps the run
sane: how many genuinely different panels the dome contains.

Not forty. The frame's 40 panels are two shapes —
10 equilateral, 30 isosceles, the
counts of Chapter 8
— and each shape is built from the same four member classes, so the
run is two panel variants in a repeating pattern, not forty
originals. The table is generated from the solved dome, and its job
at the bench is the same as every table in this book: the pile looks
like chaos, the count says otherwise, and the count is right.

By the evening of day twelve the run is done: 40
panels, stacked by variant, the reject pile spent, the bench
quiet. The fortnight has two days left, and they are the days the
photographs are taken on — the raising, where forty flat triangles
become the building the next chapter describes.

---

# 32. Days Thirteen and Fourteen: Raising

*Forty panels become one building*

## Raising

The last two days of the fortnight are the ones the photographs are
taken on, and the ones the book is most careful about, because
raising is where a frame that was built flat, checked flat and
trusted flat suddenly has to stand in three dimensions — and where
every error the earlier chapters let through comes home at once.

The job: 40 finished panels become one standing
hemisphere, in an order that lets one person do it, held up by
temporary props until the shell closes and holds itself — and then,
in this book's build, winched off the ground entirely: three cables
from the apex hanger to saddles on the trees, the brake winch doing
the hoisting, and the cabin hanging where the trees hold it, with
no footing under it at all. The order
matters more than the effort. A dome assembled in the wrong order
needs a crane and a crew; assembled bottom ring first, course by
course, it needs one person, a ladder, props and a winch — the same economy
the whole method has been spending since the tree.

The chapter ends with the honest inventory of what a hanging frame
is not, because day fourteen feels like a finish line and the book
has four more parts to go.

## Bottom ring first

The assembly order, which is the whole secret of a one-person
raising.

**Set the ring.** The ten rim panels go down first, laid out flat on
level blocks — or on the footing of Chapter
39, for a build that stays on the
ground; this book's build uses blocks, because its ground work
comes last. The ring is the only part of the frame that
touches the ground, and it is the one part that must be right
before anything rises off it — its 10 edges set
the circle, and the circle sets everything above. Chapter
38 names the ring as the one place error can
accumulate, and the check is done here, at the start, when a wrong
ring is still a morning's fix: measure the ring across its widest
points, both ways, and do not build up until the two numbers agree.

**Course by course.** The next ring of panels sits on the first,
each new panel's base edge meeting the top edge of the panels below
it — the seam, the key, the screws, the same joint the bench built,
now vertical. Work around the dome, not up one side of it: a dome
grows in levels, and a level that closes before the next begins is
the prop that holds the next level. The order is not optional; a
half-raised dome is a sail, and the wind has opinions.

**Key and screw as you go.** Every seam gets its key before the
neighbouring panel arrives — a seam closed later is a seam closed
from a ladder, reaching into a gap that has already tightened. The
keys are the schedule of Chapter 16, and the schedule is
followed in place, panel by panel, the count climbing toward
55.

**Check the ring, again.** At every course, one measurement across
the open top tells you whether the dome is coming up round or
egg-shaped. The check costs a minute and it is the difference
between a raising and a rescue; the dome that fails it today gets
propped today, not discovered tomorrow.

## Propping, and what holds it up before it is closed

The photographs show the finished frame. The raising itself is a
building held up by sticks, and the props are the chapter nobody
writes.

A dome does not carry itself until the top closes. Until the last
panel ties the structure together, every course above the ring is
leaning its weight on the course below it, and the courses below are
leaning on props — lengths of scrap set against the inside, butted
to the ground, holding the shell at its current height. The props
are the temporary structure the method admits it needs: not
scaffolding, not a crew, just the same wood the frame is made of,
pressed into service as furniture.

The prop rule is simple and absolute: **a panel goes up, a prop
goes under it, and a prop never comes out until the course above it
has closed.** The dome rises prop by prop, and the props travel —
pulled from the lower course once the ring above it is screwed
closed, carried up, reset. It looks like chaos in the middle and it
is not: every panel in a properly propped raising has a load path
to the ground at every moment, which is exactly what the finished
frame will have, temporarily, in wood.

The one danger the props exist to prevent is the panel that leans
