out. A panel set at the wrong angle, or a course started before the
course below it closed, will try to fall *outward* — and a falling
panel takes its neighbours' seams with it, opening the dome like a
flower in reverse. The prop is the answer and the order is the
prevention; together they make the raising a sequence of small,
held, stable moments instead of one long gamble.

## Closing the top

The last panel is the hardest, and it is worth saying why before
the raising reaches it.

Every seam closed so far was closed against air — the next panel
could be offered up, adjusted, persuaded. The last panel closes
against a *hole*: every one of its three edges lands on a seam whose
other side is already fixed, and the panel must fit the hole the
whole dome has been building toward for two days. If the ring was
set right, the courses keyed right and the schedule followed, the
hole is exactly the panel — the method has been aiming at this
moment since the first butt cut. If anything drifted, the drift
lives here, in the last triangle of sky.

The order of the last panel's edges matters more than its screws.
Offer the panel up gently, key the lowest seam first, then one side,
and let the last seam close under the panel's own weight — a final
seam that needs forcing is the dome telling you where the error
went, and the answer is never more force; it is a shim behind a
key, or a prop relieved, or, honestly, a panel taken down and the
neighbour adjusted. The method's tolerance budget — Chapter
38 — was built for exactly this conversation,
and the conversation is short: the frame closes, or it does not,
and it almost always closes.

Then the screws go in, the props come out, and the frame stands on
nothing but its own shape — until the winch takes it.

## The winch, and the trees

This book's build does not leave the frame on the blocks. It hangs
it, which is the step the title page promised and this section
reports.

Three cables run from the apex hanger — the mast's lifting ring, or
a temporary sling through the crown on a first build — to saddles
on two of the three trees: two legs to one tree, one to the other,
because a tripod of cables on two anchors is what keeps a hanging
load from spinning. The brake winch does the hoisting, one slow
notch at a time, the frame lifting off its props the way a boat
lifts off its trailer, and the moment the props fall free the cabin
hangs — the trees holding the whole weight of it, the rim swinging
a few feet above the ground, and the builder standing under
something that yesterday was a pile of wedges.

Say it plainly, the way the floating-dome chapter does: this is a
tested construction and a design possibility, not an engineered
rating. The trees, the saddles, the cables, the mast and the winch
all carry real loads, and none of those ratings is in this book —
the engineer's sentence from Chapter 10 binds here
harder than anywhere else in the build. The saddles are saddles,
not holes: straps that spread the load over bark, so the trees
carry the cabin the way they carried their own crowns.

What hanging buys is the deferral the book has been promising: the
ground is now *available* — no footing, no ring beam, no pad is
owed, because the cabin does not touch the earth. The floor clamps
to the mast when it is bought (Chapter 70), the skin
goes on in the air or on a lowered frame, and the foundation, when
it comes, is the last decision of all (Chapter
39). The winch did not just raise the
cabin. It un-scheduled the ground.

![Day fourteen. 120 members, 3 trees.](../../deliverables/book/figures/standing-frame.png)

## Standing

The plate is day fourteen: 120 members, 3 trees, a
fortnight — the frame this book has been describing since the first
chapter, standing where the tree once stood, before the winch took
it up between the other two.

Look at it for what the book wants you to see. No hubs. No mitred
corners. Forty panels that each remember the bench. The seams
running in their two angles, the keys seated, the rim on the
blocks. It is the same object the simulator solved before the first
tree was felled, and the fact that a solved model and a fortnight's
labour can produce the same standing thing — and then hang it —
is the method's whole
argument, in one picture.

And the next section is the book's own correction of this
photograph, because the photograph lies in the way all finished
things lie.

## What was not done

The honest inventory, written while the frame is still warm from
the raising, because day fourteen feels like an end and is not one.

The hanging frame is **not a shell**. It has no skin, no weather
barrier, no insulation — the seams are open to the sky's weather,
and the first real rain will find the book's weakest chapters and
test them. Part Seven — Chapters 40 through
44 — is the work that turns
this photograph into shelter, and it is a season's work, not a
fortnight's. The skin can go on in the air or on a lowered frame;
the order is the builder's, which is the point.

It is **not a home**. No floor but the air under it, no door but
the gap where a panel is missing, no power, no water, no heat. The
frame is
the part of the building the fortnight was sized for; the rest of
the building is sized in months, and the book's later parts are
honest about that calendar.

And it is **not finished** — it is *standing*, or rather *hanging*,
which is the
fortnight's actual deliverable and no more. The dome will move as
its green wood dries, the keys will settle, and the frame the
builder checks on day thirty is not quite the frame of day fourteen.
That settling is a real event in the build's first year, and this book's
habit is to name it, measure it where it can be measured, and leave it in
the frame rather than tune it out.

So the fortnight ends the way it began — with an honest sentence.
3 trees, one saw, fourteen days, 37.5 hands-on
hours: the frame is up, and up between the trees. The rest of the
book is what the frame makes possible, and the first thing it makes
possible is the next chapter, where the method's reference pages
begin.

---

# 33. One Bench, Forty Panels

*What the jig is and what each part of it does*

## One Bench

This is the reference chapter for the fixture itself: what the jig
is, part by part, straight from the simulator that models it, so the
bench on your floor and the bench in the model cannot disagree about
what a jig is.

Chapter 30 told the story of the day it was built.
This chapter is the thing, cold: the twelve stages a panel passes
through on it, the labelled drawing of the fixture before any wood
is on it, and the procedure for laying the whole thing out full size
on a real floor with nothing but string and a pencil. Come back to
this part while you are working, not while you are reading — the
book's reference chapters are written for the bench, and this one
lives there.

## The twelve stages

The table is the jig's whole working life, generated from the
simulator's own stage list: every stage a panel passes through
between the raw pile and the finished triangle, named and ordered.

Read it as a checklist, not as a description. The twelve stages are
the production run of Chapter 31,
expanded to the resolution the bench actually works at — where each
member is located, what gets cut when, what gets screwed when. The
order is not optional in the way a recipe's order is optional; the
head overfit of Chapter 35 only works because the
flush cut happens at the stage the table says, after the panel is
already a panel and before it leaves the bench. A stage skipped is
an error invented, and the table exists so the skip is visible.

![The twelve stages, in order.](../../deliverables/book/figures/jig-stages.png)

## The jig, labelled

The drawing is the fixture before any wood is on it: the bench, the
triangle, the rails, the fences, the cut planes — every component
called out, each with its one job.

**The bench** is the flat truth everything else is measured from.
**The triangle** is the panel's outline, built in stops around the
outside. **The rails** carry the saw where a cut must be made.
**The fences** are the lines the butts land against and the heads
are cut against. **The cut planes** hold the angles of Chapter
34 so no hand has to. Between them they do the one
thing a jig must do: they replace every decision with a position,
and the position does not move.

The drawing comes from the simulator's own jig model — the same
geometry the fabrication package exports — so the picture and the
fixture are the same object, drawn twice. If the bench you build
disagrees with this drawing, one of you is wrong, and the simulator
is not the likely one.

![Bench, triangle, rails, fences, cut planes.](../../deliverables/book/figures/jig-labelled.png)

## Setting it out on a real floor

The full-size layout, because the jig's accuracy is bought on this
afternoon and nothing after it.

**Start with the longest member.** Draw the first side of the
triangle full size, straight off the cut list of Chapter
24 — the actual length, gasket allowance included,
marked on the bench surface with a pencil and checked twice. This
line is the triangle's truth; the other two sides are located from
it.

**Swing the arcs.** From each end of the first line, swing an arc
at the length of its neighbouring side — string and a pencil, the
oldest layout tool there is, and still the straightest for distances
bigger than a square. Where the two arcs cross is the third corner.
The triangle is now on the floor, full size, and the only measured
numbers in it were the three member lengths.

**Check the angles against the drawing.** Before any stop is
screwed down, compare the laid-out triangle against the panel
drawing of Chapter 37: the base angles, the
heights, the relation between the long and short sides. A layout
that matches the drawing within a pencil line is the jig's
foundation; a layout that does not is the cheapest error the whole
build will ever catch.

**Screw the stops.** The outline becomes physical: stops along all
three outside edges, then the fences inside for the butts and the
heads, then the cut planes. Each stop is set against the pencil
line, not against the previous stop — a stop set from a stop
compounds its neighbour's error, and the whole point of the layout
is that every position is measured from the drawing once.

**Prove it with the first panel.** The first panel off the bench is
the test: lay it back over the layout lines, and if its edges fall
on the pencil, the jig is true. The fourth-panel rule of Chapter
31 applies here too — the first
panel proves the bench, and everything after trusts it.

## Locate from the sawn faces, never the bark

The last section of the reference, and the principle that makes a
jig work on natural timber at all.

Bark is irregular. No two sticks share it, it compresses under a
clamp, and it is the one surface of the wedge the saw never touched.
The two sawn faces are the opposite: flat, because the saw made them
flat, and at the same angle on every stick, because every stick is
the same sector of the same circle. So the fixture locates the
member on its sawn faces, and only on its sawn faces. The bark
floats in an oversized clearance and is never asked to locate
anything.

The rule reads as fussiness and is the whole tolerance budget of
the method in one sentence: precision where precision matters, and
tolerance where the tree varies. The sawn faces are the precision;
the bark is the tree varying; and the jig that confuses them builds
forty panels, each as individual as the log it came from — which is
the frankendome, again, wearing a better bench.

Chapter 38 follows the same principle out into
the whole build. This chapter ends it where it starts: one bench,
two datum faces, forty identical panels.

---

# 34. The Butt Cut

*The one compound angle, made before anything is assembled*

## The Butt Cut

Name it honestly at the top, because this project got it wrong in public
once: the butt cut is a compound cut. A mitre and a bevel
together, on one end of every member, made once, off the jig, before
anything is assembled. The claim this book makes is not "no mitres"
— Chapter 15 has the full correction of that claim. The
true claim is better: no member end ever meets another member end,
and the one compound cut the frame does need is made once per
stick, against a fixed setup, instead of twice per stick against a
guess.

This reference chapter is that cut: where its angle comes from,
how few distinct setups the whole dome actually needs, and the
procedure for making it with the allowance still attached. It is
the shortest of the jig chapters and the one the errata pages point at
most, because it is where the book's honesty and the saw's geometry
finally agree.

## The angle its neighbour presents

The butt end of a member is not cut square to the stick's length.
It is cut to match the two facts about the neighbour it will land
on: the neighbour's side leans away from the panel's plane by the
sector's angle, and the panel itself folds toward its neighbour by
the seam's angle. The cut that lands flat against both is a
compound — one angle in the plane of the panel, one across it — and
that compound is the butt cut.

Where the angles come from, precisely: the member's sawn face sits
22.5 degrees off the member's centreline, so
the butt must present that angle to sit flat on its neighbour's
face. The panel's fold adds the seam's angle on top — the
18.03 and 22.46 degrees of Chapter
16. Together they make the one cut the dome cannot avoid,
and the reason it cannot be avoided is the same reason the seam
key exists: the sphere curves, the panels fold, and the wood that
meets that fold must be shaped for it.

The good news, stated as a count: the whole dome needs only a few
distinct setups, not one per member. The bevel is the same
everywhere, and the mitre takes only the values the member classes
demand. The table on the next page is the complete list, generated
from the simulator's own setup schedule — and the fact that the
list is short is the second half of the correction the pinwheel
chapter opened: the compound cut is real, and it is *few*, and
"few, fixed, and made on the jig" is the honest version of the
claim the film got wrong.

## Butt cut setups

The table is the entire saw-setting vocabulary of the build: every
distinct setup, straight from the simulator's own list, with the
member classes each one serves.

Read it as the answer to the question the sceptic is owed: if the
butt is a compound cut, how many compounds are we talking about?
The answer is the table's length — small enough to write on the
wall above the saw, fixed enough that a mistake is a failure to
read, not a failure to calculate. The saw is set once per setup,
the class is cut through, and the setting is never re-derived at
the bench. That is the discipline the whole method runs on:
measure the angles once, on the drawing, and let the jig hold them
forever.

![How few distinct setups the whole dome needs.](../../deliverables/book/figures/butt-setups.png)

## Making it

The procedure, at the saw, with the allowance still attached —
because the butt cut is never made on a stick that is already its
final length.

**Leave the allowance.** The member arrives at the butt cut with
the spare stock of Chapter 35 still on its head
end and the butt allowance still on its butt end. The allowance is
not sloppiness; it is the room the cut needs to be made *against
the jig* rather than against a tape, and it is the reason a
mis-aimed cut is a do-over instead of a ruined member.

**Set the fixture, not the saw alone.** The butt cut is made with
the stick located on its sawn faces in the jig's cut plane — the
saw's own angle settings do the compounding, but the *position* of
the cut along the stick comes from the stop, and the stop is the
jig's. A butt cut made freehand is a compound angle guessed twice;
a butt cut made in the fixture is a compound angle held once.

**Cut, check, and only then cut the class.** The first stick of a
setup is the proof: cut it, offer it to its neighbour, and read
the contact. A butt that sits flat across the neighbour's face is
the setup's certificate; a butt that rocks on one edge sends the
saw setting back one degree and the proof is repeated. Only when
the first stick passes does the class get cut — the same rule as
the jig's first panel, applied to the saw.

**Keep the allowance until the member leaves the bench.** The
butt allowance is removed at the stage the jig schedule says, not
at the saw. Between the butt cut and the bench, the allowance is
still protecting the cut — and the head overfit is still
protecting the length. The two spare ends are the method's
crumple zones, and Chapter 38 is the
accounting of everything they absorb.

One cut, made once, on one end, off the jig. The butt cut is the
whole of the frame's precision joinery, and it fits in a table, a
fixture, and an afternoon — which is what "no two ends ever meet"
was always worth.

---

# 35. The Head Overfit

*Deliberately cutting it too long*

## The Head Overfit

The head overfit is the idea that makes the whole method tolerant,
and it is the single most counter-instinctive rule in the book:
**cut every member deliberately too long.**

A builder trained on dimensional lumber works measure-cut-fit:
measure the stick, cut it to the number, fit it into the building.
Rough timber will not survive that order. The stick is not the
shape the tape assumed — it bows, it tapers, its butt lands where
the neighbour's face happens to be — and a stick cut to the plan's
length before it meets the building is a stick that arrives wrong.
So the method reverses the order: cut long, locate in the jig, and
let the fixture — not the tape — decide where the end actually
falls. The overfit is the deliberate slack that makes the reversal
possible, and this chapter is its economics: where the error goes,
how much to leave, and what the tolerance costs in wood.

## Where error goes

The core argument, in one image: the head end of every member runs
6 inches past its final position, and those
inches are where the build's error goes to die.

Follow the sequence. The member is cut long at the split. It goes
into the jig with its butt located against the stop — the one
position the member must hold — and its head running free, past the
head fence, into the overfit zone. The panel is assembled: the
overlaps screwed, the triangle pulled tight, the neighbours
defining the panel's truth by *being* the panel. Only then — with
everything located, clamped and true — does the saw run along the
head fence and take the overfit off. The cut is made in place,
against the panel the member actually lives in, and the member
ends up exactly the length the *assembly* required, not the length
the tape predicted.

Every error the earlier steps could have introduced — a butt that
landed a quarter inch deep, a neighbour whose face sits proud, the
ordinary drift of split timber — was absorbed before the cut,
because the cut is the last operation and everything before it had
room to be wrong. The overfit is the method's crumple zone:
6 inches of deliberate waste on every member,
in exchange for every member being final-cut against reality.

The sidebar rule that pairs with it, stated once here and used
everywhere after: **locate first, cut second.** The fixture
establishes the cut; the cut never establishes the location. The
maxim is the opposite of how most people are taught to work with a
tape, and it is the sentence that makes rough timber buildable at
all.

![6 inches of deliberate overshoot, sawn off against the fence.](../../deliverables/book/figures/head-overfit.png)

## Long, then flush

The drawing shows the same panel twice: first with all three heads
running long past the fence — the overfit still attached, the
panel already a panel — and then after the flush cut, the heads
sawn down to the fence line, the panel exactly the triangle the
bench describes.

The two states are the chapter's whole teaching. In the first, the
panel is *assembled but not decided*: everything about it is final
except its outside edges, which still carry the slack. In the
second, the slack is gone and the edges are true — true to the
bench, not to a measurement, which is the only truth a panel's
edges can have that its neighbours will agree with.

Notice where the saw is in the drawing: riding the fence, the bar
flat, cutting all three heads on one plane. The cut is a single
motion repeated three times per panel — 3
cuts — and it is the only operation in the whole panel where
accuracy and *finality* meet. Everything before it was reversible;
after it, the panel is committed. The overfit is what bought the
reversibility.

## How much to leave

The overfit's size is a build decision, and the book declares its
own: 6 inches. The number comes from what the
overfit must absorb, and the honest way to size your own is to
count the variation in your own pile.

The overfit must cover: the drift in your splitting, the taper of
your sections, the seating of your butts against the stops, and the
settling of the sticks under the clamps. If the worst stick in
your pile wanders half an inch from the plan's ideal, a half-inch
overfit leaves the good sticks wasting half an inch and the bad
sticks with nothing to spare. So measure the pile, not the plan:
the overfit is set to the *spread* of your stock, plus a margin
for the day you are tired. 6 inches was this
build's spread plus margin; a tighter pile could run less, a
looser one more.

The trade is one-dimensional: more overfit costs wood, less
overfit costs do-overs. The table below prices the wood; the
do-overs price themselves the first time a member arrives at the
head fence already short, which is the one failure the overfit
cannot fix — because the overfit *is* the fix, and a stick cut
short never had any.

## Offcut per dome

The table is the tolerance's bill, and the book prints it the way
it prints every bill: in full, with the units the builder actually
pays in.

Every member sheds its overfit into the offcut pile: the flush
cuts of Chapter 36, one per member,
120 members, adding up to the figure in the table —
board feet of perfectly good wood, cut off and set aside. The
honest note is that the offcut is not waste in the moral sense: it
is blocking, bracing, wedges and firewood, and the method was
sized for it from the start — the tree's spare struts and the
offcut allowance are the same budget, counted in different units.
But it is wood the frame does not stand on, and the table says how
much, because a tolerance that is not priced is a tolerance that
will quietly eat the savings it was bought with.

![The price of the tolerance, in board feet.](../../deliverables/book/figures/offcut-total.png)

The overfit's ledger closes where the chapter began: cut long,
locate, assemble, flush. 6 inches of slack
per member, a table's worth of offcut per dome, and in exchange —
a frame whose every member is final-cut against the panel it
actually lives in. That is the cheapest accuracy there is, and the
next chapter is the cut that collects it.

---

# 36. Flush-Cutting in Place

*The cut that makes the panel true*

## Flush-Cutting

The flush cut is the final operation on the jig and the moment the
panel becomes itself: the three overfit heads of Chapter
35, sawn off against the fence, in one plane, so
the assembled triangle ends up exactly the triangle the bench
describes.

It is a small cut and the book gives it a chapter anyway, because
the order of operations it completes is the method's entire
tolerance argument, and because the cut is the one place a hurried
builder can undo, in thirty seconds, the half-inch of slack the
whole design paid for. The chapter has two sections: how the cut is
made, and why it cannot be made any earlier — the second being the
reason the first is written at all.

## Against the fence

The procedure, with the saw held the way it has to be held —
against the fence, never freehand.

**The panel stays clamped.** The flush cut is made on the
assembled, screwed, clamped panel — the panel does not come off the
bench for the cut, because the bench is what makes the cut true.
The clamps hold the members against their stops; the saw rides the
fence; the two truths together are the cut.

**The bar rides the plane, flat.** The saw is laid on the fence's
cut plane, bar flat to the bench, and drawn through the three
heads in one motion where the fence allows it. The saw does not
sight anything; it follows the plane the bench already holds,
which is the only way a hand-held saw makes a square cut at all.

**Cut all three heads on the same plane.** The three heads stick
past the fence by different amounts — the panel is a triangle, and
its overfits land where they land — but the *cut line* is one
plane, and all three cuts are made to it. That single plane is the
panel's final edge geometry, and a panel whose three heads were
cut to three planes is a panel whose neighbours will measure the
difference.

**Do not finish the stick, finish the panel.** The temptation at
the saw is to clean each head as it is cut — a shave here, a
touch-up there. Resist it: the flush cut's product is the panel's
truth, not three pretty ends. The offcuts go to the pile, the
panel goes to the stack, and the next panel takes its place. The
cut exists once per panel, not once per stick, and treating it as
a production step is what keeps the run of Chapter
31 at its pace.

## Why this cannot be done first

The order is not arbitrary, and the reason is the whole tolerance
argument in miniature.

The flush cut cannot be made before assembly, because before
assembly there is nothing for the cut to be true *to*. A member cut
to its final length at the split is a member cut against a tape's
idea of where its neighbours will be — and its neighbours, when
they arrive, will be wherever the wood actually is. The panel is
the only object that knows the member's true length, and the panel
only exists after the butts are located, the overlaps screwed and
the clamps closed. So the cut must come last: it is the final
measurement, and the fixture performs it.

It cannot be made *after* the panel leaves the bench, either —
off the jig, the panel's own edges are the only reference the saw
has, and a saw referenced to the thing it is cutting is a saw
cutting in circles. The bench's fence is the fixed truth; the
panel's edges are the variable; and the order that works is the
one where the fixed truth cuts the variable.

So the sequence stands as the book has built it: split long, butt
against the stop, assemble, flush against the fence. Cut long,
locate, assemble, cut true. The flush cut is the last word of the
sentence, and the sentence is the method — every member of every
panel final-cut in place, against the only truth that could know
it.

---

# 37. Panel Drawings

*One flat drawing per distinct panel, full size*

## Panel Drawings

The book's last word on the panel is a drawing, not a paragraph —
one flat, full-size drawing per distinct panel, exported directly
from the simulator, so the drawing on the page and the panel on
the bench are the same object.

The drawings are the check for everything the jig chapters
described. The jig locates members against stops; the drawing is
what the stops were set from. The panels come off the bench; the
drawing is what they are measured against before they are stacked.
And because the drawings export from the same solved model that
produced the cut list and the seam schedule, the whole build has
one source of truth, and it is printed on these pages.

## Panel drawing, type one

The first of the two distinct panels, flat, full size, exactly as
the simulator exports it — the member lengths, the overlaps, the
vertex gap, the seam allowance all in their true positions.

![Panel type one, flat, full size.](../../deliverables/book/figures/panel-1.png)

## Panel drawing, type two

The second distinct panel. Between them, the two drawings describe
every one of the frame's 40 panels: the
10 of one type, the 30 of the other,
the counts of Chapter 8.

![Panel type two, flat, full size.](../../deliverables/book/figures/panel-2.png)

## Reading a panel drawing

What every line on the drawing is, so the page can be read at the
bench and not only admired.

**The outline** is the panel's finished edge — the triangle the
neighbours will meet, gasket allowance included. **The member
lines inside it** are the three sticks: butts located on sides,
heads running to the fence line, the pinwheel's overlaps shown as
the overlaps they are. **The vertex gap** is the empty corner of
Chapter 15 — drawn, not described, so nobody looks
for a hub there. **The seam allowances** are the half-gasket
insets at every edge, the 0.75-inch key's share of
each side, which is why the outline is slightly inside the
triangle the members describe.

The drawings print full size: exported as vectors, they scale to
the bench without loss, and a printed drawing laid on the jig's
surface should match the jig's stops line for line. That is their
real job — not illustration but *witness*. When a panel comes off
the bench, it is laid on its drawing; when a jig is first set,
the drawing is what the stops are checked against; and when the
frame finally closes, every panel in it has, somewhere in its
history, been held up against one of these two pages and found to
be the thing the pages describe.

The reference part of the book ends here, with the drawings —
because after the drawings there is nothing left to know about
the panel. The next chapters leave the bench and follow the frame
to the ground it stands on.

---

# 38. Where Error Goes

*A tolerance budget for a building made of split logs*

## Where Error Goes

Every chapter so far has mentioned the method's tolerance — the
overfit absorbing a sixteenth, the key absorbing the fold, the
panel owning its edges. This chapter is the budget behind the
mentions: a complete account of where error can land in a frame
built from split logs, which of those places the method absorbs,
and which one it does not.

A building method that cannot say where its error goes is a method
that discovers the answer at raising. This book's method can say
it, because it was designed around the saying: every operation in
the fortnight either sends its error somewhere specific or refuses
to make the cut that would. The chapter walks the four landing
places — stock length, butt angle, seam gap, and the ring — marks
three of them as absorbed and one as accumulating, and ends with
the check that keeps the last one honest. It is the most
structural chapter in the book, in both senses of the word.

## Four places error can land

The complete list, because a tolerance budget that omits a landing
place is a budget with a hole in it.

**Stock length.** The member arrives at the bench longer than it
should be — the split drifted, the bucking was generous, the plan
was optimistic. This is the commonest error in the build and the
cheapest, because the overfit exists for exactly it: the head runs
long, the assembly locates the butt, and the flush cut of Chapter
36 removes the error with the offcut.

**Butt angle.** The compound cut of Chapter 34 lands
a degree off, and the butt no longer sits flat on its neighbour's
face. The error is real but bounded: the butt's job is to present
area to the side, and a butt a degree off still presents nearly
all of it — the screws pull the overlap closed, and the contact
settles into the wood. What the method refuses is the opposite
error: a butt cut *short*, which is why the allowance stays on
until the bench decides.

**Seam gap.** Two panels meet and the gap between them is a
fraction wider or narrower than the key's share — the fold is
right but the wood is itself. The seam absorbs it by design: the
key compresses, the gasket gives, and the 55 seams of
the frame share the difference so no single seam carries it all.
This is the landing place the whole seam system was built for, and
the one the book spent its middle chapters engineering.

**The ring.** The base ring is where the frame meets the ground,
and it is the one landing place that does not give. A sixteenth of
error in the ring is a sixteenth of error in the circle the whole
dome stands on, and the dome cannot compress its way out of a
wrong foundation. The ring is the subject of the last section,
because it is the budget's one honest failure point.

## Three of them are absorbed

Why three of the four land safely, and what "absorbed" actually
means in wood.

An absorbed error is one that leaves the build as something else:
offcut, compression, distribution. The stock-length error leaves as
the flush cut's offcut — Chapter 35 priced it, and
the price is the board feet in its table. The butt-angle error
leaves as contact: the overlap's screw row pulls the butt into the
wood until the bearing is full, and a degree of bevel error
becomes a fraction of an inch of settlement that the frame never
sees again. The seam error leaves as compression: the key takes
up the difference, the gasket breathes it, and the dome closes
with the error spread invisibly across 55 joints.

The three share one property: each is absorbed at the *place it
was made*, by the design of that place, without travelling. The
stock error never leaves the bench. The butt error never leaves
the joint. The seam error never leaves the seam. That locality is
the whole secret — an error that dies where it is born cannot
compound with its neighbours, and a frame built from rough wood
needs exactly that: forty small absorpions instead of one long
accumulation.

![Error, and where the method sends it.](../../deliverables/book/figures/tolerance-budget.png)

## The tolerance budget

The drawing is the budget as a flow: error enters at the four
places, three of them send it sideways into absorption — the
offcut pile, the bearing, the gasket — and one of them, the ring,
passes it on.

Read the arrows as the method's real structure, because they are.
Every chapter of the build is somewhere on this drawing: the
overfit is the stock-length arrow, the screw row is the butt
arrow, the key is the seam arrow, and the ring is the arrow with
no exit. A builder who has internalised the drawing stops fearing
the ordinary errors of rough timber — they are budgeted — and
starts fearing only the one the drawing marks in the colour of
warning.

## The one that accumulates

The ring, named and checked, because it is the budget's single
honest failure point.

The base ring's error does not absorb; it *accumulates*. The ring
is a closed circle of 10 panels, and an error
anywhere in it is an error everywhere: the dome above it rises
from whatever circle the ring actually is, and every course
multiplies the ring's truth by the frame's own height. A ring set
a half inch out of round is a dome that leans a half inch at the
base and more at the crown — the one error in the method that
grows as the building does.

So the ring is where the book's checking discipline concentrates.
The check is simple and it is done three times: when the ring is
first set, measure across every opposite pair of its
10 sides — the numbers must agree within the
gasket's give, and any pair that does not is the ring telling you
where it is wrong while the fix is still a shim. When the first
course closes, measure the ring again — the courses above have
already begun leaning on it, and a ring that moved has moved the
whole frame's opinion of round. And before the top closes, once
more, from outside: the dome's last chance to be told, cheaply,
what the raising is about to make permanent.

The ring's honesty is the book's last word on tolerance. The
method forgives stock, forgives butts, forgives seams — and it
never forgave the ring, because the ring is the one place where
forgiveness would have to come from the ground. Chapter
39 gives the ring its footing; this
chapter gave it its warning. Build accordingly.

---

# 39. The Footing and the Ring

*What the dome stands on*

## The Footing

The frame is up — hanging, in this book's build, between the trees
— and the question of what it stands on, if it ever stands, is the
one the fortnight deliberately left open: the ground work comes
last, and it may never come at all. This chapter is the footing for
the day it does — the part of the building under everything, the
part the photographs never show, and the part where the book's own
warning from Chapter 38 is most literally true:
the ring's error accumulates, and the footing is the ring's floor.

A dome's weight arrives at the ground along one circle — the base
ring's 10 edges, ten points where the panels meet
the earth. The footing's whole job is to receive that circle and
hold it: level, round, and against the wind that wants to lift the
whole thing away. The chapter covers the two ways to do it, the
layout that keeps the circle true, the uplift a dome invites, and
the point where the book stops and the engineer starts.

## Ten points, or one ring

Two approaches carry a dome's weight to the ground, and the book
recommends one of them for its own build while pricing both.

**Ten points.** Each of the ring's ten base vertices sits on its
own small footing — a pad block, a pier, a driven post — and the
frame bridges between them. It is the cheapest material answer, it
works where the ground is firm and level, and it leaves the middle
of the circle empty — no concrete where nothing stands. Its cost is
precision: ten separate points must land in one plane and one
circle, or the ring that spans them comes out twisted, and Chapter
38 has already explained what a twisted ring
does to everything above it.

**One ring.** A continuous footing under the whole base ring — a
ring beam of concrete, or a wooden ring on blocks — receives the
dome's weight as one circle and spreads it as one circle. It costs
more material than ten points, and it is *forgiving* in exactly the
way ten points are not: the ring beam carries the frame's local
imperfections around the circle instead of exposing them at every
vertex. The book's own build stands on the continuous answer,
because the build's error budget — the chapter just spent on it —
is worth more than the concrete it saves.

The honest note, kept from the dome books that skip it: a dome
puts its whole weight on the *outside* of its circle. Pouring a
slab across the middle is concrete nothing stands on — the centre
of the floor carries no frame load at all, and the money it costs
is money the ring beam could have used.

![The base ring from above: ten edges on the ground circle, nothing in the middle.](../../deliverables/book/figures/ring-plan.png)

Chapter 64
prices the two properly when the dome graduates to a permanent
pad; here the rule is only: foot the circle, not the disc.

## Setting out a circle accurately

The layout, because the footing's truth is the ring's truth, and
the ring's truth is the whole frame's.

**One stake, one string.** The dome's centre goes in first: a
stake driven where the middle of the floor will be, and it never
moves again for the rest of the build — every measurement of the
footing, the ring and the raising is taken from it. Then a string
the length of the dome's radius, a loop at each end, and the
circle is drawn the way circles have been drawn since before
anybody wrote this down: walk the string around the stake, marking
as you go.

**Set the ten points from the circle.** The ring's ten base
vertices sit on the circle at equal spacing, found by stepping a
second string — the chord between neighbouring vertices — around
the circumference and adjusting until the last step lands exactly
on the first. The step-and-adjust is the check: a ten-point layout
that closes on itself with the last step is round; one that misses
its starting mark by an inch is telling you where the circle
isn't.

**Check the circle before anything is poured.** Measure across
every opposite pair of points — the diameters — and demand they
agree. Then check the level of the ten points against a water
level or a laser, and shim until they share one plane. The checks
are the chapter's whole discipline: a footing that is round and
level on the day it is made is the only thing in this part of the
build that cannot be fixed later, so it is checked the way things
that cannot be fixed later are checked — twice, from different
directions, before the concrete is mixed.

## Anchoring against uplift

The sentence the footing chapter must say plainly, because the
shape itself refuses to let it be skipped: **a dome is a wing.**

A hemisphere is a lifting body. Wind over the crown does to the
dome what it does to an aircraft: pressure drops on the curved top,
and the whole building feels a pull toward the sky. The frame's
own weight resists — green pine is heavy, and the skin chapters
add their share — but a dome that is only *sitting* on its footing
is a dome the right wind can pick up by the brim, and the stories
of domes that went rolling are not folklore. The footing's job is
therefore two jobs: hold the weight *down*, and hold the building
*down against the wind*.

The anchor is the ring's connection to the ground, and it is not
optional. Every base vertex — or the ring beam at intervals —
needs a mechanical tie into the footing: a cast-in anchor, a
bracket, a strap over the ring member. The tie's job is uplift,
not weight, and it is sized for the worst wind the site's records
hold, which is exactly the calculation the last section of this
chapter hands to somebody else. The book's own build used straps
at every base point, and the straps are the one part of the
footing the book will not let a reader improvise.

## This is where to ask somebody

The safety page, and it is shorter than the chapter because the
book's rule from the front matter simply applies here with full
force.

Footings are load and soil, and neither is a matter of carpentry.
The dome's weight, the wind's uplift, the ground's bearing
capacity, the frost line, the drainage — every one of them is a
site-specific number that belongs to someone who can read the site
and the code together. This book tells you the *shape* of the
footing — a circle, not a disc; anchored, not sitting; checked,
not assumed. It does not tell you how deep, how wide, or how much
steel, because those answers come from your ground, not from a
book that has never seen it.

The division of labour is the one Chapter 10 drew for
loads, applied to the earth: the geometry is the book's, the
engineering is the engineer's, and the boundary between them is a
page you cannot miss. Build the circle the book describes. Have
the circle's ground signed off by someone who can. Then, and only
then, stand the frame on it.

And remember the order the title page promised: the hanging cabin
never owed this chapter anything. The foundation is the last
decision of the build, made when the cabin finally comes down from
the trees — and the chapters that follow this one are the reason
the decision could wait.

---

# 40. Closing the Shell

*Forty triangles of something over forty triangles of nothing*

## Closing the Shell

The standing frame is a skeleton, and this chapter is its skin:
40 triangles of something, over 40
triangles of nothing, each panel closed so the weather stays on
the outside.

The options are few and the book ranks them the way it ranks
everything — by cost and behaviour, computed, not by feel. Sheet
goods dominate, because they are cheap, local and flat, and the
chapter's first two sections are the craft of cutting forty
triangles out of rectangles without wasting half the stack. The
table prices the whole skin in sheets. The last section runs the
alternatives — fabric, shingle, living roof — because each one
exists for a reason and each one asks the frame for something
different, and the builder who knows all four picks the right one
instead of the first one.

## Sheathing a triangle

The craft problem the rectangle-building world never has: every
panel of a dome is a triangle, and sheet goods come in rectangles.

The answer is the panel drawing, again. Chapter
37 printed the two panel shapes flat and full
size; the sheathing job is those shapes, cut in sheet, one per
panel, with the seam allowance carried around the edge. The
procedure that keeps the waste honest:

**One template, not forty measurements.** Trace each panel shape
once onto scrap, cut it, and use it as the pattern for its whole
class — the 10 of one shape, the 30 of
the other. The template is the sheathing's jig: the shape made
physical, so the saw never re-derives an angle it already cut
correctly once.

**Cut inside the line, then trim to fit.** Sheet cut to the
template's outside is always slightly big; sheet cut inside it can
be trimmed with a knife in place. The panel gets its skin offered
up, marked against its own frame, and trimmed to the frame's
actual edges — locate first, cut second, the same maxim the jig
chapters ran, applied to skin.

**Seam allowance stays outside the panel.** The skin's edge
belongs to the *seam*, not to the panel: the gasket's share, the
flashings, the overlap of the next panel's skin — all of it lives
beyond the frame's own edge, and a skin cut flush to the frame is
a skin that leaves the seam naked. Cut generous, trim at the seam,
and the 55 seams get the overlap the weather chapter
demands.

**Screw to the frame, not through it.** The skin fastens into the
members' faces — the wide flat of the wedge — at the spacing the
sheet's own spec asks. The skin is not structural; it is the
panel's face, and its screws' only job is to keep it flat against
the frame while the seams do the weather work.

## Nesting the cuts

The second craft problem, and it is pure geometry: how to fit two
triangles into one rectangle.

The two panel shapes of the dome, paired point-to-point along
their long sides, nest inside a standard sheet with the waste
reduced to two thin edge strips — the arrangement the table below
assumes. The nesting works because the panels were *not* sized to
the sheet and the sheet was not sized to the panels; they meet in
the middle, and the meeting wastes less than either feared.

The honest limit, stated because the film that discovered it made
a point of it: this dome's panels do not fit a standard sheet
*singly* — one panel is wider than half a sheet, so the naive
layout of one-panel-per-sheet wastes nearly half the stack. The
nested pair is the fix, and it is why the chapter treats the two
shapes as a pair rather than as forty separate cuts. Where the
nesting does not reach — the odd counts, the rim panels with
their different edge — the waste goes to the offcut pile and the
pile goes to blocking, the same honest destination as every other
offcut in the book.

## Sheet count

The table is the skin's bill: shell area and sheets, computed
from the solved geometry, not estimated from the drawing.

The numbers are the answer to the question the chapter opened —
what it costs, in sheets, to close 40 triangles. The
area is the dome's own surface, the sheet count is the nested
layout's requirement, and the difference between the two is the
waste the nesting section promised to keep honest. The figure is
worth reading twice, because it is the first number in the book
that is *bought* rather than *cut* — everything until now came out
of the trees, and the skin is where the fortnight's ledger first
touches a lumberyard.

![Shell area and sheets, from the solved geometry.](../../deliverables/book/figures/sheet-count.png)

## The alternatives

Sheet goods are the default, not the only answer. The other three
exist for other builds.

**Fabric.** A membrane skin — canvas, shade cloth, the soft
shells of Chapter 69 — closes the frame for the
price of cloth and rope, sheds rain where the seams are taped, and
gives up insulation and years. It is the right skin for a
seasonal dome, a greenhouse's first year, or a frame that is
still deciding; it is the wrong skin for a home, and the book
says so rather than romancing the tent.

**Shingle.** Shingling a compound surface is slow, and it works —
each shingle is small enough to follow the curve, the overlaps
shed water the way the frame's ridges want, and the result is the
most repairable roof there is: one shingle at a time, forever.
The cost is the labour, which is why shingle is the upgrade for
the dome that has already earned its place, not the first skin it
wears.

**Living roof.** Soil and plants over a waterproof layer, the
heaviest and coolest of the four, and the one that asks the most
of the frame: the chapters on load have already said who decides
whether the members carry it. It is listed because it is real and
because somebody always asks; it is not recommended for the first
build, and the reason is the engineer's sentence, not the
gardener's.

The chapter closes where the shell does: the frame is covered,
the seams are taped, and the dome is a *room* in the only sense
that matters outdoors — it keeps weather on its outside. The next
chapter cuts the holes that make it usable, and the chapter after
that is the one that decides whether the shell's honesty holds up
to the weather it was built for.

---

# 41. Doors, Windows and the Rim

*Cutting holes in something that works by being closed*

## Doors and Windows

Every building needs holes, and a dome is the building that resents
them most: its strength is its closure, its panels are its
structure, and a hole cut wrong does not just let the weather in —
it un-triangulates the frame at the exact place the frame was
leaning on itself.

So this chapter is the craft of opening a dome without breaking
it. The principle, stated once and used throughout, is the one the
wedge book's own chapter taught: **an opening is a panel you did
not close** — take a whole panel out and the frame loses nothing
structural, because the panel was always its own independent unit.
Cut *into* a panel, and the triangle stops being a triangle, and
the load that was travelling through it now has nowhere to go. The
chapter runs the openings in order of mercy: the door at the rim,
the window in a panel, the risers that cheat height — and the
drawing that shows all three without a single un-triangulated
triangle.

## A door at the rim

The easy case, and the book says so plainly: the door belongs at
the rim, because the rim is the one place a hole costs the frame
nothing.

The base ring's 10 edges join nothing — Chapter
8 separated them
from the seams for exactly this reason. Leave one rim panel out
entirely, and the gap is a doorway: the panels on either side are
complete, their seams close as they always did, and the dome loses
a panel that was never structural in the way the crown panels are.
The door is framed the way the missing panel was framed — two
jambs running the panel's full height, a header across its top
edge — and the frame's own triangle does the lintel's work,
because the triangle above the door is still a triangle.

The one requirement is the header's honesty: the panels above the
door lean their weight down through the ring, and the opening
interrupts the ring. The header takes that interruption — a beam
across the top of the opening, tied to the jambs, carrying the
ring's continuity across the gap. In a full-height rim door this
header is small, because the ring is low and the loads above it
are modest; in a taller opening the engineer's sentence from
Chapter 10 reappears, and rightly. The rule for the
chapter: take the whole panel, frame the gap, header the ring.

## A window in a panel

The harder case, and the craft is keeping the triangle a triangle.

A window wants light where the wall leans — mid-panel, high on
the dome — and a hole in the middle of a triangle is exactly what
the frame's logic forbids: the triangle's load runs around its
edges, and a hole in its middle interrupts the members that were
carrying it. So the window is not cut into a panel; it *replaces*
part of a panel, and the part it replaces is re-triangulated
around the hole.

The procedure is the panel's own logic, applied smaller: frame
the window opening inside the panel with members that run from
the window's corners to the panel's edges — a smaller triangle
above the window, two beside it, one below — so every region of
the panel is still triangulated, and the panel's loads still
travel along members, now detouring around the glass. The window
unit itself is fixed into its own sub-frame, flashed to the
panel's skin, and the panel remains one independently-built,
independently-lifted thing — the property Chapter
18 paid for, spent here on daylight.

The honest cost, said aloud: a windowed panel is a panel built
twice — once as the triangle, again as the triangle-with-a-hole
— and its member count rises accordingly. That is why the
windows go high and few, where the glazing earns its keep in a
leaning wall and the view, rather than everywhere the floor
plan's habit would put them.

## Three openings that work

The drawing collects the chapter: the rim door, the windowed
panel, and the risers option of the next section — three ways to
open the dome, each one keeping the frame's triangulation intact.

Read the drawing against the rule it illustrates. The door takes
a whole panel and frames the gap; the window sub-frames inside a
panel and re-triangulates around the glass; the risers lift the
whole shell and put the openings in a *straight* wall, where
doors and windows stop being dome problems at all. Three openings,
three strategies, one invariant — no hole ever interrupts a load
path without a smaller triangle being drawn around it.

![Three ways to open a dome without un-triangulating it.](../../deliverables/book/figures/openings-three.png)

## Risers: cheating height at the rim

The third strategy is not an opening but an avoidance: raise the
whole dome onto a short vertical wall — a riser, a knee wall, a
stem wall — and the openings stop fighting the curve.

A riser is a straight cylinder of wall under the hemisphere's
brim: knee-high for a shed door, waist-high for furniture to
lean against, full-height where the building wants ordinary
windows and an ordinary door. The dome above it is unchanged —
the same frame, the same panels, the same seam schedule — and the
wall below it is ordinary stud framing, which is the entire
point: the expensive part of the building keeps its shape, and
the parts that want rectangles get rectangles.

What the riser costs is the ring beam's reappearance: the dome's
base ring now sits on a wall instead of on the ground, the wall
needs a top plate to receive it, and the wall's own top must be
as round as the ring it carries — the same layout discipline as
Chapter 39, moved up one storey.
What it buys, Chapter 57 prices in full: usable
floor at the edge, headroom where the dome's wall leans hardest,
and the cheapest square footage in the building, bought by the
foot of wall height.

The book's order of operations closes the chapter: frame, shell,
then openings — and if the openings want a straight wall, the
riser is the answer, because it is cheaper to lift a whole dome
than to argue with forty curved triangles one window at a time.

---

# 42. Weather and Water

*Where it leaks, and where the water goes*

## Weather and Water

The honest chapter about domes and rain, because the dome's
reputation was made by this subject and the subject deserves the
book's full discipline.

The title page inverted the usual order on purpose: the frame is
the part you are allowed to get wrong, and the skin is not — a
frame out of line stands for fifty years, and a skin that leaks one
winter takes the frame with it. This chapter is the skin's side of
that bargain, and it is the reason the book treats the seams here
with more care than anywhere else.

Here is the fact the frame chapters have been circling: a dome's
roof is one surface made of 40 flat panels meeting
at 55 interior seams, and every seam is a place water
can get in. A rectangle concentrates its roof's risk in two
valleys and a ridge; the dome spreads the same risk across
55 lines. The answer is not fifty-five seals; it is
the two principles this chapter teaches — shed, don't seal; and
where water must be managed, manage it deliberately, at the rim.
The chapter closes with the inside face, because the dome that
survives the rain outside can still be ruined by the water its own
breathing makes inside, and the book says that before the reader
discovers it in month three.

## Every seam is a potential leak

Say the number, because the number is the strategy:
55 seams. Fifty-five lines on the outside of the
building where two panels meet, fifty-five keys, fifty-five
weather details — and the honest way to think about them is not
"one roof" but "55 small roofs, all meeting".

The frame chapters already did the structural half of each seam:
the key locates the geometry, the gasket takes the tolerance.
The weather half is a different question, and it is the one that
decides whether the building lasts. A seam that fits is not
automatically a seam that sheds — water moves downhill, and a
seam's fit matters only where the water actually runs. The
dome's seams run in two families: the steep ones near the crown,
where water moves fast and the seam's own slope carries it away,
and the shallow ones near the rim, where water lingers, pools in
the key's shadow, and waits for a gap. The strategy follows the
slope: the crown seams need alignment, the rim seams need
*weathering* — a drip edge, an overlap, a path that takes the
water off the seam before it can sit on it.

The count is the discipline. A builder who treats
55 seams as one repeated detail does the same
detail wrong 55 times; a builder who separates them
into the two families the geometry gives — the steep and the
shallow — has two details to perfect and a schedule to repeat
them by. Chapter 16 is the schedule. This chapter is
the weather it has to survive.

![One channel, many jobs -- a frame of the seam-climate film.](../../deliverables/book/figures/weather-channel.png)

## Shedding, not sealing

The principle that actually works on a compound surface, and the
reason "waterproof" is the wrong word for what this book builds.

A sealed surface tries to stop water at the membrane — one
barrier, perfect, everywhere. Sealing fails on a dome because a
dome moves: the frame dries, the panels settle, the seams open
and close with the seasons, and a seal that was perfect in July
is a cracked line by January. The answer the roof-building world
already knows — and shinglers have known for centuries — is to
stop trying to hold the water back and instead *hand it
downhill*: every surface overlaps the one below it, every seam
wears a shingle, and the water is always on top of the roof,
never inside it.

So the dome is weathered as 55 little roofs, each
seam flashed like a valley: the upper panel's skin laps over the
lower's, the key is protected rather than depended on, and the
seam's own slope is the drainage. The skin chapters supplied the
panels; this chapter supplies the lapping order; and the result
is the honest claim the book makes about weather: not "the dome
never leaks" — no roof can promise that — but "where water
reaches the seam, the seam hands it down, and the lowest seam
hands it off the rim". Shedding is a path, not a promise, and
paths can be walked, checked and repaired — which seals, once
breached, cannot.

## Harvesting it instead

The one place the shape helps with water, and it is not small:
a dome is a very good rain collector.

Every drop that lands on the shell runs down the panels to the
brim, and the brim is a circle — one continuous gutter, already
built, waiting only for the collection the next chapter's
hardware provides. Chapter 58 computes the annual
catch: the footprint, the local rainfall, a declared efficiency,
and the answer comes out in thousands of gallons a year off a
building this size. The dome does not merely *tolerate* rain; it
gathers it, and the gathering is free — the roof was already a
gutter, the shape was already a funnel, and the only purchase is
the tank.

The honest note, kept from the films that made this point: the
catch is not drinking water without treatment. The filter makes
it clear; the lamp makes it safe; neither does the other's job —
and the book's water system, when Chapter
44 reaches it, is a
first-flush diverter and a UV lamp downstream of the tank, not a
rain barrel and optimism. For everything else — irrigation,
flushing, washing, the buffer against a dry well — the catch is
as good as the roof, and the roof is the dome's.

## Condensation and the inside face

The last section is the problem people discover in month three,
and it is worth a whole section because it is not rain.

A dome is warm inside and cold outside, and the meeting of the
two happens *inside the skin*: moist room air reaches a cold
surface, the water condenses, and the builder who sealed every
outside seam perfectly wakes up to water running down the inside
of the shell. The seams were not the leak. The air was.

The answer is the seam channel, and it is the reason the later
chapters of this book engineer it deliberately rather than
sealing it away: the cavity between the frame's wedges is the
one continuous void that connects the whole shell, and it is
exactly where condensation belongs — collected, drained at the
rim, vented at the crown. The full climate system — the dew
points, the levels, the drawer, the plate — is Chapters
81 and 82, and they are the book's
real answer to the inside face. Here the principle is enough:
**never seal the inside face against the air; give the water a
path, and drain it at the rim.** The dome that leaks from rain
failed its seams. The dome that leaks from condensation was
built by someone who thought the inside face was not part of
the weather.

The chapter ends where every weather chapter should: outside,
shed; inside, drain; at the rim, catch. The dome is not a box,
and its water is not a nuisance to be sealed away — it is a
flow to be directed, and the direction is downhill, through
55 honest seams, off the brim, into the tank.

---

# 43. Inside a Round Room

*Living with curves*

## Inside a Round Room

This is the chapter about living in the thing, written the way the
rest of the book is written: the numbers first, the compromises
named, and the romance earned rather than assumed.

A round room is not a rectangular room with curved wallpaper. It
changes where you can stand, how furniture fits, how sound
behaves, and where the light falls — and the changes are
measurable. This chapter measures them: the honest floor, the
standing-height map, the furniture problem and its real
solutions, and the acoustic quirk a hemisphere brings for free.
It is the chapter to read *before* building, because the fixes it
describes are layout decisions that cost nothing on paper and a
renovation once the walls are up.

## Where the usable floor really is

The honest number, because the floor a dome advertises and the
floor a person can use are different things.

The dome's plan area — 365 square feet for this
build — counts the whole circle. But the shell meets the floor at
a tangent: the wall leans inward from the rim, and the last ring
of floor around the edge has almost no headroom over it. The
usable floor is the smaller circle where the ceiling is over
your head, and the difference between the two numbers is the
dome's real estate tax — floor you paid for, in frame and skin,
that only a dog can use.

The map on the next page draws the honest version: the full
circle, the standing-height contour inside it, and the dead ring
between. The dead ring is not waste in the moral sense — it is
where low storage, benches and beds belong, the furniture that
does not need to stand up — but it is real, and the chapter
states it because the alternative is the surprise every dome
builder watches a visitor have: they step in, they look up, and
they say the roof is lower than the picture suggested. The
picture suggested the plan area. The roof was always the
geometry.

The fix the book already spent a chapter on is the riser of
Chapter 57: lift the shell a few feet and the
tangent moves outward, the dead ring shrinks, and the floor
becomes genuinely usable to the wall. The round-room chapter
recommends it for exactly this reason — not for looks, but
because the standing-height map improves more per dollar than
anything else in the whole fit-out.

## Standing height, mapped

The map is the chapter's central object: a plan of the
365-square-foot floor, coloured by headroom,
with the standing-height contour drawn where the ceiling passes
head height.

Read the map as a furniture plan before it is a chart. The
central disc is the room: full height, 10.8 feet at
the crown, the space where a person stands, works, cooks. The
ring between the contour and the rim is the compromise zone:
overhead clearance shrinking smoothly to nothing at the wall,
exactly the profile a bed, a bench, a desk-with-a-view or a row
of shelves wants. And the rim itself is the dead zone the
previous section named — reachable, useful, not standable.

The map also settles the oldest argument in dome living: where
the bed goes. The answer is where the map says — in the ring,
head toward the wall, because the only dimension a sleeping
person needs at the wall is the one the dome has plenty of:
length. The furniture section follows the same logic for
everything else.

![365 square feet of floor. Rather less of it lets you stand up.](../../deliverables/book/figures/headroom-map.png)

## Furniture, storage and the wall that curves away

The practical answers, including the ones that are honestly just
compromises, because a chapter that only offers custom joinery
is writing for a budget this book does not assume.

**The curve costs a wedge of dead air.** Any straight-backed
piece against a curved wall leaves a tapering gap behind it —
the round-room tax of Chapter 11, paid here in
inches. The cheap answers: pull furniture off the wall entirely
and let the gap be a cable run and a shadow line; angle the
piece so one edge touches and the gap opens somewhere
unreachable; or accept the gap and fill it with the things that
are soft. The honest answer for the pieces that matter is
built-in storage on the curve — the one place custom work pays
for itself, because it converts the dead ring into drawers.

**Low is the ring's whole vocabulary.** Beds, benches, window
seats, shoe racks, the wood stove's hearth — everything that
does not need headroom lives in the ring, and the centre of the
room stays open, which is how a round room ends up feeling
larger than its number: the walls are furnished, the middle is
air.

**The middle wants to be the table.** A round room's centre is
its tallest, brightest, most reachable point, and the instinct
to put the table there is the geometry's instinct, not a style
choice. Everything radiates from it; every path through the
room crosses it; and the light from the crown falls exactly on
it. Furnish the ring, leave the centre, put the table in the
middle — the round room's layout writes itself once the map is
accepted.

## Acoustics, and the whispering problem

The last section is the quirk nobody expects and everybody
notices in the first week: a hemisphere focuses sound.

A dome is half of a whispering gallery. Sound from one side of
the room runs up the curved wall and comes back down at the
centre — the geometry that lets a whisper cross a cathedral —
and in a house-sized dome the effect is not magical, it is
*tiring*: conversation pools at the middle of the room, a
kitchen tap at the rim is audible everywhere, and the building
has no quiet corner, because corners are where quiet lives and
the dome has none.

The fixes are the same ones theatres use, scaled down. Soft
surfaces break the focus: the quilted layers of Chapter
59 do double duty as acoustic treatment, rugs
and fabric kill the floor's reflection, and the furniture in the
ring breaks up the wall's curve the way baffles do. The one
thing not to do is nothing — an empty dome is an echo chamber,
and the echo arrives before the furniture does.

The chapter closes with the round room's honest summary: a
floor that is smaller than its number, a wall that leans, a
centre that rings — and a room that, furnished the way the map
dictates, is the calmest, most enveloping space most people
will ever sleep in. The curves are not the problem. The curves
are the room, and this chapter is the manual for living inside
them.

---

# 44. Heat, Power and the Small Systems

*Making a shell into somewhere you can be in February*

## The Small Systems

A shell keeps weather out. Somewhere you can be in February needs
the small systems: insulation in the walls, power in the dark,
heat in the cold, and a decision about the grid. This chapter is
those systems, at the scale the book's method implies — small,
deferrable, and run through the same voids the frame was built
around.

The dome's gift to its systems is the wedge frame itself: the
cavity between the members, the channel of the seams, the column
of the centre. The systems ride in those voids instead of
requiring a stud wall's worth of drilling, and the chapter's
sections follow the voids: insulation between the wedges,
services in the seam channel, heat in the round volume, and the
grid decision stated without romance. The chapter closes the
home-making half of the book; after it, the dome is a house.

## Insulating between wedges

The frame's wedges are 5.00 inches deep, and the
depth is a cavity: between the skin outside and the inside face
lies the whole thickness of the member, interrupted by nothing —
no studs, no plates, no bays that were not drawn in from the
start. Insulation goes in that cavity, and the shape does the
rest.

The options are the ladder of Chapter 59,
rung by rung: nothing first, then the quilted layers, each one
a size up, each winter warmer than the last. The book's own
preference is the quilt — layers of recycled clothing, sewn by
the network of Chapter 60 — because it is the
one insulation that matches the frame's own economy: it fits the
cavity loosely, it comes off in order, and it costs what a
sewing machine costs. Rigid board fits too, cut to the panels'
triangles and pressed into the cavity; it insulates better per
inch and gives up the removability the quilt was designed for.
Either way the principle is the cavity's: **insulate the space
between the members, never in front of them** — the members are
the frame's structure and its look, and burying them under a
blanket of foam throws away the only thing the building's walls
ever had to say.

The one rule the chapter will not soften is the moisture rule
from Chapter 42: insulation without a path
for vapour is a rot factory. The cavity must breathe toward the
seam channel, and the seam channel drains at the rim — the
system Chapters 81 and 82 engineer
in full. Insulate the dome as a living shell, not as a sealed
box, and it gets warmer every winter instead of wetter.

![It gets warmer every winter -- a frame of the seed-pitch film.](../../deliverables/book/figures/warmer-still.png)

## Running services in a frame with no studs

A conventional wall hides its wires and pipes in the stud bays.
A wedge frame has no stud bays — and it does not need them,
because the seam channel is the chase, and it runs everywhere.

The seams of Chapter 81 are the building's
service corridor: a continuous void between the panels that
follows the frame from rim to crown, wide enough for cable,
small pipe and the sensor wiring the later chapters assume.
The rules from the channel chapter bind here: water and wire
fit, a round duct does not, no pressure fitting inside a seam,
and the cable-in-chase question is the inspector's call, asked
before the skin closes over it. Power runs from the utility
column — the centre, where the core of Chapter 78
lives — outward along the seams to the points of use, and the
dome's own geometry makes the routing obvious: centre to rim,
like the spokes it is.

The discipline the chapter adds is the *deferral* discipline,
borrowed from the stem-cell chapters and stated once here: run
the services where the building will never be cut open again —
the channel, the column, the chase — and leave the end
connections surface-mounted, where a change of mind is a
screwdriver, not a renovation. Threaded inserts in the wedge
faces, set at panel time, are the cheap version of the same
promise: the services bolt on, they unbolt, and the frame never
learns their names. The building that can change its mind — Chapter
62 is the argument in full — is the building whose services were run
as if change were coming, because it always is.

## Heating a round volume

Why a dome is easier to heat than a rectangle, and the one way
it is not, stated together because both are true.

The easy half is geometry: the dome has less skin per floor
than a box — Chapter 56 computed the margin — and
heat loss is a skin phenomenon. Less skin, less loss, less
heat to buy; and the round volume mixes itself, so a single
small source warms the whole room instead of leaving the far
corner cold while the thermostat argues with the window.

The hard half is the one the book's own audit keeps repeating:
a dome is not *automatically* cheaper to heat. The skin is
cheaper to insulate well, and the shell ladder exists because
an uninsulated dome loses heat faster than an uninsulated box —
less skin, but skin with a seam every few feet, and air moving
through a seam carries heat with it. The heating claim the
book will make is narrow and honest: **the same build quality,
spent on a dome, buys more warmth per dollar than on a box** —
the margin is geometry's, the build quality is yours, and
Chapter 68 keeps the metered version of the
claim in its audit, marked as not yet metered.

The practical system follows the shape: a small stove or a
single heat source at the centre, the round volume mixing the
air, the quilted layers doing the retention. February in the
dome is a solved problem, not a promised one.

## Off grid, or not

The last decision, treated without romance, because the dome's
audience includes both the grid-connected and the gridless, and
the book refuses to pretend the choice is free.

Off grid is a battery question before it is a panel question.
The panels are cheap and getting cheaper; the storage is the
real purchase, and the arithmetic of Chapter 68
already printed the honest version of a solar budget — the
demand, the array, the bank, the price, and the days of
autonomy the bank actually buys. Off grid means the lights go
out when the bank is empty, the fridge becomes a weather
gamble, and the system's owner becomes its operator — none of
which is a romance, all of which is survivable for the person
who chose it.

On grid, the dome is simply a small building with a small
bill, and the chapter's recommendation for the first build is
the unglamorous one: connect, if the line is there, and let
the off-grid system be an upgrade bought with the money the
dome saved. The deferred-order rule of Chapter
62 applies: run the conduit for
the future array while the walls are open, buy the panels
later, and never cut the shell twice for a decision that could
have waited.

The small systems close the home chapter the way the dome
closes its shell: one layer at a time, each one bought when
the last one proved itself. Insulate first — the cheapest
warmth. Then power — the difference between a room and a
home. Then heat, then the grid question last, when the
building's own behaviour has answered it. The dome does not
need its systems all at once, and the builder who remembers
that spends the winter warm instead of broke.

---

# 45. The Money, Both Ways

*What was spent, and what was avoided*

> **This chapter corrects something.** The $50/hour figure in the project brief, which doubled a volume ratio for safety rather than computing the section.

## The Money

The accounting chapter, and it begins by saying how it counts,
because the two numbers a build produces — what was spent and what
was avoided — are different species, and most dome books breed
them into a hybrid that flatters both parents.

This chapter counts cash out, itemised, with the dashes left
where the original build did not log a line. It counts the value
of the frame three ways, with the disagreement between the ways
printed rather than averaged away. And it corrects its own
project's most-quoted hourly figure, on the page, the way this
book corrects everything. The number that does not help the
argument is here, several of them are, because the book's
reputation was staked on printing them, and a chapter that only
argued one way would un-stake it.

## What was actually spent

The table is cash out: the saw, the fuel, the chain, the
fasteners, the gaskets, the sheeting — every line the fortnight
actually paid, with the source of each number and the honest
dashes where the original build did not keep a line.

The table's headline row is the one the whole method exists to
produce: timber, zero dollars. The structural wood cost the fuel
to cut it, and the fuel is one of the small lines above. Everything
else in the table is the *conversion* — the saw, the chain, the
screws, the keys — which is the entire point of Chapters
2 and 13 made into a receipt: the
building material was free, and the receipt is for the machine
that made it building material.

The dashes are not a gap to be filled. They are lines the
original build did not log, and this book will not fill them with
plausible numbers — the declared-constants discipline from the
front matter applies to money exactly as it applies to geometry.
A cost that was not recorded is a cost that is not claimed.

![Cash out, itemised.](../../deliverables/book/figures/money-spent.png)

## Substitution value is not income

The most important paragraph in the chapter, and it gets its own
section because every honest number after it depends on the
distinction.

What the frame *avoids* is not money the builder *has*. The frame
bought at the national rate would cost 9,798
dollars; the fortnight spends 497; the
difference — 9,301 dollars — is what the build
*keeps*. (That cash figure is the film's own ledger, which
counted two trees standing; this book's build fells
3, and the third tree's stumpage — 23.23
dollars a ton at the pine film's researched rate, on a tree the
size of a car — barely moves the line. The
book says so rather than silently rounding.) It is a real number, and it is not income. It buys
groceries only if the builder was going to buy the frame anyway,
and the builder who was never going to buy the frame has not
earned nine thousand dollars; they have simply not spent it.
The same distinction runs through the whole book: the strut's
value, the hour's rate, the pine's ladder rungs — all of them
are substitution values, stated as such, and the one sentence
that converts a substitution value into a lie is the sentence
that drops the word "substitution".

The useful, honest use of the number is as a *rate on time*:
9,301 dollars kept, over 80 hours of
hands-on work, is 116 dollars an hour — the wage the
fortnight paid the builder in avoided cost. Against a
manufactured home's cheaper rate the same arithmetic gives
59; against a carpenter's median
29.12 dollars an hour, and a typical worker's
take-home 19.69, buying that frame would have cost
498 hours of ordinary pay. Those comparisons are
the chapter's real product: the fortnight's work, priced against
the hours a wage would have needed to buy the same frame. The
cash never existed. The hours did.

## The strut, valued three ways

The table is the same arithmetic run at the strut's scale, and
